# Astronomy claim verification checklist

> Generated by `npm run verify:report` from `content/`. Do not edit by hand; record results with the commands below and regenerate.

**Progress:** 0 / 447 claims fully verified · 0 verified, 470 unverified, 0 incorrect, 0 unsupported (470 claim–source checks).

## How to verify

1. Open the source URL. Each section below is one source page with every claim that cites it.
2. For each claim, find the sentence on the page that supports it. Numbers must match (rounding like "about" is fine; a different figure is not).
3. Record the result (or fill in the CSV and run `npm run verify:import-csv -- docs/verification/astronomy.csv --by "Your Name"`):
   - **verified**: the page states it. Paste the supporting sentence as the quote.
   - **unsupported**: the page does not say it. Find a better source or change the claim.
   - **incorrect**: the page (or better evidence) contradicts it. The card/question must be revised in a new revision.
4. A level can only be published when every claim it states or teaches is verified against every source it cites. `npm run validate:content` enforces this.

"Pre-check" notes were written while drafting. They point at what to look at closely. They are not verification.

"Fact-check" results come from an automated check against independent sources found by web search (not the cited page, which couldn't be opened). A **corrected** or **disputed** claim was reworded to match those sources; the old wording is shown. They are not verification either: the cited page still has to be checked.

## Alpha Centauri (Encyclopaedia Britannica)

- **Source:** `source.brit_alpha_centauri`
- **URL:** https://www.britannica.com/place/Alpha-Centauri
- **License:** reference_only · **Accessed:** 2026-09-24 · **Source record checked (URL/licence):** no

### ☐ `fact.astronomy.alpha_centauri_triple`: unverified

> **Claim:** The nearest star system, Alpha Centauri, has three stars, including Proxima Centauri.

- **Concept:** Binary stars (`concept.astronomy.binary_stars`)
- **Appears in:**
  - L65 “Star Partners: Binary Stars” · `card.astronomy.065.c2` (text): Stars in pairs / Many stars orbit a companion, including about half of Sun-like stars. The Sun is single, and that’s no scandal: most stars are small red dwarfs, and most of those have no companion either. Our nearest neighbor, Alpha Centauri, is a family of three stars, including Proxima Centauri. Even Sirius, the sky’s brightest star, has a small, faint partner: Sirius B, a white dwarf. / Alpha Centauri: 3 stars
- **Tested by:**
  - L65 `question.astronomy.065.q1`: How many stars are in the Alpha Centauri system? → **Three**
  - L68 `question.astronomy.068.q3`: From Level 65: which famous star has a white dwarf as its companion? → **Sirius, the brightest star in the night sky**
- **Fact-check (2026-09-24):** corroborated. Alpha Centauri is the nearest star system: A, B and Proxima Centauri (C), a triple system about 4.3 light-years away. Sources: https://www.nasa.gov/image-article/alpha-centauri-triple-star-system-about-4-light-years-from-earth/, https://www.space.com/18090-alpha-centauri-nearest-star-system.html

```sh
npm run verify:record -- fact.astronomy.alpha_centauri_triple source.brit_alpha_centauri --status verified --by "Your Name" --quote "…"
```

### ☐ `fact.astronomy.proxima_red_dwarf`: unverified

> **Claim:** Proxima Centauri, the nearest star to the Sun, is a red dwarf.

- **Concept:** A star’s mass sets its life (`concept.astronomy.main_sequence_mass`)
- **Appears in:**
  - L64 “The Main Sequence” · `card.astronomy.064.c4` (fact): Our nearest stellar neighbor is a red dwarf. / Proxima Centauri, the nearest star to the Sun, is a small, cool, dim red dwarf. Like other red dwarfs, it can shine for trillions of years. The Sun gets about 10 billion. Long after the Sun has retired, Proxima will still be at work.
- **Tested by:**
  - L64 `question.astronomy.064.q3`: From Level 8: Proxima Centauri is the nearest star after the Sun. What kind of star is it? → **A red dwarf that can shine for trillions of years**
- **Fact-check (2026-09-24):** corroborated. Proxima Centauri, about 4.2 light-years away, is the closest star to the Sun and a red dwarf. Sources: https://www.britannica.com/science/Proxima-Centauri, https://earthsky.org/astronomy-essentials/proxima-centauri-our-suns-nearest-neighbor/

## Andromeda Galaxy (Encyclopaedia Britannica)

- **Source:** `source.brit_andromeda`

### ☐ `fact.astronomy.andromeda_approach`: unverified

> **Claim:** The Andromeda galaxy is moving toward the Milky Way. A merger in about 4.5 billion years was long expected, but a 2025 study put the chance of the two merging within the next 10 billion years at only about 50%.

- **Concept:** Galaxy collisions (`concept.astronomy.galaxy_collisions`)
- **Appears in:**
  - L83 “When Galaxies Collide” · `card.astronomy.083.c1` (text): Andromeda is heading toward the Milky Way. / Our biggest neighbor is on the move, and it's coming our way. Should you worry? Short answer: no. Long answer: a galaxy collision is stranger, and gentler, than it sounds.
  - L83 “When Galaxies Collide” · `card.astronomy.083.c2` (text): The forecast / Galaxies can collide and merge, pulling each other into new shapes over hundreds of millions of years. Andromeda, about 2.5 million light-years away, is moving toward the Milky Way. For years the forecast was a merger in about 4.5 billion years. Then a 2025 study put the chance of one within the next 10 billion years at only about 50%. The sure thing became a coin flip. / Merger: a coin flip within 10 billion years
  - L83 “When Galaxies Collide” · `card.astronomy.083.c4` (fact): You can see our galactic neighbor with your own eyes. / From a dark site, Andromeda shows up to the naked eye as a faint smudge. That smudge is the galaxy in this story, about 2.5 million light-years away and heading our way. It's the rare forecast you can check from your backyard.
  - L98 “Integration: Futures of the Sun, Earth and Universe” · `card.astronomy.098.c2` (timeline): The long forecast: Over ~26,000 years: Earth’s axis wobbles through a full circle, so Polaris won’t stay the pole star; In ~1 billion years: A slowly brightening Sun makes Earth too hot for oceans; In ~4.5 billion years, maybe: The Milky Way and Andromeda may merge; a 2025 study put it at about a coin flip; In ~5 billion years: The Sun swells into a red giant, later becoming a white dwarf
  - L84 “The Local Group” · `card.astronomy.084.c4` (fact): Andromeda is the Local Group's other giant. / It's the other mansion, about 2.5 million light-years away, and from a dark site you can spot it with the naked eye as a faint smudge. Remember Level 83: it's also the neighbor that might, someday, move in.
- **Tested by:**
  - L83 `question.astronomy.083.q1`: Which large galaxy is moving toward the Milky Way? → **Andromeda**
  - L83 `question.astronomy.083.q3`: From Level 10: Andromeda is about 2.5 million light-years away. What did a 2025 study say about a merger? → **About a 50% chance within 10 billion years**
- **Pre-check:** Recent studies suggest the merger is less certain than once thought; confirm the page’s current wording.
- **Fact-check (2026-09-24):** disputed. Andromeda is approaching, but a 2025 Nature Astronomy study (Sawala et al., Hubble and Gaia data) found only about a 50% chance of a merger within 10 billion years, overturning the confident 4.5-billion-year forecast. Sources: https://www.smithsonianmag.com/smart-news/our-milky-way-might-not-crash-into-the-andromeda-galaxy-after-all-new-simulations-suggest-a-50-50-chance-of-merging-180986737/, https://phys.org/news/2025-06-astronomers-thought-milky-doomed-andromeda.html
- **Previous wording:** The Andromeda galaxy is moving toward the Milky Way and the two are expected to merge in about 4.5 billion years.

```sh
npm run verify:record -- fact.astronomy.andromeda_approach source.brit_andromeda --status verified --by "Your Name" --quote "…"
```

### ☐ `fact.astronomy.galaxies_merge`: unverified

> **Claim:** Galaxies can collide and merge, reshaping each other over hundreds of millions of years.

- **Concept:** Galaxy collisions (`concept.astronomy.galaxy_collisions`)
- **Appears in:**
  - L83 “When Galaxies Collide” · `card.astronomy.083.c2` (text): The forecast / Galaxies can collide and merge, pulling each other into new shapes over hundreds of millions of years. Andromeda, about 2.5 million light-years away, is moving toward the Milky Way. For years the forecast was a merger in about 4.5 billion years. Then a 2025 study put the chance of one within the next 10 billion years at only about 50%. The sure thing became a coin flip. / Merger: a coin flip within 10 billion years
  - L83 “When Galaxies Collide” · `card.astronomy.083.c3` (text): Crash without a crash / Even if it happens, don't picture a pileup. When galaxies collide, individual stars almost never hit each other, because the space between stars is so vast. The two galaxies pass through each other while their gravity reshapes both. Even a full merger leaves almost every star without a scratch.
- **Tested by:**
  - L83 `question.astronomy.083.q1`: Which large galaxy is moving toward the Milky Way? → **Andromeda**
  - L83 `question.astronomy.083.q2`: When two galaxies collide, what happens to most of their stars? → **They pass each other without hitting**
  - L83 `question.astronomy.083.q3`: From Level 10: Andromeda is about 2.5 million light-years away. What did a 2025 study say about a merger? → **About a 50% chance within 10 billion years**
- **Fact-check (2026-09-24):** corroborated. Galaxy collisions are driven by gravity and take hundreds of millions of years to unfold, reshaping the galaxies, often into ellipticals. Sources: https://www.scientificamerican.com/article/what-will-happen-if-the-milky-way-and-andromeda-galaxies-collide/
- **Weak evidence:** one-page (revisit first)

```sh
npm run verify:record -- fact.astronomy.galaxies_merge source.brit_andromeda --status verified --by "Your Name" --quote "…"
```

- **Source:** `source.brit_aristarchus`

### ☐ `fact.astronomy.aristarchus_earlier`: unverified

> **Claim:** Aristarchus of Samos had proposed a Sun-centered arrangement around 1,800 years before Copernicus.

- **Concept:** The Sun-centered model (`concept.astronomy.heliocentric_model`)
- **Appears in:**
  - L24 “Copernicus Moves the Sun” · `card.astronomy.024.c4` (fact): Copernicus wasn't the first to put the Sun in the middle. / Aristarchus of Samos proposed a Sun-centered arrangement around 1,800 years earlier. It didn't win: the Earth-centered view ruled for about 1,400 years instead. Being right, it turns out, isn't the same as being believed.
- **Pre-check:** Aristarchus lived c. 310–230 BCE; ~1,800 years before 1543. Confirm.
- **Fact-check (2026-09-24):** corroborated. Aristarchus (c. 310 to 230 BCE) proposed a heliocentric system; Copernicus published in 1543, about 1,800 years later. Sources: https://www.worldhistory.org/Aristarchus_of_Samos/, https://www2.hao.ucar.edu/education/scientists/aristarchus-of-samos-310-230-bc


## Astronomical unit (Encyclopaedia Britannica)

- **Source:** `source.brit_au`
- **URL:** https://www.britannica.com/science/astronomical-unit
- **License:** reference_only · **Accessed:** 2026-09-24 · **Source record checked (URL/licence):** no

### ☐ `fact.astronomy.au_definition`: unverified

> **Claim:** The astronomical unit (AU) is about 150 million km, roughly the average distance between Earth and the Sun. Since 2012 it has been fixed at exactly 149,597,870.7 km.

- **Concept:** The astronomical unit (`concept.astronomy.astronomical_unit`)
- **Appears in:**
  - L28 “Measuring the Solar System” · `card.astronomy.028.c4` (fact): 1 astronomical unit (AU) is about 150 million km. / That's roughly the average distance between Earth and the Sun, and it's the solar system's standard yardstick. Sunlight crosses it in about 8 minutes (Level 1's slightly late Sun). Mars sits at about 1.5 AU. The map finally has its scale bar.
- **Tested by:**
  - L28 `question.astronomy.028.q1`: What is an astronomical unit? → **The average distance between Earth and the Sun**
  - L28 `question.astronomy.028.q3`: From Level 1: sunlight takes about 8 minutes to reach Earth. What distance does that light cover? → **About 150 million km (1 AU)**
  - L50 `question.astronomy.050.q5`: Neptune is about 30 AU from the Sun. About how long does sunlight take to reach it? → **About 4 hours**
- **Fact-check (2026-09-24):** corrected. The AU was historically conceived as the mean Earth-Sun distance, but since 2012 the IAU defines it as exactly 149,597,870,700 m, a fixed unit roughly equal to that average distance. Sources: https://observatoiredeparis.psl.eu/the-new-definition-of-the-astronomical-unit.html, https://phys.org/news/2012-09-iau-votes-redefine-astronomical-constant.html, https://cneos.jpl.nasa.gov/glossary/au.html
- **Previous wording:** The astronomical unit (AU) is the average distance between Earth and the Sun, about 150 million km.

```sh
npm run verify:record -- fact.astronomy.au_definition source.brit_au --status verified --by "Your Name" --quote "…"
```

### ☐ `fact.astronomy.kepler_relative_distances`: unverified

> **Claim:** Kepler’s laws gave the relative distances of the planets, but astronomers still needed one real distance to set the scale.

- **Concept:** The astronomical unit (`concept.astronomy.astronomical_unit`)
- **Appears in:**
  - L28 “Measuring the Solar System” · `card.astronomy.028.c1` (text): Knowing the shape of the solar system isn’t the same as knowing its size. / Kepler gave astronomers an accurate map of the solar system. It just came without a scale bar.
  - L28 “Measuring the Solar System” · `card.astronomy.028.c2` (text): A map without a scale / Kepler's laws gave the relative distances of the planets: Mars orbits about 1.5 times farther from the Sun than Earth does, for example. Handy, but 1.5 times what? The laws gave proportions, not kilometers. It was a street map with the scale bar torn off. Measure one real distance, though, and it sets the scale for all the rest.
- **Tested by:**
  - L28 `question.astronomy.028.q2`: Why did astronomers still need to measure one real distance after Kepler? → **Kepler’s laws gave proportions but no kilometers**
- **Fact-check (2026-09-24):** corroborated. Kepler's third law gave planetary distances in AU but not the AU's size; one absolute distance (e.g. Mars parallax 1672, transits of Venus) set the scale. Sources: https://annex.exploratorium.edu/venus/question4.html, https://ned.ipac.caltech.edu/level5/March19/Mignard/Mignard2.html

```sh
npm run verify:record -- fact.astronomy.kepler_relative_distances source.brit_au --status verified --by "Your Name" --quote "…"
```

### ☐ `fact.astronomy.mars_1_5_au`: unverified

> **Claim:** Mars orbits about 1.5 times farther from the Sun than Earth does (about 1.5 AU).

- **Concept:** The astronomical unit (`concept.astronomy.astronomical_unit`)
- **Appears in:**
  - L28 “Measuring the Solar System” · `card.astronomy.028.c2` (text): A map without a scale / Kepler's laws gave the relative distances of the planets: Mars orbits about 1.5 times farther from the Sun than Earth does, for example. Handy, but 1.5 times what? The laws gave proportions, not kilometers. It was a street map with the scale bar torn off. Measure one real distance, though, and it sets the scale for all the rest.
  - L28 “Measuring the Solar System” · `card.astronomy.028.c4` (fact): 1 astronomical unit (AU) is about 150 million km. / That's roughly the average distance between Earth and the Sun, and it's the solar system's standard yardstick. Sunlight crosses it in about 8 minutes (Level 1's slightly late Sun). Mars sits at about 1.5 AU. The map finally has its scale bar.
- **Tested by:**
  - L28 `question.astronomy.028.q1`: What is an astronomical unit? → **The average distance between Earth and the Sun**
  - L28 `question.astronomy.028.q2`: Why did astronomers still need to measure one real distance after Kepler? → **Kepler’s laws gave proportions but no kilometers**
  - L28 `question.astronomy.028.q3`: From Level 1: sunlight takes about 8 minutes to reach Earth. What distance does that light cover? → **About 150 million km (1 AU)**
  - L50 `question.astronomy.050.q5`: Neptune is about 30 AU from the Sun. About how long does sunlight take to reach it? → **About 4 hours**
- **Pre-check:** Confirm on the Mars facts page instead if needed (~1.52 AU).
- **Fact-check (2026-09-24):** corroborated. Mars's mean distance from the Sun is about 1.52 AU (228 million km), about 1.5 times Earth's. Sources: https://lco.global/spacebook/solar-system/mars/, https://www.skyatnightmagazine.com/space-science/how-far-is-mars-from-sun

```sh
npm run verify:record -- fact.astronomy.mars_1_5_au source.brit_au --status verified --by "Your Name" --quote "…"
```

### ☐ `fact.astronomy.radar_au`: unverified

> **Claim:** Today the astronomical unit is known very precisely thanks to radar measurements and spacecraft.

- **Concept:** The astronomical unit (`concept.astronomy.astronomical_unit`)
- **Appears in:**
  - L28 “Measuring the Solar System” · `card.astronomy.028.c3` (text): Chasing Venus / The distance to pick was Earth to Sun, and the tool was Venus. In 1761 and 1769, astronomers spread out around the world to watch Venus cross the face of the Sun. Comparing their observations let them work out the Earth–Sun distance. Today radar and spacecraft pin it down very precisely, no round-the-world expedition required.
- **Fact-check (2026-09-24):** corroborated. Radar ranging to planets (e.g. Venus from 1961) and spacecraft tracking gave very precise AU values; since 2012 the IAU has fixed it at exactly 149,597,870,700 m. Sources: https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Radar_astronomy, https://observatoiredeparis.psl.eu/the-new-definition-of-the-astronomical-unit.html

```sh
npm run verify:record -- fact.astronomy.radar_au source.brit_au --status verified --by "Your Name" --quote "…"
```

### ☐ `fact.astronomy.babylon_records`: unverified

> **Claim:** Babylonian astronomers recorded the positions of the Moon and planets on clay tablets and used the records to predict eclipses.

- **Concept:** The sky as the first calendar (`concept.astronomy.ancient_sky_calendars`)
- **Appears in:**
  - L21 “The First Sky-Watchers” · `card.astronomy.021.c3` (text): Records that predicted the sky / The Babylonians took another route: they wrote everything down. Their astronomers recorded the positions of the Moon and planets on clay tablets, night after night. Those records became good enough to predict eclipses. They didn't need to know what caused an eclipse. They only needed to know when the last ones happened. Data science, in clay.
- **Tested by:**
  - L21 `question.astronomy.021.q2`: How could Babylonian astronomers predict eclipses without knowing what caused them? → **Their long records of the Moon and planets**
- **Fact-check (2026-09-24):** corroborated. Babylonian astronomical diaries on clay tablets recorded lunar and planetary positions; these records let them predict eclipses (e.g. via the saros cycle). Sources: https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Babylonian_astronomical_diaries, https://skyandtelescope.org/astronomy-news/how-did-the-ancients-predicted-eclipses-the-saros-cycle/

```sh

```

## Friedrich Wilhelm Bessel (Encyclopaedia Britannica)

- **Source:** `source.brit_bessel`
- **URL:** https://www.britannica.com/biography/Friedrich-Wilhelm-Bessel
- **License:** reference_only · **Accessed:** 2026-09-24 · **Source record checked (URL/licence):** no

### ☐ `fact.astronomy.bessel_1838`: unverified

> **Claim:** In 1838 Friedrich Bessel measured the parallax of the star 61 Cygni, the first measured distance to a star.

- **Concept:** Proof that Earth moves (`concept.astronomy.stellar_parallax_proof`)
- **Appears in:**
  - L30 “Checkpoint: How We Know” · `card.astronomy.030.c2` (timeline): How we figured it out: ~240 BCE: Eratosthenes measures Earth with the noon Sun; ~150 CE: Ptolemy puts a still Earth at the center; 1543: Copernicus hands the center to the Sun; 1610: Galileo finds moons circling Jupiter; 1687: Newton's gravity explains why planets move; 1838: Bessel measures stellar parallax
  - L30 “Checkpoint: How We Know” · `card.astronomy.030.c3` (fact): In 1838 Friedrich Bessel finally measured a star shifting as Earth orbits. / The star, 61 Cygni, moved by a tiny angle over the year. That shift, parallax, is exactly what the doubters of Level 23 had looked for and never found. It gave the first measured distance to a star, and direct proof that Earth goes around the Sun, just as Copernicus argued. The ground still feels still. It just isn't.
- **Tested by:**
  - L30 `question.astronomy.030.q4`: Why was Bessel’s 1838 measurement of stellar parallax so important? → **It showed Earth really does move around the Sun**
  - L61 `question.astronomy.061.q3`: From Level 30: Bessel measured stellar parallax in 1838. Why had no one managed it before? → **The shift is so tiny that it is very hard to detect**
  - L100 `question.astronomy.100.q4`: Why was Bessel’s 1838 measurement of a star’s parallax such a milestone? → **It directly showed Earth moves around the Sun**
- **Fact-check (2026-09-24):** corroborated. In 1838 Bessel announced a parallax of 0.314 arcsec for 61 Cygni, the first published distance to a star other than the Sun. Sources: https://aas.org/posts/news/2021/11/month-astronomical-history-november-2021, https://www.lindahall.org/about/news/scientist-of-the-day/friedrich-wilhelm-bessel/

```sh
npm run verify:record -- fact.astronomy.bessel_1838 source.brit_bessel --status verified --by "Your Name" --quote "…"
```

### ☐ `fact.astronomy.parallax_proves_orbit`: unverified

> **Claim:** Stellar parallax is a direct observation of Earth’s motion around the Sun, answering an objection that had stood for about 2,000 years.

- **Concept:** Proof that Earth moves (`concept.astronomy.stellar_parallax_proof`)
- **Appears in:**
  - L30 “Checkpoint: How We Know” · `card.astronomy.030.c3` (fact): In 1838 Friedrich Bessel finally measured a star shifting as Earth orbits. / The star, 61 Cygni, moved by a tiny angle over the year. That shift, parallax, is exactly what the doubters of Level 23 had looked for and never found. It gave the first measured distance to a star, and direct proof that Earth goes around the Sun, just as Copernicus argued. The ground still feels still. It just isn't.
  - L30 “Checkpoint: How We Know” · `card.astronomy.030.c1` (text): Chapter 3 finale. The last ancient objection falls. / Back in Level 23, the strongest case for a still Earth was simple: the stars never shift. For about 2,000 years, nobody could see them move. Then someone did.
- **Tested by:**
  - L30 `question.astronomy.030.q4`: Why was Bessel’s 1838 measurement of stellar parallax so important? → **It showed Earth really does move around the Sun**
  - L61 `question.astronomy.061.q3`: From Level 30: Bessel measured stellar parallax in 1838. Why had no one managed it before? → **The shift is so tiny that it is very hard to detect**
  - L100 `question.astronomy.100.q4`: Why was Bessel’s 1838 measurement of a star’s parallax such a milestone? → **It directly showed Earth moves around the Sun**
- **Fact-check (2026-09-24):** corroborated. Aristotle (4th century BCE) argued a moving Earth should show stellar parallax; Bessel's 1838 measurement of 61 Cygni answered this and confirmed Earth's orbital motion, roughly 2,000+ years later. Sources: https://www.britannica.com/biography/Friedrich-Wilhelm-Bessel, https://cosmology.carnegiescience.edu/timeline/1838.html, https://www.astronomy.com/science/when-did-we-realize-that-the-earth-orbits-the-sun/

```sh
npm run verify:record -- fact.astronomy.parallax_proves_orbit source.brit_bessel --status verified --by "Your Name" --quote "…"
```

### ☐ `fact.astronomy.bb_light_elements`: unverified

> **Claim:** In its first few minutes, the young universe formed the lightest elements: hydrogen, helium and small traces of lithium.

- **Concept:** The Big Bang (`concept.astronomy.big_bang`)
- **Appears in:**
  - L91 “The Big Bang” · `card.astronomy.091.c4` (text): The first ingredients / In its first few minutes, the young universe made its starter kit: hydrogen, helium and a trace of lithium. That’s it. Nearly every heavier element, from the carbon in you to the iron in a frying pan, would be made later, inside stars. And what came before the Big Bang, if anything? The theory doesn’t say. It tells the story of the cool-down, not what came before the first page.
  - L93 “The First Stars and Galaxies” · `card.astronomy.093.c2` (text): The cosmic dark ages / Picture a universe with no stars at all: just cooling gas, mostly hydrogen and helium, drifting in the dark. Astronomers call this long stretch the cosmic dark ages. Gravity was busy, though, pulling the gas into collapsing clumps. Within a few hundred million years after the Big Bang, the first stars lit up. The longest blackout in history, ended by gravity.
  - L96 “Integration: From Atoms to Galaxies” · `card.astronomy.096.c2` (timeline): From the Big Bang to you: First few minutes: Hydrogen, helium and a trace of lithium form; A few hundred million years: The first stars light up; Billions of years: Generations of stars forge heavier elements and scatter them into space; 4.6 billion years ago: The Sun and planets form from that enriched gas
  - L93 “The First Stars and Galaxies” · `card.astronomy.093.c3` (text): Giants of two elements / The first stars had a short ingredient list: only hydrogen and helium, since that’s nearly all the Big Bang had made. They’re thought to have been very massive and short-lived, the rock stars of the early universe. When they died, they made the first heavier elements and scattered them into space, seeding the gas that later stars and planets formed from. Live fast, die young, leave behind the makings of planets.
- **Tested by:**
  - L91 `question.astronomy.091.q3`: From Level 32: stars are made mostly of hydrogen and helium. Where did those elements first come from? → **The first minutes after the Big Bang**
  - L96 `question.astronomy.096.q1`: Which elements did the Big Bang itself produce? → **Hydrogen, helium and a trace of lithium**
- **Fact-check (2026-09-24):** corroborated. Big Bang nucleosynthesis in the first few minutes produced hydrogen (with deuterium), helium and trace lithium-7; about 75% hydrogen, 25% helium by mass. Sources: https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Nucleosynthesis, https://www.astro.ubc.ca/people/scott/bbn.html

### ☐ `fact.astronomy.bb_name_hoyle`: unverified

> **Claim:** The name “Big Bang” was coined in 1949 by astronomer Fred Hoyle, who favored a rival theory.

- **Concept:** The Big Bang (`concept.astronomy.big_bang`)

  - L91 “The Big Bang” · `card.astronomy.091.c3` (text): Not an explosion / Forget the fireworks. The Big Bang was not an explosion flinging stuff into empty space. It was the expansion of space itself, happening everywhere at once. No center, no blast site, no seat with the best view. The twist: the name came in 1949 from astronomer Fred Hoyle, who favored a rival theory. The most famous name in cosmology was coined by the other team.

### ☐ `fact.astronomy.bb_not_what_before`: unverified

> **Claim:** The Big Bang theory describes how the universe evolved from a hot, dense early state; it does not explain what, if anything, came before.

- **Concept:** The Big Bang (`concept.astronomy.big_bang`)
- **Appears in:**
  - L91 “The Big Bang” · `card.astronomy.091.c4` (text): The first ingredients / In its first few minutes, the young universe made its starter kit: hydrogen, helium and a trace of lithium. That’s it. Nearly every heavier element, from the carbon in you to the iron in a frying pan, would be made later, inside stars. And what came before the Big Bang, if anything? The theory doesn’t say. It tells the story of the cool-down, not what came before the first page.
  - L99 “Integration: The Big Open Questions” · `card.astronomy.099.c3` (text): Unsolved puzzles / Not every mystery is dark. Measure how fast the universe expands today in different ways and the answers disagree slightly, a puzzle called the Hubble tension. The Sun’s corona is far hotter than the surface below it, like air above a campfire being hotter than the flames, and nobody fully knows why. And what, if anything, came before the Big Bang? The theory doesn’t say.
- **Tested by:**

- **Fact-check (2026-09-24):** corroborated. The Big Bang theory describes expansion from a hot dense state and says nothing about what, if anything, preceded it. Sources: https://www.iop.org/explore-physics/big-ideas-physics/big-bang, https://www.space.com/25126-big-bang-theory.html

### ☐ `fact.astronomy.cosmic_recycling`: unverified

> **Claim:** Matter is recycled across cosmic history: gas from the Big Bang formed stars, stars forged heavier elements and returned them to space, and that enriched gas formed new stars, planets and eventually people.

- **Concept:** From atoms to galaxies (`concept.astronomy.cosmic_recycling`)
- **Appears in:**
  - L96 “Integration: From Atoms to Galaxies” · `card.astronomy.096.c1` (text): The atoms in your body have been on a 13.8-billion-year journey. / Levels 91 to 93 made the universe’s first ingredients and its first stars. Now follow those ingredients all the way to you.
  - L96 “Integration: From Atoms to Galaxies” · `card.astronomy.096.c3` (text): The great cycle / Think of the galaxy as a kitchen that never throws out leftovers. Stars fuse light elements into heavier ones, just as the Sun turns hydrogen into helium. Massive stars end in supernovae that blast those elements out, scattering them into space. New stars and planets form from that enriched gas, and the cycle repeats. Every generation of stars cooks with what the last one left behind. / Gas → stars → elements → new stars
  - L93 “The First Stars and Galaxies” · `card.astronomy.093.c3` (text): Giants of two elements / The first stars had a short ingredient list: only hydrogen and helium, since that’s nearly all the Big Bang had made. They’re thought to have been very massive and short-lived, the rock stars of the early universe. When they died, they made the first heavier elements and scattered them into space, seeding the gas that later stars and planets formed from. Live fast, die young, leave behind the makings of planets.
  - L96 “Integration: From Atoms to Galaxies” · `card.astronomy.096.c2` (timeline): From the Big Bang to you: First few minutes: Hydrogen, helium and a trace of lithium form; A few hundred million years: The first stars light up; Billions of years: Generations of stars forge heavier elements and scatter them into space; 4.6 billion years ago: The Sun and planets form from that enriched gas
- **Tested by:**
  - L96 `question.astronomy.096.q1`: Which elements did the Big Bang itself produce? → **Hydrogen, helium and a trace of lithium**
  - L96 `question.astronomy.096.q2`: Which sequence is in the right order? → **Hydrogen forms → first stars → the Sun forms**
  - L96 `question.astronomy.096.q3`: From Level 69: the iron in your blood was made inside stars. How did it reach the cloud that formed the solar system? → **Dying stars scattered it into space**
  - L100 `question.astronomy.100.q10`: Which is the best summary of where Earth’s iron came from? → **Forged in stars, scattered when they died**
- **Fact-check (2026-09-24):** corroborated. NASA: apart from Big Bang hydrogen and helium, elements were made in stars and supernovae, enriched gas clouds and were recycled into new stars, planets and life. Sources: https://science.nasa.gov/astrobiology/learning-resources/alp/are-we-really-made-of-star-stuff/, https://www.planetary.org/articles/were-made-of-starstuff-what-does-that-mean

- **Source:** `source.brit_binary_star`

### ☐ `fact.astronomy.binaries_common`: unverified

> **Claim:** Many stars orbit a companion star, including about half of Sun-like stars. The Sun is single, but that is not unusual: most stars in the Milky Way are small red dwarfs, and most of those have no companion.

- **Concept:** Binary stars (`concept.astronomy.binary_stars`)
- **Appears in:**
  - L65 “Star Partners: Binary Stars” · `card.astronomy.065.c1` (text): Our Sun is a single star. Many of its neighbors aren’t. / Being single is perfectly normal for a star. But the ones in pairs have a gift for astronomers: they let us weigh them.
  - L65 “Star Partners: Binary Stars” · `card.astronomy.065.c2` (text): Stars in pairs / Many stars orbit a companion, including about half of Sun-like stars. The Sun is single, and that’s no scandal: most stars are small red dwarfs, and most of those have no companion either. Our nearest neighbor, Alpha Centauri, is a family of three stars, including Proxima Centauri. Even Sirius, the sky’s brightest star, has a small, faint partner: Sirius B, a white dwarf. / Alpha Centauri: 3 stars
- **Tested by:**
  - L65 `question.astronomy.065.q1`: How many stars are in the Alpha Centauri system? → **Three**
  - L68 `question.astronomy.068.q3`: From Level 65: which famous star has a white dwarf as its companion? → **Sirius, the brightest star in the night sky**
- **Pre-check:** Britannica says multiple systems are very common. Confirm the wording supports “the Sun is unusual”.
- **Fact-check (2026-09-24):** corrected. About half of Sun-like stars have companions, but red dwarfs make up most stars and are mostly single, so most star systems are single; the Sun being single is not atypical. Sources: https://skyandtelescope.org/astronomy-news/surprise-most-star-systems-are-single/, https://arxiv.org/pdf/astro-ph/0601375
- **Previous wording:** Many stars orbit a companion star; the Sun, which is single, is not typical in this respect.

```sh
npm run verify:record -- fact.astronomy.binaries_common source.brit_binary_star --status verified --by "Your Name" --quote "…"
```

### ☐ `fact.astronomy.binaries_mass`: unverified

> **Claim:** Measuring how two stars orbit each other lets astronomers calculate their masses using the laws of gravity.

- **Concept:** Binary stars (`concept.astronomy.binary_stars`)
- **Appears in:**
  - L65 “Star Partners: Binary Stars” · `card.astronomy.065.c3` (text): Weighing stars / You can’t put a star on a bathroom scale. But two stars orbiting each other have to obey gravity, so their dance gives them away. Measure how they orbit each other and Newton’s law of universal gravitation, from Level 27, lets you calculate their masses. Some pairs even pass in front of each other as seen from Earth, so their combined light dims like clockwork. Those are eclipsing binaries. Not bad for something you can never touch. / Watch the orbit → get the masses
  - L65 “Star Partners: Binary Stars” · `card.astronomy.065.c1` (text): Our Sun is a single star. Many of its neighbors aren’t. / Being single is perfectly normal for a star. But the ones in pairs have a gift for astronomers: they let us weigh them.
- **Tested by:**
  - L65 `question.astronomy.065.q2`: Why are binary stars so useful to astronomers? → **Their orbits reveal the stars’ masses**
  - L65 `question.astronomy.065.q3`: From Level 27: which law lets astronomers turn a binary star’s orbit into a mass? → **Newton’s law of universal gravitation**
- **Fact-check (2026-09-24):** corroborated. Applying Newton's form of Kepler's third law to binary orbits gives stellar masses; binaries are the main direct way to measure them. Sources: https://www.atnf.csiro.au/outreach/education/senior/astrophysics/binary_mass.html, https://www.astronomynotes.com/starprop/s10.htm

```sh
npm run verify:record -- fact.astronomy.binaries_mass source.brit_binary_star --status verified --by "Your Name" --quote "…"
```

### ☐ `fact.astronomy.cmb_1965`: unverified

> **Claim:** The CMB was discovered by accident in 1965 by Arno Penzias and Robert Wilson, who at first suspected a fault in their radio antenna.

- **Concept:** The cosmic microwave background (`concept.astronomy.cmb`)
- **Appears in:**
  - L92 “The Afterglow: Cosmic Microwave Background” · `card.astronomy.092.c4` (fact): It was found by accident in 1965. / Arno Penzias and Robert Wilson thought their radio antenna had a fault. It didn’t. They had picked up the CMB. The Big Bang theory had predicted a leftover glow like this, so the discovery became key evidence for the theory. Few suspected equipment faults have ever turned out this well.
- **Tested by:**
  - L92 `question.astronomy.092.q3`: From Level 91: why did finding the CMB give such strong support to the Big Bang theory? → **The theory had predicted such a glow**
  - L100 `question.astronomy.100.q9`: Which list puts these discoveries in the order they happened? → **Jupiter’s moons → galaxy redshifts → the CMB**
- **Pre-check:** Confirm the “suspected a fault” detail; the pigeon story is well known but check wording.
- **Fact-check (2026-09-24):** corroborated. In 1965 Penzias and Wilson found unexplained noise in their Holmdel horn antenna, suspected the equipment (even pigeon droppings), cleaned it, and found the noise remained: the CMB. Sources: https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Discovery_of_cosmic_microwave_background_radiation, https://www.aps.org/funding-recognition/historic-sites/holmdel-horn-antenna

### ☐ `fact.astronomy.cmb_380k`: unverified

> **Claim:** The CMB was released about 380,000 years after the Big Bang, when the universe had cooled enough for atoms to form; before then it was too hot and dense for light to travel far.

- **Concept:** The cosmic microwave background (`concept.astronomy.cmb`)
- **Appears in:**
  - L92 “The Afterglow: Cosmic Microwave Background” · `card.astronomy.092.c2` (text): When light broke free / For roughly its first 380,000 years, the universe was too hot and dense for light to travel far, like headlights in thick fog. Then it cooled enough for atoms to form, and light could finally travel freely. That released light is still here. We call it the cosmic microwave background, or CMB: leftover light from the early universe, filling the whole sky. Every direction you look holds a baby picture of the universe. / Released ~380,000 years after the Big Bang
- **Tested by:**
  - L92 `question.astronomy.092.q1`: What is the cosmic microwave background? → **Leftover light from the early universe**
- **Fact-check (2026-09-24):** corroborated. About 380,000 years after the Big Bang the universe cooled to about 3000 K, electrons and protons combined into atoms, and photons could travel freely. Sources: https://www.astro.ucla.edu/~wright/CMB.html, https://www.cfa.harvard.edu/research/topic/cosmic-microwave-background

### ☐ `fact.astronomy.cmb_stretched`: unverified

Then it spent billions of years crossing expanding space, and as space expanded, its light was stretched to longer and longer wavelengths, all the way to microwaves. ### ☐ `fact.astronomy.copernicus_1543`: unverified

> **Claim:** In 1543 Nicolaus Copernicus published a Sun-centered (heliocentric) model in which Earth and the other planets orbit the Sun.

- **Concept:** The Sun-centered model (`concept.astronomy.heliocentric_model`)
- **Appears in:**
  - L24 “Copernicus Moves the Sun” · `card.astronomy.024.c1` (text): In 1543, one book quietly demoted Earth to “just another planet.” / Remember Ptolemy's motionless Earth at the center? Copernicus took the center seat away from us and gave it to the Sun.
  - L24 “Copernicus Moves the Sun” · `card.astronomy.024.c2` (text): The Sun in the middle / Nicolaus Copernicus published a model with the Sun at the center and Earth as just one of the planets orbiting it. In one move, Earth went from the middle of everything to the third planet out, one of the crowd. A promotion for the Sun, a demotion for us. And the planets' strange backward loops were about to get a much simpler explanation.
  - L30 “Checkpoint: How We Know” · `card.astronomy.030.c2` (timeline): How we figured it out: ~240 BCE: Eratosthenes measures Earth with the noon Sun; ~150 CE: Ptolemy puts a still Earth at the center; 1543: Copernicus hands the center to the Sun; 1610: Galileo finds moons circling Jupiter; 1687: Newton's gravity explains why planets move; 1838: Bessel measures stellar parallax
  - L30 “Checkpoint: How We Know” · `card.astronomy.030.c3` (fact): In 1838 Friedrich Bessel finally measured a star shifting as Earth orbits. / The star, 61 Cygni, moved by a tiny angle over the year. That shift, parallax, is exactly what the doubters of Level 23 had looked for and never found. It gave the first measured distance to a star, and direct proof that Earth goes around the Sun, just as Copernicus argued. The ground still feels still. It just isn't.
- **Tested by:**
  - L24 `question.astronomy.024.q1`: What did Copernicus put at the center of his model? → **The Sun**
- **Fact-check (2026-09-24):** corroborated. De revolutionibus, published in Nuremberg in 1543, set out a heliocentric model with Earth and planets orbiting the Sun. Sources: https://www.britannica.com/topic/De-revolutionibus-orbium-coelestium-libri-VI, https://www2.hao.ucar.edu/education/scientists/nicolaus-copernicus-1473-1543

### ☐ `fact.astronomy.copernicus_circles`: unverified

> **Claim:** Copernicus still assumed perfectly circular orbits, so his model needed extra small circles to match observations.

- **Concept:** The Sun-centered model (`concept.astronomy.heliocentric_model`)
- **Appears in:**
  - L24 “Copernicus Moves the Sun” · `card.astronomy.024.c3` (text): Loops without epicycles / In the Sun-centered model, retrograde motion is simply Earth overtaking the slower outer planets, like passing a slower car on the highway (Level 16). No extra circles needed to cause it. But Copernicus kept one old habit: he assumed perfectly circular orbits. Real orbits aren't perfect circles, so his model still needed some small extra circles of its own to match observations. Old habits die hard.
  - L26 “Kepler’s Ellipses” · `card.astronomy.026.c1` (text): Planets don’t move in circles. Proving it took the best data of the age. / Copernicus kept perfect circles, and they never quite fit. Fixing that took years of careful sky-watching and one very stubborn mathematician.
  - L26 “Kepler’s Ellipses” · `card.astronomy.026.c2` (text): Ellipses, not circles / Johannes Kepler worked from the precise naked-eye observations of Tycho Brahe, the best data of the age. Circles never quite matched them. Ellipses did. His first law: planets orbit the Sun in ellipses, with the Sun at one focus rather than the center. Picture a slightly squashed hoop with the Sun sitting off to one side. Not perfect, just right. / Law 1: orbits are ellipses
- **Tested by:**
  - L24 `question.astronomy.024.q2`: How did the heliocentric model explain retrograde motion? → **As Earth overtaking the slower outer planets**
  - L24 `question.astronomy.024.q3`: From Level 23: what was one weakness Copernicus’s model shared with Ptolemy’s? → **It still assumed perfectly circular orbits**
- **Fact-check (2026-09-24):** corroborated. Copernicus kept uniform circular motion, so his model still needed epicycles to match observations; ellipses came with Kepler. Sources: https://www.pas.rochester.edu/~blackman/ast104/copernican9.html, https://explainingscience.org/2017/11/28/copernicus/

```sh
npm run verify:record -- fact.astronomy.copernicus_circles source.brit_copernicus --status verified --by "Your Name" --quote "…"
```

No extra circles needed to cause it. But Copernicus kept one old habit: he assumed perfectly circular orbits. Real orbits aren't perfect circles, so his model still needed some small extra circles of its own to match observations. Old habits die hard.
  - L24 “Copernicus Moves the Sun” · `card.astronomy.024.c2` (text): The Sun in the middle / Nicolaus Copernicus published a model with the Sun at the center and Earth as just one of the planets orbiting it. In one move, Earth went from the middle of everything to the third planet out, one of the crowd. A promotion for the Sun, a demotion for us. And the planets' strange backward loops were about to get a much simpler explanation.

### ☐ `fact.astronomy.no_parallax_seen`: unverified

> **Claim:** If Earth orbited the Sun, nearby stars should shift slightly over a year; ancient observers could not detect this shift (parallax), which seemed to support a motionless Earth.

- **Concept:** Why an Earth-centered view made sense (`concept.astronomy.geocentric_reasons`)
- **Appears in:**
  - L23 “An Earth-Centered Universe” · `card.astronomy.023.c3` (text): Why it made sense / First, you can't feel Earth moving. Second, there was a sharp test. If Earth orbited the Sun, nearby stars should shift slightly over a year, the way a nearby tree shifts against distant hills as you walk past. Nobody could detect that shift, called parallax. No shift, so no motion, it seemed. And the model predicted planet positions well enough to run calendars for centuries. Wrong, but very useful.
  - L30 “Checkpoint: How We Know” · `card.astronomy.030.c1` (text): Chapter 3 finale. The last ancient objection falls. / Back in Level 23, the strongest case for a still Earth was simple: the stars never shift. For about 2,000 years, nobody could see them move. Then someone did.
  - L30 “Checkpoint: How We Know” · `card.astronomy.030.c3` (fact): In 1838 Friedrich Bessel finally measured a star shifting as Earth orbits. / The star, 61 Cygni, moved by a tiny angle over the year. That shift, parallax, is exactly what the doubters of Level 23 had looked for and never found. It gave the first measured distance to a star, and direct proof that Earth goes around the Sun, just as Copernicus argued. The ground still feels still. It just isn't.
  - L61 “Parallax: Measuring the Stars” · `card.astronomy.061.c3` (text): Closer shifts more / Try the finger trick with your arm stretched out. The hop shrinks. Stars work the same way: the closer the star, the bigger its parallax shift, so measuring the shift gives you its distance. Even for nearby stars the shift is so tiny that ancient observers never detected it. Astronomers still built a unit on it: a parsec, the distance at which the shift would be one arcsecond, is about 3.26 light-years. / 1 parsec ≈ 3.26 light-years
- **Tested by:**
  - L23 `question.astronomy.023.q2`: Why did the lack of an observed stellar shift (parallax) seem to support an Earth-centered universe? → **A moving Earth should shift nearby stars, and none moved**
  - L30 `question.astronomy.030.q4`: Why was Bessel’s 1838 measurement of stellar parallax so important? → **It showed Earth really does move around the Sun**
- **Pre-check:** Confirm the page mentions the absence of observed stellar parallax as an objection.
- **Fact-check (2026-09-24):** corroborated. Astronomy textbooks note that the Greeks could not detect stellar parallax, and so rejected a moving Earth. Copernicus answered that the stars are too far away for the shift to be seen. Sources: https://openstax.org/books/astronomy/pages/2-summary, https://courses.lumenlearning.com/towson-astronomy/chapter/ancient-astronomy/

```sh
npm run verify:record -- fact.astronomy.no_parallax_seen source.brit_copernicus --status verified --by "Your Name" --quote "…"
```

## Dark energy (Encyclopaedia Britannica)

### ☐ `fact.astronomy.de_1998`: unverified

> **Claim:** In 1998 two teams studying distant supernovae found them fainter than expected, showing that the expansion of the universe is speeding up.

- **Concept:** Dark energy (`concept.astronomy.dark_energy`)
- **Appears in:**
  - L94 “Dark Energy” · `card.astronomy.094.c1` (text): The universe’s expansion is speeding up, and no one knows why. / Everyone expected the opposite. Then, in 1998, the universe turned out to have its foot on the gas.
  - L94 “Dark Energy” · `card.astronomy.094.c2` (text): Expecting a slowdown / Toss a ball upward and gravity slows it down. Gravity pulls everything together, so astronomers expected the expansion of the universe to be slowing the same way. In 1998 two teams checked, using distant supernovae. The supernovae were fainter than a slowing universe allowed. The expansion is speeding up. The ball wasn’t slowing down. It was taking off. / Expected: slowing · Found: speeding up
  - L98 “Integration: Futures of the Sun, Earth and Universe” · `card.astronomy.098.c3` (text): The universe’s fate / Now zoom all the way out. Level 94 showed dark energy speeding up the expansion. If it keeps doing so, galaxies beyond our local neighborhood will eventually drift out of view. Picture a party where the guests slowly back out the door until only your closest neighbors are left. The Sun’s own story ends quietly: a white dwarf that no longer fuses anything, shining with leftover heat and slowly cooling. No grand finale, just a very long fade.
- **Tested by:**
  - L94 `question.astronomy.094.q1`: What did astronomers discover in 1998? → **The expansion of the universe is speeding up**
  - L98 `question.astronomy.098.q3`: From Level 94: if dark energy keeps accelerating the expansion, what happens to distant galaxies? → **They eventually drift out of view**
- **Fact-check (2026-09-24):** corroborated. In 1998 two teams studying distant Type Ia supernovae found them fainter than expected, showing the expansion is accelerating. Sources: https://www.nobelprize.org/prizes/physics/2011/popular-information/, https://www.nobelprize.org/prizes/physics/2011/summary/

Earth's shadow falls on the Moon, and its edge is always curved. Only a sphere casts a curved shadow every time, from every angle. So Eratosthenes could start from a round Earth and ask the next question: how big?
- **Tested by:**

- **Pre-check:** Aristotle’s argument is standard; confirm on the cited page or attach a better one.
- **Fact-check (2026-09-24):** corroborated. NASA's eclipse site notes that Aristotle used the curved shadow of Earth in lunar eclipses, which is round whatever the Moon's altitude, as proof that Earth is round. ### ☐ `fact.astronomy.eratosthenes_accuracy`: unverified

> **Claim:** Eratosthenes’ estimate was remarkably close to the modern value of about 40,000 km, though how close depends on the length of the ancient stadion he used.

- **Concept:** Eratosthenes measures Earth (`concept.astronomy.eratosthenes`)
- **Appears in:**
  - L22 “Eratosthenes Measures the Earth” · `card.astronomy.022.c3` (text): From angle to planet / That 7° is about one-fiftieth of a full circle. So on a round Earth, the two cities sit one-fiftieth of the way around the globe. Multiply the distance between them by 50 and you get Earth's whole circumference. His answer was remarkably close to the modern value of about 40,000 km. Not bad for a shadow and some multiplication.
- **Tested by:**
  - L22 `question.astronomy.022.q2`: The shadow angle was about one-fiftieth of a circle. What did Eratosthenes do next? → **Multiplied the distance between the cities by 50**
- **Fact-check (2026-09-24):** corroborated. Eratosthenes gave 252,000 stadia; with a stadion of 155 to 160 m the error is about -2.4% to +0.8%, but with longer stadia it is 10% or more. Modern meridional circumference is about 40,008 km. Sources: https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Earth's_circumference, https://www.aps.org/apsnews/2006/06/eratosthenes-measures-earth

### ☐ `fact.astronomy.eratosthenes_method`: unverified

> **Claim:** Around 240 BCE, Eratosthenes compared the Sun’s noon angle in two Egyptian cities, Syene and Alexandria, to estimate Earth’s circumference.

- **Concept:** Eratosthenes measures Earth (`concept.astronomy.eratosthenes`)
- **Appears in:**
  - L22 “Eratosthenes Measures the Earth” · `card.astronomy.022.c1` (text): Around 240 BCE, someone measured the whole Earth without leaving Egypt. / No satellites, no planes, no trip around the world. Just a well, a pole, a shadow and one very good idea.
  - L22 “Eratosthenes Measures the Earth” · `card.astronomy.022.c2` (text): Two cities, one Sun / Eratosthenes heard a curious thing about Syene: at noon on the summer solstice, the Sun shone straight down a deep well. At the same moment in Alexandria, a vertical pole cast a shadow about 7° from vertical. Same Sun, same moment, two different angles of the noon Sun. If the ground were flat, the two cities would see the same angle. They didn't, so the ground between them had to curve. / 7° ≈ 1/50 of a circle
  - L30 “Checkpoint: How We Know” · `card.astronomy.030.c2` (timeline): How we figured it out: ~240 BCE: Eratosthenes measures Earth with the noon Sun; ~150 CE: Ptolemy puts a still Earth at the center; 1543: Copernicus hands the center to the Sun; 1610: Galileo finds moons circling Jupiter; 1687: Newton's gravity explains why planets move; 1838: Bessel measures stellar parallax
  - L97 “Integration: How We Know What We Know” · `card.astronomy.097.c4` (text): Old tricks still work / And some clues needed no telescope at all. Around 240 BCE, Eratosthenes estimated Earth’s size by comparing the Sun’s noon angle in two Egyptian cities. A shadow, some geometry, and he had measured a planet without leaving it. From a shadow in Alexandria to a ripple in spacetime, it’s the same game: measure carefully, then reason carefully.
- **Tested by:**
  - L22 `question.astronomy.022.q1`: What did Eratosthenes compare to estimate Earth’s size? → **The angle of the noon Sun in two cities**
  - L30 `question.astronomy.030.q1`: Who first estimated Earth’s circumference from shadows? → **Eratosthenes**
- **Fact-check (2026-09-24):** corroborated. Around 240 BC Eratosthenes compared the noon Sun at Syene (overhead) and Alexandria (about 7.2 degrees) at the solstice to estimate Earth's circumference. Sources: https://www.britannica.com/biography/Eratosthenes, https://www.scienceabc.com/nature/eratosthene-calculate-circumference-earth-240-bc

### ☐ `fact.astronomy.eratosthenes_well`: unverified

> **Claim:** At Syene on the summer solstice, the noon Sun shone straight down a deep well, while in Alexandria it cast a shadow about 7° from vertical, about 1/50 of a circle; so the distance between the cities was about 1/50 of Earth’s circumference.

- **Concept:** Eratosthenes measures Earth (`concept.astronomy.eratosthenes`)
- **Appears in:**
  - L22 “Eratosthenes Measures the Earth” · `card.astronomy.022.c2` (text): Two cities, one Sun / Eratosthenes heard a curious thing about Syene: at noon on the summer solstice, the Sun shone straight down a deep well. At the same moment in Alexandria, a vertical pole cast a shadow about 7° from vertical. Same Sun, same moment, two different angles of the noon Sun. If the ground were flat, the two cities would see the same angle. They didn't, so the ground between them had to curve. / 7° ≈ 1/50 of a circle
  - L22 “Eratosthenes Measures the Earth” · `card.astronomy.022.c3` (text): From angle to planet / That 7° is about one-fiftieth of a full circle. So on a round Earth, the two cities sit one-fiftieth of the way around the globe. Multiply the distance between them by 50 and you get Earth's whole circumference. His answer was remarkably close to the modern value of about 40,000 km. Not bad for a shadow and some multiplication.
  - L22 “Eratosthenes Measures the Earth” · `card.astronomy.022.c1` (text): Around 240 BCE, someone measured the whole Earth without leaving Egypt. / No satellites, no planes, no trip around the world. Just a well, a pole, a shadow and one very good idea.
  - L97 “Integration: How We Know What We Know” · `card.astronomy.097.c4` (text): Old tricks still work / And some clues needed no telescope at all. Around 240 BCE, Eratosthenes estimated Earth’s size by comparing the Sun’s noon angle in two Egyptian cities. A shadow, some geometry, and he had measured a planet without leaving it. From a shadow in Alexandria to a ripple in spacetime, it’s the same game: measure carefully, then reason carefully.
- **Tested by:**
  - L22 `question.astronomy.022.q1`: What did Eratosthenes compare to estimate Earth’s size? → **The angle of the noon Sun in two cities**
  - L22 `question.astronomy.022.q2`: The shadow angle was about one-fiftieth of a circle. What did Eratosthenes do next? → **Multiplied the distance between the cities by 50**
  - L30 `question.astronomy.030.q1`: Who first estimated Earth’s circumference from shadows? → **Eratosthenes**
- **Pre-check:** Many retellings; confirm the page mentions the 7.2°/one-fiftieth detail (the well is traditional).
- **Fact-check (2026-09-24):** corroborated. Accounts of Eratosthenes: at Syene the solstice noon Sun lit the bottom of a well, while at Alexandria it stood 7.2 degrees from vertical, 1/50 of a circle, so the Syene-Alexandria distance was 1/50 of Earth's circumference. Sources: https://www.aps.org/apsnews/2006/06/eratosthenes-measures-earth
- **Weak evidence:** one-page (revisit first)

### ☐ `fact.astronomy.escape_11_2`: unverified

> **Claim:** Escape velocity from Earth’s surface is about 11.2 km per second (about 40,000 km/h).

- **Concept:** Escape velocity (`concept.astronomy.escape_velocity`)
- **Appears in:**
  - L37 “Getting to Orbit” · `card.astronomy.037.c4` (fact): To leave Earth for good, you need about 11.2 km per second. / That's escape velocity: about 40,000 km/h. Anything slower stays with Earth, either falling back or circling like the station. Orbiting means falling and missing. Escaping means leaving for good.
  - L73 “Black Holes” · `card.astronomy.073.c1` (text): A place where escape velocity is faster than light. / Level 37 said you need about 11.2 km per second to escape Earth. Now picture a place where even light, the fastest thing there is, isn’t fast enough.
- **Tested by:**
  - L37 `question.astronomy.037.q2`: A probe launches at more than 11.2 km per second. What can it do that the space station can’t? → **Leave Earth for good instead of orbiting**
  - L73 `question.astronomy.073.q3`: From Level 37: escape velocity is the speed needed to break free of gravity. What is true at a black hole? → **Its escape velocity exceeds the speed of light**
- **Fact-check (2026-09-24):** corroborated. Earth's surface escape velocity is about 11.2 km/s, which is about 40,320 km/h (more than 40,000 km/h). Sources: https://letstalkscience.ca/educational-resources/stem-explained/escape-velocity, https://hypertextbook.com/facts/2005/LeoTam.shtml

### ☐ `fact.astronomy.orbit_is_falling`: unverified

> **Claim:** An object in orbit is constantly falling toward Earth, but it moves sideways fast enough that Earth’s surface curves away beneath it.

- **Concept:** An orbit is falling around (`concept.astronomy.orbit_falling`)
- **Appears in:**
  - L37 “Getting to Orbit” · `card.astronomy.037.c2` (text): Falling around Earth / Throw a ball and Earth's gravity curves it down to the ground. Throw it harder and it lands farther away. Throw it fast enough sideways and the ground curves away beneath it as quickly as it falls. That's an orbit: falling forever, and always missing. The International Space Station does this at about 28,000 km/h, circling Earth about every 90 minutes. Around the world in the length of a movie. / Orbit = falling while moving sideways
  - L37 “Getting to Orbit” · `card.astronomy.037.c1` (text): Astronauts aren’t floating. They’re falling, and missing. / Gravity hasn't let go of the space station. It's falling the whole time. It just never hits the ground.
  - L37 “Getting to Orbit” · `card.astronomy.037.c4` (fact): To leave Earth for good, you need about 11.2 km per second. / That's escape velocity: about 40,000 km/h. Anything slower stays with Earth, either falling back or circling like the station. Orbiting means falling and missing. Escaping means leaving for good.
- **Tested by:**
  - L37 `question.astronomy.037.q2`: A probe launches at more than 11.2 km per second. What can it do that the space station can’t? → **Leave Earth for good instead of orbiting**
  - L37 `question.astronomy.037.q3`: From Level 27: which force makes a spacecraft fall around Earth in orbit? → **Earth’s gravity**
  - L100 `question.astronomy.100.q6`: Astronomers on the space station float. Which explanation is correct? → **They and the station are falling together**
- **Pre-check:** Newton’s cannonball idea. Confirm the source covers orbits, or attach a NASA orbit explainer.
- **Fact-check (2026-09-24):** corroborated. Newton's cannonball explanation: an orbiting body falls continuously but moves sideways fast enough that Earth's surface curves away beneath it at the same rate. Sources: https://www.eg.bucknell.edu/physics/astronomy/astr101/specials/newtscannon.html, https://www.nsta.org/blog/focus-physics-moon-falling

  - L86 “Galaxy Clusters and Gravitational Lenses” · `card.astronomy.086.c2` (text): Cities of galaxies / Galaxies aren't sprinkled evenly across space. Many gather into clusters of hundreds or thousands of galaxies, bound together by gravity. If the Local Group from Level 84 is a small town, a cluster is a city. And remember Level 85: there's roughly five times as much dark matter as ordinary matter, so much of the gravity holding a cluster together comes from dark matter.
  - L90 “Checkpoint: A Universe of Galaxies” · `card.astronomy.090.c2` (text): A ladder of scale / Climb it one rung at a time. The Sun circles the Milky Way once every 230 million years or so. The Milky Way is one of about 100 or more galaxies in the Local Group, our small town. Bigger still are clusters of hundreds or thousands of galaxies, cities held together largely by dark matter. And we're not at the top yet. / Galaxy → group → cluster
  - L90 “Checkpoint: A Universe of Galaxies” · `card.astronomy.090.c1` (text): Chapter 9 finale. Zoom out as far as galaxies go. / Start at home, with the Sun circling the Milky Way. Then keep zooming: our neighborhood, cities of galaxies, and the biggest pattern in the universe.

> **Claim:** Elliptical galaxies are smooth, rounded collections of mostly older stars with little new star formation.

Elliptical galaxies are smooth, rounded collections of mostly older stars, with little new star formation. One is a busy nursery. The other is a quiet retirement home. / Spiral · Elliptical · Irregular
  - L81 “Kinds of Galaxies” · `card.astronomy.081.c1` (text): Galaxies come in shapes, and each shape tells a story. / Look through a big telescope and galaxies don't all look alike. Spiral galaxies are the pinwheels: a flat, rotating disk with spiral arms, where young stars are still forming. ### ☐ `fact.astronomy.irregular_galaxies`: unverified

> **Claim:** Irregular galaxies have no clear shape; the Large and Small Magellanic Clouds are examples.

The Large and Small Magellanic Clouds are small galaxies orbiting the Milky Way, the irregulars from Level 81 with no clear shape. ### ☐ `fact.astronomy.galilean_moons`: unverified

> **Claim:** In 1610 Galileo discovered four large moons orbiting Jupiter, now called the Galilean moons: Io, Europa, Ganymede and Callisto.

- **Concept:** Galileo’s telescope discoveries (`concept.astronomy.galileo_observations`)
- **Appears in:**
  - L25 “Galileo’s Telescope” · `card.astronomy.025.c1` (text): In 1610, four tiny moons around Jupiter cracked the Earth-centered universe. / Copernicus had an argument. Galileo had something harder to argue with: a better way to look.
  - L25 “Galileo’s Telescope” · `card.astronomy.025.c3` (text): Moons that don't orbit Earth / In 1610 Galileo found four large moons circling Jupiter: Io, Europa, Ganymede and Callisto. That was bad news for Ptolemy. His model said everything circles Earth, and here were four worlds plainly orbiting Jupiter, not Earth. Earth wasn't the center of every orbit. It wasn't even the only planet with a moon. / Io · Europa · Ganymede · Callisto
  - L30 “Checkpoint: How We Know” · `card.astronomy.030.c2` (timeline): How we figured it out: ~240 BCE: Eratosthenes measures Earth with the noon Sun; ~150 CE: Ptolemy puts a still Earth at the center; 1543: Copernicus hands the center to the Sun; 1610: Galileo finds moons circling Jupiter; 1687: Newton's gravity explains why planets move; 1838: Bessel measures stellar parallax
  - L46 “Jupiter’s Storms and Moons” · `card.astronomy.046.c3` (text): Four worlds of their own / Jupiter has around 100 known moons, and more keep turning up. But the four Galileo spotted in 1610 are by far the largest, and each has a personality. Io is the most volcanically active world in the solar system. Ganymede is the largest moon of all, bigger than the planet Mercury: a moon that outsizes a planet. Callisto is one of the most heavily cratered worlds known. And Europa? It gets the next level to itself. / Io · Europa · Ganymede · Callisto
- **Tested by:**
  - L25 `question.astronomy.025.q1`: What did Galileo discover orbiting Jupiter in 1610? → **Four large moons**
  - L25 `question.astronomy.025.q2`: Why were Jupiter’s moons a problem for the Earth-centered model? → **They orbited Jupiter, not Earth**
  - L46 `question.astronomy.046.q3`: From Level 25: Galileo saw Jupiter’s four large moons as tiny dots. What do we know about Io today? → **It is the most volcanically active world in the solar system**
  - L100 `question.astronomy.100.q9`: Which list puts these discoveries in the order they happened? → **Jupiter’s moons → galaxy redshifts → the CMB**
- **Fact-check (2026-09-24):** corroborated. Galileo observed Jupiter's four largest moons starting 7 January 1610; they are Io, Europa, Ganymede and Callisto (names proposed by Simon Marius). Sources: https://www.ebsco.com/research-starters/history/galileo-discovers-major-moons-jupiter, https://www.planetary.org/articles/what-are-jupiters-galilean-moons

> **Claim:** Galileo did not invent the telescope, which first appeared in the Netherlands in 1608, but he built improved ones and turned them on the sky in 1609.

### ☐ `fact.astronomy.venus_full_phases`: unverified

> **Claim:** Galileo saw that Venus shows a full set of phases like the Moon, which the Ptolemaic model could not explain but a Sun-centered model could.

- **Concept:** Galileo’s telescope discoveries (`concept.astronomy.galileo_observations`)
- **Appears in:**

- **Fact-check (2026-09-24):** corroborated. Galileo observed the full cycle of Venus's phases, impossible in the Ptolemaic system but explained by a Sun-centered orbit (the Tychonic model could also account for it). Sources: https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Phases_of_Venus, https://thonyc.wordpress.com/2014/06/09/the-phases-of-venus-and-heliocentricity-a-rough-guide/

### ☐ `fact.astronomy.lensing`: unverified

> **Claim:** The gravity of a massive galaxy cluster bends and magnifies light from galaxies behind it, acting as a gravitational lens, as Einstein’s relativity predicts.

- **Concept:** Galaxy clusters and gravitational lenses (`concept.astronomy.clusters_lensing`)
- **Appears in:**
  - L86 “Galaxy Clusters and Gravitational Lenses” · `card.astronomy.086.c1` (text): Some of the universe’s best telescopes are made of gravity. / No glass, no mirrors, no launch date. Just an enormous amount of mass, bending light on its way to us.
  - L86 “Galaxy Clusters and Gravitational Lenses” · `card.astronomy.086.c3` (text): Bending light / Einstein's relativity predicts that mass bends light. Pile up enough mass and you get a lens. A massive galaxy cluster bends and magnifies the light of galaxies far behind it, like a magnifying glass held up to the deep universe. Webb's first deep-field image, released in 2022, used exactly this trick: a cluster in front magnified much more distant galaxies behind it. Free zoom, courtesy of gravity. / Mass bends light
  - L86 “Galaxy Clusters and Gravitational Lenses” · `card.astronomy.086.c4` (fact): Most of a cluster's lens is invisible. / With roughly five times as much dark matter as ordinary matter, most of the mass bending that light gives off, absorbs and reflects no light at all. So the lens is mostly something we can't see. Astronomers are peering through an invisible magnifying glass.
- **Tested by:**
  - L86 `question.astronomy.086.q1`: What is a gravitational lens? → **A massive object whose gravity bends light from behind it**
  - L86 `question.astronomy.086.q2`: How did Webb’s first deep-field image see such distant galaxies? → **A foreground galaxy cluster magnified their light**
  - L90 `question.astronomy.090.q5`: Chapter 9 recap: why can a galaxy cluster act as a gravitational lens? → **Its enormous mass bends passing light**
- **Fact-check (2026-09-24):** corroborated. Massive galaxy clusters bend, distort and magnify light from background galaxies, as predicted by general relativity. Sources: https://science.nasa.gov/mission/hubble/science/science-behind-the-discoveries/hubble-gravitational-lenses/, https://www.cfa.harvard.edu/research/topic/gravitational-lensing

### ☐ `fact.astronomy.hr_1910`: unverified

> **Claim:** The diagram is named after Ejnar Hertzsprung and Henry Norris Russell, who developed it independently in the early 1900s.

- **Concept:** The Hertzsprung–Russell diagram (`concept.astronomy.hr_diagram`)
- **Appears in:**
  - L63 “The H–R Diagram” · `card.astronomy.063.c2` (text): Luminosity versus temperature / The Hertzsprung–Russell diagram, or H–R diagram, plots stars by luminosity and surface temperature. More luminous stars sit higher up. Hotter stars sit farther left, which feels like a map with west on the right. Remember Level 9: blue stars are the hottest, so they land on the left and red ones on the right. Ejnar Hertzsprung and Henry Norris Russell developed the chart independently in the early 1900s. Two people, one great idea. / Up = more luminous · Left = hotter
- **Tested by:**
  - L63 `question.astronomy.063.q1`: What two properties does the H–R diagram plot? → **Luminosity and surface temperature**
  - L63 `question.astronomy.063.q3`: From Level 9: a blue-white star is hotter than a red one. On the H–R diagram, which side is it on? → **The left side, with hotter stars**
- **Fact-check (2026-09-24):** corroborated. Hertzsprung (1911) and Russell (1913) created the diagram independently, neither knowing of the other's work, consistent with 'early 1900s'. Sources: https://www.atnf.csiro.au/resources/education/senior-astrophysics/stellarevolution/hrintro/, https://www.britannica.com/science/Hertzsprung-Russell-diagram

Remember Level 9: blue stars are the hottest, so they land on the left and red ones on the right. Ejnar Hertzsprung and Henry Norris Russell developed the chart independently in the early 1900s. ### ☐ `fact.astronomy.hr_giants_dwarfs`: unverified

> **Claim:** Red giants appear in the upper right of the diagram (cool but very luminous) and white dwarfs in the lower left (hot but dim).

- **Concept:** The Hertzsprung–Russell diagram (`concept.astronomy.hr_diagram`)
- **Appears in:**
  - L63 “The H–R Diagram” · `card.astronomy.063.c3` (text): Where the stars fall / Most stars fall along a diagonal band called the main sequence. The Sun sits near its middle, which sounds average, but it isn’t: the most common stars are dim red dwarfs, so the Sun is hotter and brighter than most. Off the band live the oddballs. Red giants sit in the upper right: cool but very luminous. White dwarfs sit in the lower left: hot but dim. The Sun will visit both corners one day, red giant first, white dwarf last.
  - L63 “The H–R Diagram” · `card.astronomy.063.c2` (text): Luminosity versus temperature / The Hertzsprung–Russell diagram, or H–R diagram, plots stars by luminosity and surface temperature. More luminous stars sit higher up. Hotter stars sit farther left, which feels like a map with west on the right. Remember Level 9: blue stars are the hottest, so they land on the left and red ones on the right. Ejnar Hertzsprung and Henry Norris Russell developed the chart independently in the early 1900s. Two people, one great idea. / Up = more luminous · Left = hotter
  - L67 “Red Giants and Planetary Nebulae” · `card.astronomy.067.c1` (text): Someday the Sun will swallow Mercury and Venus. / Level 63 said the Sun will one day move to the red giant corner of the H–R diagram. Here’s what that trip looks like, and the glowing mess it leaves behind.
  - L67 “Red Giants and Planetary Nebulae” · `card.astronomy.067.c2` (text): Swelling up / When a Sun-like star’s core runs low on hydrogen, the core contracts and heats up while the outer layers swell enormously. The star becomes a red giant: cool but very luminous, so it moves up and to the right on the H–R diagram. In about 5 billion years the Sun should grow large enough to engulf Mercury and Venus, and possibly Earth. Inner-planet real estate: not a long-term investment. / Core shrinks · outer layers swell
  - L68 “White Dwarfs” · `card.astronomy.068.c1` (text): A teaspoon of this star would weigh several tons. / When the planetary nebula from Level 67 drifts away, something is left behind. It’s small, it’s hot, and it’s astonishingly heavy for its size.
  - L68 “White Dwarfs” · `card.astronomy.068.c3` (text): Slowly fading / A white dwarf no longer fuses anything. It shines only with leftover heat, like an oven after it’s switched off, and slowly cools over billions of years. That’s why it sits in the lower left of the H–R diagram: hot, but dim. A famous example is Sirius B, the faint partner of Sirius, the brightest star in the night sky. It’s also the most common ending: most stars in the Milky Way, the Sun included, will finish as white dwarfs.
- **Tested by:**
  - L63 `question.astronomy.063.q1`: What two properties does the H–R diagram plot? → **Luminosity and surface temperature**
  - L63 `question.astronomy.063.q2`: Where on the H–R diagram are white dwarfs found? → **Lower left: hot but dim**
  - L63 `question.astronomy.063.q3`: From Level 9: a blue-white star is hotter than a red one. On the H–R diagram, which side is it on? → **The left side, with hotter stars**
  - L67 `question.astronomy.067.q3`: From Level 63: where on the H–R diagram would the Sun move as it becomes a red giant? → **Up and to the right: cooler but more luminous**
- **Fact-check (2026-09-24):** corroborated. On the H-R diagram, cool but luminous giants lie at upper right and hot, dim white dwarfs at lower left. Sources: https://www.britannica.com/science/Hertzsprung-Russell-diagram, https://astro.unl.edu/naap/hr/hr_background3.html

  - L63 “The H–R Diagram” · `card.astronomy.063.c3` (text): Where the stars fall / Most stars fall along a diagonal band called the main sequence.
