## Hubble’s law (Encyclopaedia Britannica)

- **Source:** `source.brit_hubble_law`
- **URL:** https://www.britannica.com/science/Hubbles-law
- **License:** reference_only · **Accessed:** 2026-09-24 · **Source record checked (URL/licence):** no

### ☐ `fact.astronomy.expansion_not_center`: unverified

> **Claim:** The expansion has no center: galaxies are moving apart because space itself is expanding, so every galaxy sees the others receding.

- **Concept:** Hubble’s expanding universe (`concept.astronomy.hubble_expansion`)
- **Appears in:**
  - L88 “Hubble’s Expanding Universe” · `card.astronomy.088.c4` (text): No center / So is everything fleeing from us? No. The expansion has no center. Space itself is stretching, so every galaxy sees the others moving away. Picture raisins in rising dough: from any raisin, all the others drift apart. Sorry, Milky Way. You're just a raisin.
  - L91 “The Big Bang” · `card.astronomy.091.c3` (text): Not an explosion / Forget the fireworks. The Big Bang was not an explosion flinging stuff into empty space. It was the expansion of space itself, happening everywhere at once. No center, no blast site, no seat with the best view. The twist: the name came in 1949 from astronomer Fred Hoyle, who favored a rival theory. The most famous name in cosmology was coined by the other team.
- **Tested by:**
  - L88 `question.astronomy.088.q2`: Observers in every galaxy would see the other galaxies moving away. What does this tell us? → **Space itself is expanding, with no center**
- **Fact-check (2026-09-24):** corroborated. Uniform Hubble expansion has no center; it is space itself expanding, and every observer sees distant galaxies receding. Sources: https://math.ucr.edu/home/baez/physics/Relativity/GR/centre.html, https://courses.ems.psu.edu/astro801/content/l10_p4.html

```sh
npm run verify:record -- fact.astronomy.expansion_not_center source.brit_hubble_law --status verified --by "Your Name" --quote "…"
```

### ☐ `fact.astronomy.hubble_law`: unverified

> **Claim:** In 1929 Hubble reported that galaxies are moving away from us at speeds that increase with their distance, evidence that the universe is expanding.

- **Concept:** Hubble’s expanding universe (`concept.astronomy.hubble_expansion`)
- **Appears in:**
  - L88 “Hubble’s Expanding Universe” · `card.astronomy.088.c3` (text): Everything is moving apart / In 1929 Hubble reported that galaxies are moving away from us, and the farther away a galaxy is, the faster it recedes. This is Hubble's law, and it means the universe is expanding. Credit where due: Georges Lemaître had proposed the same idea a few years earlier. The universe wasn't just bigger than anyone thought. It was getting bigger. / Farther → faster
  - L90 “Checkpoint: A Universe of Galaxies” · `card.astronomy.090.c3` (text): The cosmic web / At the top of the ladder, galaxies and clusters form a vast web of long filaments and sheets wrapped around huge, nearly empty voids. Picture the foam in a bubble bath: galaxies in the soapy film, emptiness in the bubbles. And the whole web is stretching as the universe expands, which is why distant galaxies look redshifted. The largest pattern in the universe is still stretching. / Filaments · sheets · voids
  - L91 “The Big Bang” · `card.astronomy.091.c2` (text): Rewind the expansion / If galaxies are drifting apart today, they were closer together yesterday. Keep rewinding and everything crowds closer, hotter and denser. The Big Bang theory says the universe began in an extremely hot, dense state about 13.8 billion years ago and has been expanding and cooling ever since. So the universe has a birthday, and everything since has been one very long cool-down. / Hot and dense → expanding and cooling
  - L97 “Integration: How We Know What We Know” · `card.astronomy.097.c3` (text): New windows / Some discoveries needed brand-new kinds of clues. Stars at galaxies’ edges orbit too fast for the visible matter to hold them, which gave away dark matter, something no one has ever seen. Redshift in galaxies’ light revealed the expanding universe. And in 2015 LIGO first detected gravitational waves from merging black holes: not light at all, but ripples in spacetime. Astronomy learned to feel the universe shake.
  - L88 “Hubble’s Expanding Universe” · `card.astronomy.088.c1` (text): In 1924, the known universe suddenly got much bigger. / One astronomer, one fuzzy patch of sky, and our galaxy went from possibly everything to one of many. Five years later, he found something even stranger.
  - L89 “Redshift” · `card.astronomy.089.c2` (text): Stretched light / Remember Level 88: space is expanding. As light crosses expanding space, its wavelength gets stretched, like a wave drawn on a rubber band as you pull it. Red has the longest wavelength of visible light, so stretched light shifts toward the red end of the spectrum. Astronomers call this redshift: light stretched to longer, redder wavelengths. / Stretched light → redder
  - L91 “The Big Bang” · `card.astronomy.091.c1` (text): Everything you’ve met in this course began hot and dense. / Level 88 showed galaxies moving apart. So run the movie backwards. Where does it lead?
- **Tested by:**
  - L88 `question.astronomy.088.q1`: What did Hubble report in 1929? → **Galaxies recede faster the farther away they are**
  - L90 `question.astronomy.090.q4`: A faraway galaxy shows a very large redshift. What does that tell you? → **It is very far away and receding fast**
  - L100 `question.astronomy.100.q9`: Which list puts these discoveries in the order they happened? → **Jupiter’s moons → galaxy redshifts → the CMB**
- **Fact-check (2026-09-24):** corroborated. In 1929 Hubble published the velocity-distance relation: more distant galaxies recede faster, interpreted as evidence of an expanding universe. Sources: https://www.pnas.org/doi/10.1073/pnas.1424299112, https://www.atnf.csiro.au/resources/education/senior-cosmicengine/cosmology/hubble/

### ☐ `fact.astronomy.hubble_tension`: unverified

> **Claim:** Different ways of measuring how fast the universe is expanding today give slightly different answers, a puzzle known as the Hubble tension.

- **Concept:** The big open questions (`concept.astronomy.open_questions`)
- **Appears in:**
  - L99 “Integration: The Big Open Questions” · `card.astronomy.099.c3` (text): Unsolved puzzles / Not every mystery is dark. Measure how fast the universe expands today in different ways and the answers disagree slightly, a puzzle called the Hubble tension. The Sun’s corona is far hotter than the surface below it, like air above a campfire being hotter than the flames, and nobody fully knows why. And what, if anything, came before the Big Bang? The theory doesn’t say.
- **Tested by:**
  - L99 `question.astronomy.099.q1`: What is the Hubble tension? → **Expansion measurements that disagree slightly**
- **Pre-check:** Confirm either page covers the tension; otherwise cite a specific NASA Hubble/Webb release.
- **Fact-check (2026-09-24):** corroborated. NASA's Webb/Hubble releases describe the Hubble tension: local distance-ladder measurements (~73 km/s/Mpc) disagree with CMB-based predictions (~67 km/s/Mpc). Sources: https://science.nasa.gov/missions/hubble/nasas-webb-hubble-telescopes-affirm-universes-expansion-rate-puzzle-persists, https://science.nasa.gov/blogs/webb/2023/09/12/webb-confirms-accuracy-of-universes-expansion-rate-measured-by-hubble-deepens-mystery-of-hubble-constant-tension/

### ☐ `fact.astronomy.jansky_1932`: unverified

- **Appears in:**

Suddenly the sky could be studied around the clock.

  - L72 `question.astronomy.072.q3`: From Level 35: what kind of telescope found the first pulsar? → **A radio telescope**
- **Fact-check (2026-09-24):** corroborated. Jansky detected radio emission from the Milky Way (Sagittarius) in 1932 and announced it in April 1933; he is regarded as a founder of radio astronomy. Sources: https://public.nrao.edu/news/milky-ways-black-hole-was-birth-cry-of-radio-astronomy/, https://aas.org/posts/news/2018/07/month-astronomical-history-1

## Kepler’s laws of planetary motion (Encyclopaedia Britannica)

- **Source:** `source.brit_kepler_laws`
- **URL:** https://www.britannica.com/science/Keplers-laws-of-planetary-motion
- **License:** reference_only · **Accessed:** 2026-09-24 · **Source record checked (URL/licence):** no

### ☐ `fact.astronomy.kepler_ellipse`: unverified

> **Claim:** Kepler’s first law: planets orbit the Sun in ellipses, with the Sun at one focus.

- **Concept:** Kepler’s laws (`concept.astronomy.kepler_laws`)
- **Appears in:**
  - L26 “Kepler’s Ellipses” · `card.astronomy.026.c2` (text): Ellipses, not circles / Johannes Kepler worked from the precise naked-eye observations of Tycho Brahe, the best data of the age. Circles never quite matched them. Ellipses did. His first law: planets orbit the Sun in ellipses, with the Sun at one focus rather than the center. Picture a slightly squashed hoop with the Sun sitting off to one side. Not perfect, just right. / Law 1: orbits are ellipses
  - L24 “Copernicus Moves the Sun” · `card.astronomy.024.c3` (text): Loops without epicycles / In the Sun-centered model, retrograde motion is simply Earth overtaking the slower outer planets, like passing a slower car on the highway (Level 16). No extra circles needed to cause it. But Copernicus kept one old habit: he assumed perfectly circular orbits. Real orbits aren't perfect circles, so his model still needed some small extra circles of its own to match observations. Old habits die hard.
- **Tested by:**
  - L26 `question.astronomy.026.q1`: What shape did Kepler find planetary orbits to be? → **Ellipses**
  - L30 `question.astronomy.030.q2`: What did Kepler discover about the shape of orbits? → **They are ellipses**
  - L30 `question.astronomy.030.q5`: Chapter 3 recap: what put the final piece in place for why the planets move as they do? → **Newton’s gravity explaining Kepler’s laws**
- **Fact-check (2026-09-24):** corroborated. Kepler's first law: planets move in elliptical orbits with the Sun at one focus. Sources: https://science.nasa.gov/solar-system/orbits-and-keplers-laws/, https://www.britannica.com/science/Keplers-first-law-of-planetary-motion

### ☐ `fact.astronomy.kepler_equal_areas`: unverified

> **Claim:** Kepler’s second law: a line from the Sun to a planet sweeps out equal areas in equal times, so a planet moves fastest when it is closest to the Sun.

- **Concept:** Kepler’s laws (`concept.astronomy.kepler_laws`)
- **Appears in:**
  - L26 “Kepler’s Ellipses” · `card.astronomy.026.c3` (text): Faster when closer / His second law says a line from the Sun to a planet sweeps out equal areas in equal times. In plain terms: a planet moves fastest when it's closest to the Sun and slowest when farthest, like a skateboarder who's quickest at the bottom of the ramp. Earth is closest in early January (Level 5), so that's when it moves fastest. His third law adds that the farther out a planet orbits, the longer its year. / Law 2: faster near the Sun · Law 3: farther = longer year
- **Tested by:**
  - L26 `question.astronomy.026.q2`: According to Kepler’s second law, when does a planet move fastest? → **When it is closest to the Sun**
  - L26 `question.astronomy.026.q3`: From Level 5: Earth is closest to the Sun in early January. By Kepler’s second law, what follows? → **Earth moves fastest along its orbit in January**
  - L75 `question.astronomy.075.q3`: From Level 26: which laws let astronomers turn those stars’ orbits into a mass for Sagittarius A*? → **The laws of orbits and gravity from Kepler and Newton**
  - L85 `question.astronomy.085.q3`: From Level 26: planets far from the Sun orbit more slowly. Why was it surprising that outer stars in galaxies don’t slow down? → **It means far more mass is present than we can see**
- **Fact-check (2026-09-24):** corroborated. Second law: the Sun-planet line sweeps equal areas in equal times, so the planet is fastest at perihelion (closest approach). Sources: https://science.nasa.gov/solar-system/orbits-and-keplers-laws/, https://www.britannica.com/science/Keplers-second-law-of-planetary-motion

```sh
npm run verify:record -- fact.astronomy.kepler_equal_areas source.brit_kepler_laws --status verified --by "Your Name" --quote "…"
```

### ☐ `fact.astronomy.kepler_third`: unverified

> **Claim:** Kepler’s third law: the farther a planet is from the Sun, the longer it takes to complete an orbit.

- **Concept:** Kepler’s laws (`concept.astronomy.kepler_laws`)
- **Appears in:**
  - L26 “Kepler’s Ellipses” · `card.astronomy.026.c3` (text): Faster when closer / His second law says a line from the Sun to a planet sweeps out equal areas in equal times. In plain terms: a planet moves fastest when it's closest to the Sun and slowest when farthest, like a skateboarder who's quickest at the bottom of the ramp. Earth is closest in early January (Level 5), so that's when it moves fastest. His third law adds that the farther out a planet orbits, the longer its year. / Law 2: faster near the Sun · Law 3: farther = longer year
  - L85 “Dark Matter” · `card.astronomy.085.c2` (text): Galaxies spin too fast / Remember Kepler from Level 26: the farther a planet is from the Sun, the longer its orbit takes. You'd expect a galaxy to work the same way. But in the 1970s Vera Rubin measured how fast stars orbit in spiral galaxies, and stars far from the center were moving far too fast for the visible matter to hold them. They should have flown off, like mud from a spinning bike wheel. Something unseen had to be adding gravity. / Too fast → hidden mass
- **Tested by:**
  - L26 `question.astronomy.026.q2`: According to Kepler’s second law, when does a planet move fastest? → **When it is closest to the Sun**
  - L26 `question.astronomy.026.q3`: From Level 5: Earth is closest to the Sun in early January. By Kepler’s second law, what follows? → **Earth moves fastest along its orbit in January**
  - L75 `question.astronomy.075.q3`: From Level 26: which laws let astronomers turn those stars’ orbits into a mass for Sagittarius A*? → **The laws of orbits and gravity from Kepler and Newton**
  - L85 `question.astronomy.085.q3`: From Level 26: planets far from the Sun orbit more slowly. Why was it surprising that outer stars in galaxies don’t slow down? → **It means far more mass is present than we can see**
- **Pre-check:** The law is precise (period² ∝ distance³); the card states it qualitatively.
- **Fact-check (2026-09-24):** corroborated. Kepler's third law (P squared proportional to a cubed) implies the farther a planet is from the Sun, the longer its orbital period; the card's qualitative wording is accurate. Sources: https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Kepler's_laws_of_planetary_motion, https://www.space.com/keplers-third-law

```sh
npm run verify:record -- fact.astronomy.kepler_third source.brit_kepler_laws --status verified --by "Your Name" --quote "…"
```

### ☐ `fact.astronomy.newton_explains_kepler`: unverified

> **Claim:** Newton’s law of gravity explains why Kepler’s laws work.

- **Concept:** Newton’s law of gravity (`concept.astronomy.newton_gravity`)
- **Appears in:**
  - L27 “Newton’s Gravity” · `card.astronomy.027.c3` (text): Heaven and Earth, united / Here's the big leap. The gravity that makes an apple fall on Earth is the same force that keeps the Moon in orbit and the planets circling the Sun. One force, from your kitchen to the edge of the solar system. It also explained why Kepler's laws work, turning a description into an explanation. It even reaches the beach: the Moon's gravity pulling on the oceans is what raises the tides (Level 17).
  - L27 “Newton’s Gravity” · `card.astronomy.027.c1` (text): The force that drops an apple also holds the Moon in the sky. / Kepler described how the planets move. Newton explained why, and the answer was the same thing that pulls your toast to the kitchen floor.
  - L30 “Checkpoint: How We Know” · `card.astronomy.030.c2` (timeline): How we figured it out: ~240 BCE: Eratosthenes measures Earth with the noon Sun; ~150 CE: Ptolemy puts a still Earth at the center; 1543: Copernicus hands the center to the Sun; 1610: Galileo finds moons circling Jupiter; 1687: Newton's gravity explains why planets move; 1838: Bessel measures stellar parallax
  - L75 “The Black Hole at Our Center” · `card.astronomy.075.c2` (text): Following the stars / About 27,000 light-years away, at the center of the Milky Way, stars race around something nobody can see. Picture a racetrack where you can see the cars but not what they’re circling. Astronomers tracked those orbits, and the laws of orbits and gravity from Kepler and Newton turned them into a mass: about 4 million times the Sun’s. One star, S2, laps it about every 16 years. Its name is Sagittarius A*, and it’s a supermassive black hole. / ~4 million Suns
- **Tested by:**
  - L27 `question.astronomy.027.q1`: What did Newton show about gravity? → **One force makes apples fall and holds the Moon in orbit**
  - L27 `question.astronomy.027.q3`: From Level 17: what connects Newton’s law to the ocean tides? → **The Moon’s gravity pulls on Earth’s oceans**
  - L30 `question.astronomy.030.q5`: Chapter 3 recap: what put the final piece in place for why the planets move as they do? → **Newton’s gravity explaining Kepler’s laws**
  - L37 `question.astronomy.037.q3`: From Level 27: which force makes a spacecraft fall around Earth in orbit? → **Earth’s gravity**
- **Fact-check (2026-09-24):** corroborated. Newton showed that Kepler's laws follow mathematically from his inverse-square law of gravitation and laws of motion. Sources: http://galileo.phys.virginia.edu/classes/152.mf1i.spring02/KeplersLaws.htm, https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Kepler's_laws_of_planetary_motion

### ☐ `fact.astronomy.local_group_members`: unverified

> **Claim:** The Milky Way belongs to the Local Group, a gathering of about 100 or more galaxies, mostly small dwarfs, dominated by the Milky Way and Andromeda.

- **Concept:** The Local Group (`concept.astronomy.local_group`)
- **Appears in:**
  - L84 “The Local Group” · `card.astronomy.084.c1` (text): Our galaxy lives in a neighborhood. / The Milky Way isn't alone out there. It has a big neighbor, a couple of small hangers-on, and a crowd of little ones. Time to meet the neighborhood.
  - L84 “The Local Group” · `card.astronomy.084.c2` (text): Two mansions, lots of cottages / The Milky Way belongs to the Local Group, about 100 or more galaxies spread across about 10 million light-years. Most of them are small dwarf galaxies. Two big spirals run the place: the Milky Way and Andromeda. Think of a small town with two mansions and a lot of cottages. We live in one of the mansions. / ~100+ galaxies · ~10 million ly
  - L90 “Checkpoint: A Universe of Galaxies” · `card.astronomy.090.c2` (text): A ladder of scale / Climb it one rung at a time. The Sun circles the Milky Way once every 230 million years or so. The Milky Way is one of about 100 or more galaxies in the Local Group, our small town. Bigger still are clusters of hundreds or thousands of galaxies, cities held together largely by dark matter. And we're not at the top yet. / Galaxy → group → cluster
  - L84 “The Local Group” · `card.astronomy.084.c4` (fact): Andromeda is the Local Group's other giant. / It's the other mansion, about 2.5 million light-years away, and from a dark site you can spot it with the naked eye as a faint smudge. Remember Level 83: it's also the neighbor that might, someday, move in.
  - L86 “Galaxy Clusters and Gravitational Lenses” · `card.astronomy.086.c2` (text): Cities of galaxies / Galaxies aren't sprinkled evenly across space. Many gather into clusters of hundreds or thousands of galaxies, bound together by gravity. If the Local Group from Level 84 is a small town, a cluster is a city. And remember Level 85: there's roughly five times as much dark matter as ordinary matter, so much of the gravity holding a cluster together comes from dark matter.
  - L90 “Checkpoint: A Universe of Galaxies” · `card.astronomy.090.c1` (text): Chapter 9 finale. Zoom out as far as galaxies go. / Start at home, with the Sun circling the Milky Way. Then keep zooming: our neighborhood, cities of galaxies, and the biggest pattern in the universe.
- **Tested by:**
  - L84 `question.astronomy.084.q1`: Which two galaxies dominate the Local Group? → **The Milky Way and Andromeda**
  - L90 `question.astronomy.090.q2`: Which list runs from smallest to largest? → **Milky Way, Local Group, galaxy cluster, cosmic web**
- **Fact-check (2026-09-24):** corrected. Current censuses count roughly 100 or more Local Group galaxies (astronomy.com cites about 125), mostly dwarfs; 'more than 50' is an outdated undercount. Milky Way and Andromeda dominate. Sources: https://www.astronomy.com/observing/michaels-miscellany-meet-the-local-group/, https://earthsky.org/clusters-nebulae-galaxies/what-is-the-local-group/
- **Previous wording:** The Milky Way belongs to the Local Group, a gathering of more than 50 galaxies dominated by the Milky Way and Andromeda.

## Luminosity (Encyclopaedia Britannica)

- **Source:** `source.brit_luminosity`
- **URL:** https://www.britannica.com/science/luminosity
- **License:** reference_only · **Accessed:** 2026-09-24 · **Source record checked (URL/licence):** no

### ☐ `fact.astronomy.apparent_vs_true`: unverified

> **Claim:** How bright a star looks from Earth depends both on how much light it actually gives off (its luminosity) and on how far away it is.

- **Concept:** Brightness versus luminosity (`concept.astronomy.luminosity`)
- **Appears in:**
  - L62 “Bright or Close? Luminosity” · `card.astronomy.062.c2` (text): Two kinds of bright / A porch light next door can outshine a stadium floodlight across town. Stars play the same trick on you. How bright a star looks from Earth depends on two things: how much light it actually gives off, called its luminosity, and how far away it is. So a star that looks bright might be truly powerful, or just close. / Looks bright = luminosity + distance
  - L62 “Bright or Close? Luminosity” · `card.astronomy.062.c1` (text): The brightest star in the sky has a secret: it lives nearby. / Sirius outshines every other star in the night sky. But a star can look bright for two very different reasons, and Sirius has help from the easier one.
  - L62 “Bright or Close? Luminosity” · `card.astronomy.062.c4` (fact): Sirius, the brightest night-time star, is about 8.6 light-years away. / Sirius really is more luminous than the Sun, and it’s also relatively close. Power plus proximity: that’s the winning combination. A star with more power but far more distance can easily lose the contest.
- **Tested by:**
  - L62 `question.astronomy.062.q1`: What is a star’s luminosity? → **How much light it actually gives off**
- **Fact-check (2026-09-24):** corroborated. Apparent brightness is proportional to luminosity and inversely proportional to the square of distance. Sources: https://www.astronomy.ohio-state.edu/pogge.1/Ast162/Unit1/bright.html, https://courses.ems.psu.edu/astro801/content/l4_p4.html

### ☐ `fact.astronomy.brightness_inverse_square`: unverified

> **Claim:** A star’s apparent brightness falls with the square of its distance: twice as far away, it looks a quarter as bright.

- **Concept:** Brightness versus luminosity (`concept.astronomy.luminosity`)
- **Appears in:**
  - L62 “Bright or Close? Luminosity” · `card.astronomy.062.c3` (text): Fading with distance / Distance dims starlight fast. A star’s apparent brightness falls with the square of its distance: move a star twice as far away and it looks only a quarter as bright. Ten times as far? A hundredth. If that rule sounds familiar, it should: gravity in Level 27 weakens with the square of distance too. The universe likes this pattern. / Twice as far → ¼ as bright
  - L62 “Bright or Close? Luminosity” · `card.astronomy.062.c4` (fact): Sirius, the brightest night-time star, is about 8.6 light-years away. / Sirius really is more luminous than the Sun, and it’s also relatively close. Power plus proximity: that’s the winning combination. A star with more power but far more distance can easily lose the contest.
- **Tested by:**
  - L62 `question.astronomy.062.q2`: A star is moved to twice its distance. How bright does it look? → **A quarter as bright**
  - L62 `question.astronomy.062.q3`: From Level 27: which other effect weakens with the square of distance, just like starlight? → **Gravity**
- **Fact-check (2026-09-24):** corroborated. Apparent brightness is inversely proportional to distance squared; a source twice as far appears one quarter as bright. Sources: https://www.astronomynotes.com/starprop/s3.htm, https://courses.ems.psu.edu/astro801/content/l4_p4.html

## Main-sequence star (Encyclopaedia Britannica)

- **Source:** `source.brit_main_sequence`

- **License:** reference_only · **Accessed:** 2026-09-24 · **Source record checked (URL/licence):** no

### ☐ `fact.astronomy.main_sequence_fusion`: unverified

> **Claim:** A main-sequence star is one fusing hydrogen into helium in its core; stars spend most of their lives this way.

- **Concept:** A star’s mass sets its life (`concept.astronomy.main_sequence_mass`)
- **Appears in:**
  - L64 “The Main Sequence” · `card.astronomy.064.c2` (text): Life on the main sequence / Remember the main sequence band from Level 63? A star on it is fusing hydrogen into helium in its core, the same job the Sun is doing right now. Stars spend most of their lives this way, which is why most of them land on that band: it’s where they spend their working years. The Sun gets about 10 billion years there in total. / Sun: ~10 billion years
  - L70 “Checkpoint: The Lives of Stars” · `card.astronomy.070.c3` (timeline): A Sun-like star’s life, start to finish: Birth: Collapses from a cold, dense cloud of gas and dust; Main sequence: Fuses hydrogen into helium for billions of years; Red giant: Core runs low on hydrogen; outer layers swell; The end: Sheds a planetary nebula; a white dwarf remains and slowly cools
- **Tested by:**
  - L64 `question.astronomy.064.q1`: What is a main-sequence star doing in its core? → **Fusing hydrogen into helium**
- **Fact-check (2026-09-24):** corroborated. Main-sequence stars fuse hydrogen into helium in their cores, and stars spend about 90% of their lives in this stage. ## Isaac Newton (Encyclopaedia Britannica)

- **Source:** `source.brit_newton`
- **URL:** https://www.britannica.com/biography/Isaac-Newton
- **License:** reference_only · **Accessed:** 2026-09-24 · **Source record checked (URL/licence):** no

### ☐ `fact.astronomy.gravity_inverse_square`: unverified

> **Claim:** In Newton’s law, gravity grows with the masses of the objects and weakens with the square of the distance between them.

- **Concept:** Newton’s law of gravity (`concept.astronomy.newton_gravity`)
- **Appears in:**
  - L27 “Newton’s Gravity” · `card.astronomy.027.c2` (text): One law for everything / In 1687 Isaac Newton published his law of universal gravitation. Every object pulls on every other. The pull grows with their masses and weakens with the square of the distance: twice as far, a quarter of the pull. You'd feel the difference on the Moon, where surface gravity is only about one-sixth of Earth's, so you would weigh about one-sixth as much. Great news for your high jump. / Twice as far → ¼ the pull
  - L29 “New Planets: Uranus and Neptune” · `card.astronomy.029.c3` (text): Found by mathematics / Then Uranus misbehaved. It didn't quite follow its predicted path, as if something unseen were tugging on it. Newton's law says every object pulls on every other, so a mystery tug means a mystery puller. Working from those unexplained tugs, Urbain Le Verrier worked out where that unseen planet should be. In 1846 Johann Galle looked there and found Neptune, within about 1° of the prediction. A planet pinned down with math before anyone had knowingly seen it. / Gravity math → new planet
  - L62 “Bright or Close? Luminosity” · `card.astronomy.062.c3` (text): Fading with distance / Distance dims starlight fast. A star’s apparent brightness falls with the square of its distance: move a star twice as far away and it looks only a quarter as bright. Ten times as far? A hundredth. If that rule sounds familiar, it should: gravity in Level 27 weakens with the square of distance too. The universe likes this pattern. / Twice as far → ¼ as bright
  - L75 “The Black Hole at Our Center” · `card.astronomy.075.c2` (text): Following the stars / About 27,000 light-years away, at the center of the Milky Way, stars race around something nobody can see. Picture a racetrack where you can see the cars but not what they’re circling. Astronomers tracked those orbits, and the laws of orbits and gravity from Kepler and Newton turned them into a mass: about 4 million times the Sun’s. One star, S2, laps it about every 16 years. Its name is Sagittarius A*, and it’s a supermassive black hole. / ~4 million Suns
- **Tested by:**
  - L27 `question.astronomy.027.q2`: If you doubled the distance between two objects, what would happen to their gravitational pull? → **It would drop to a quarter**
  - L29 `question.astronomy.029.q3`: From Level 27: which law explains the mystery tug on Uranus that led to Neptune? → **Newton’s law of universal gravitation**
  - L62 `question.astronomy.062.q3`: From Level 27: which other effect weakens with the square of distance, just like starlight? → **Gravity**
  - L65 `question.astronomy.065.q3`: From Level 27: which law lets astronomers turn a binary star’s orbit into a mass? → **Newton’s law of universal gravitation**
- **Fact-check (2026-09-24):** corroborated. Newton's law: force is proportional to the product of the masses and inversely proportional to the square of the distance. Sources: https://openstax.org/books/physics/pages/7-2-newtons-law-of-universal-gravitation-and-einsteins-theory-of-general-relativity, https://www.physicsclassroom.com/class/circles/lesson-3/newton-s-law-of-universal-gravitation

```sh
npm run verify:record -- fact.astronomy.gravity_inverse_square source.brit_newton --status verified --by "Your Name" --quote "…"
```

### ☐ `fact.astronomy.gravity_universal`: unverified

> **Claim:** Newton showed that the same force that makes objects fall on Earth also holds the Moon in orbit and the planets around the Sun.

- **Concept:** Newton’s law of gravity (`concept.astronomy.newton_gravity`)
- **Appears in:**
  - L27 “Newton’s Gravity” · `card.astronomy.027.c1` (text): The force that drops an apple also holds the Moon in the sky. / Kepler described how the planets move. Newton explained why, and the answer was the same thing that pulls your toast to the kitchen floor.
  - L27 “Newton’s Gravity” · `card.astronomy.027.c3` (text): Heaven and Earth, united / Here's the big leap. The gravity that makes an apple fall on Earth is the same force that keeps the Moon in orbit and the planets circling the Sun. One force, from your kitchen to the edge of the solar system. It also explained why Kepler's laws work, turning a description into an explanation. It even reaches the beach: the Moon's gravity pulling on the oceans is what raises the tides (Level 17).
  - L29 “New Planets: Uranus and Neptune” · `card.astronomy.029.c3` (text): Found by mathematics / Then Uranus misbehaved. It didn't quite follow its predicted path, as if something unseen were tugging on it. Newton's law says every object pulls on every other, so a mystery tug means a mystery puller. Working from those unexplained tugs, Urbain Le Verrier worked out where that unseen planet should be. In 1846 Johann Galle looked there and found Neptune, within about 1° of the prediction. A planet pinned down with math before anyone had knowingly seen it. / Gravity math → new planet
  - L37 “Getting to Orbit” · `card.astronomy.037.c2` (text): Falling around Earth / Throw a ball and Earth's gravity curves it down to the ground. Throw it harder and it lands farther away. Throw it fast enough sideways and the ground curves away beneath it as quickly as it falls. That's an orbit: falling forever, and always missing. The International Space Station does this at about 28,000 km/h, circling Earth about every 90 minutes. Around the world in the length of a movie. / Orbit = falling while moving sideways
- **Tested by:**
  - L27 `question.astronomy.027.q1`: What did Newton show about gravity? → **One force makes apples fall and holds the Moon in orbit**
  - L27 `question.astronomy.027.q3`: From Level 17: what connects Newton’s law to the ocean tides? → **The Moon’s gravity pulls on Earth’s oceans**
  - L29 `question.astronomy.029.q3`: From Level 27: which law explains the mystery tug on Uranus that led to Neptune? → **Newton’s law of universal gravitation**
  - L30 `question.astronomy.030.q5`: Chapter 3 recap: what put the final piece in place for why the planets move as they do? → **Newton’s gravity explaining Kepler’s laws**
  - L37 `question.astronomy.037.q3`: From Level 27: which force makes a spacecraft fall around Earth in orbit? → **Earth’s gravity**
- **Fact-check (2026-09-24):** corroborated. Newton showed that the gravity making objects fall on Earth also keeps the Moon in orbit and the planets around the Sun. Sources: https://www.britannica.com/science/How-Was-Gravity-Discovered, https://www.physicsclassroom.com/class/circles/Lesson-3/The-Apple,-the-Moon,-and-the-Inverse-Square-Law

## Nucleosynthesis (Encyclopaedia Britannica)

- **Source:** `source.brit_nucleosynthesis`

### ☐ `fact.astronomy.elements_in_stars`: unverified

> **Claim:** Nearly all elements heavier than hydrogen and helium were made by nuclear reactions inside stars and in stellar explosions.

- **Concept:** Supernovae and star stuff (`concept.astronomy.supernovae_elements`)
- **Appears in:**
  - L69 “Supernovae and Star Stuff” · `card.astronomy.069.c3` (text): Cosmic recycling / Nearly all the elements heavier than hydrogen and helium were made by nuclear reactions inside stars and in stellar explosions. Supernovae scatter them into space, where they become part of new stars, planets and people. The calcium in your bones and the iron in your blood were made in stars that lived and died before the Sun formed. You are, quite literally, secondhand star.
  - L96 “Integration: From Atoms to Galaxies” · `card.astronomy.096.c2` (timeline): From the Big Bang to you: First few minutes: Hydrogen, helium and a trace of lithium form; A few hundred million years: The first stars light up; Billions of years: Generations of stars forge heavier elements and scatter them into space; 4.6 billion years ago: The Sun and planets form from that enriched gas
  - L96 “Integration: From Atoms to Galaxies” · `card.astronomy.096.c3` (text): The great cycle / Think of the galaxy as a kitchen that never throws out leftovers. Stars fuse light elements into heavier ones, just as the Sun turns hydrogen into helium. Massive stars end in supernovae that blast those elements out, scattering them into space. New stars and planets form from that enriched gas, and the cycle repeats. Every generation of stars cooks with what the last one left behind. / Gas → stars → elements → new stars
  - L91 “The Big Bang” · `card.astronomy.091.c4` (text): The first ingredients / In its first few minutes, the young universe made its starter kit: hydrogen, helium and a trace of lithium. That’s it. Nearly every heavier element, from the carbon in you to the iron in a frying pan, would be made later, inside stars. And what came before the Big Bang, if anything? The theory doesn’t say. It tells the story of the cool-down, not what came before the first page.
- **Tested by:**
  - L69 `question.astronomy.069.q2`: Where did most of the elements in your body come from? → **They were forged inside stars and spread by stellar explosions**
  - L69 `question.astronomy.069.q3`: From Level 32: how do astronomers know which elements a supernova blasted out? → **They read the spectral lines in its light**
  - L76 `question.astronomy.076.q3`: From Level 69: supernovae spread heavy elements. What other event, seen in 2017, forges elements like gold? → **Two neutron stars colliding**
  - L96 `question.astronomy.096.q3`: From Level 69: the iron in your blood was made inside stars. How did it reach the cloud that formed the solar system? → **Dying stars scattered it into space**
- **Fact-check (2026-09-24):** corroborated. Elements up to iron form by fusion in stars; heavier ones in supernovae and neutron-star mergers (kilonova explosions). Some Li, Be, B come from cosmic-ray spallation, covered by 'nearly all'. Sources: https://public.nrao.edu/ask/how-are-elements-heavier-than-helium-formed/, https://www.energy.gov/science/doe-explainsnucleosynthesis

## Parallax (Encyclopaedia Britannica)

- **Source:** `source.brit_parallax`
- **URL:** https://www.britannica.com/science/parallax
- **License:** reference_only · **Accessed:** 2026-09-24 · **Source record checked (URL/licence):** no

### ☐ `fact.astronomy.parallax_closer_bigger`: unverified

> **Claim:** The closer a star is, the larger its parallax shift, so measuring the shift gives its distance.

- **Concept:** Measuring star distances with parallax (`concept.astronomy.parallax_method`)
- **Appears in:**
  - L61 “Parallax: Measuring the Stars” · `card.astronomy.061.c3` (text): Closer shifts more / Try the finger trick with your arm stretched out. The hop shrinks. Stars work the same way: the closer the star, the bigger its parallax shift, so measuring the shift gives you its distance. Even for nearby stars the shift is so tiny that ancient observers never detected it. Astronomers still built a unit on it: a parsec, the distance at which the shift would be one arcsecond, is about 3.26 light-years. / 1 parsec ≈ 3.26 light-years
  - L61 “Parallax: Measuring the Stars” · `card.astronomy.061.c1` (text): Blink one eye, then the other. That jump is how we measure the stars. / Hold up a finger and blink: it hops against the wall behind it. Now swap your two eyes for two spots on Earth’s orbit, and your finger for a nearby star. How far it hops tells you how far away it is.
  - L97 “Integration: How We Know What We Know” · `card.astronomy.097.c2` (comparison): Every discovery has a method: What stars are made of (Spectral lines in their light: each element leaves its own set) vs Distance to nearby stars (Parallax: a star’s slight shift as Earth orbits the Sun) vs Planets around other stars (Transits (a dip in starlight) and wobbles (a star tugged back and forth))
- **Tested by:**
  - L61 `question.astronomy.061.q2`: Star A shows a bigger parallax shift than Star B. What does that tell you? → **Star A is closer than Star B**
  - L61 `question.astronomy.061.q3`: From Level 30: Bessel measured stellar parallax in 1838. Why had no one managed it before? → **The shift is so tiny that it is very hard to detect**
  - L97 `question.astronomy.097.q2`: An astronomer wants the distance to a nearby star. Which method fits best? → **Measuring its parallax**
- **Fact-check (2026-09-24):** corroborated. Parallax is inversely proportional to distance (d in parsecs = 1/p in arcseconds), so closer stars show larger shifts. Sources: https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Parallax_in_astronomy, https://sci.esa.int/web/education/-/35616-stellar-distances

```sh
npm run verify:record -- fact.astronomy.parallax_closer_bigger source.brit_parallax --status verified --by "Your Name" --quote "…"
```

### ☐ `fact.astronomy.parallax_shift`: unverified

> **Claim:** As Earth orbits the Sun, a nearby star appears to shift slightly against more distant stars; this shift is called parallax.

- **Concept:** Measuring star distances with parallax (`concept.astronomy.parallax_method`)
- **Appears in:**
  - L61 “Parallax: Measuring the Stars” · `card.astronomy.061.c1` (text): Blink one eye, then the other. That jump is how we measure the stars. / Hold up a finger and blink: it hops against the wall behind it. Now swap your two eyes for two spots on Earth’s orbit, and your finger for a nearby star. How far it hops tells you how far away it is.
  - L61 “Parallax: Measuring the Stars” · `card.astronomy.061.c2` (text): Earth is the head that moves / Your eyes see your finger from two spots a few centimeters apart. Astronomers use two spots on Earth’s orbit around the Sun. Look at a nearby star, then look again half a year later from the far side of the orbit, and the star seems to shift slightly against the more distant stars behind it. That shift is called parallax. The star didn’t move. You did.
  - L97 “Integration: How We Know What We Know” · `card.astronomy.097.c2` (comparison): Every discovery has a method: What stars are made of (Spectral lines in their light: each element leaves its own set) vs Distance to nearby stars (Parallax: a star’s slight shift as Earth orbits the Sun) vs Planets around other stars (Transits (a dip in starlight) and wobbles (a star tugged back and forth))
- **Tested by:**
  - L61 `question.astronomy.061.q1`: What causes a nearby star’s parallax shift? → **Earth orbiting the Sun**
  - L70 `question.astronomy.070.q1`: What measures a nearby star’s distance directly? → **Its parallax shift**
  - L97 `question.astronomy.097.q2`: An astronomer wants the distance to a nearby star. Which method fits best? → **Measuring its parallax**
- **Fact-check (2026-09-24):** corroborated. Stellar parallax is the apparent shift of a nearby star against more distant stars as Earth moves around its orbit. Sources: https://www.britannica.com/science/stellar-parallax, https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Stellar_parallax

### ☐ `fact.astronomy.planetary_nebula`: unverified

> **Claim:** Late in life, a Sun-like star sheds its outer layers into a glowing shell of gas called a planetary nebula.

- **Concept:** Red giants and planetary nebulae (`concept.astronomy.red_giants`)
- **Appears in:**
  - L67 “Red Giants and Planetary Nebulae” · `card.astronomy.067.c3` (text): A glowing farewell / Late in life, a Sun-like star sheds its outer layers into space as a glowing shell of gas called a planetary nebula. Despite the name, it has nothing to do with planets: early astronomers thought these glows looked like planets’ disks through small telescopes, and the name stuck. Enjoy them while they last. Each one glows for only tens of thousands of years, a blink in a star’s life. Then only the star’s exposed core remains.
  - L70 “Checkpoint: The Lives of Stars” · `card.astronomy.070.c3` (timeline): A Sun-like star’s life, start to finish: Birth: Collapses from a cold, dense cloud of gas and dust; Main sequence: Fuses hydrogen into helium for billions of years; Red giant: Core runs low on hydrogen; outer layers swell; The end: Sheds a planetary nebula; a white dwarf remains and slowly cools
  - L70 “Checkpoint: The Lives of Stars” · `card.astronomy.070.c2` (text): Two paths / A star’s final fate depends mainly on its mass. A Sun-like star takes the gentle road: it swells into a red giant, sheds a planetary nebula and settles down as a white dwarf. A star far more massive than the Sun takes the loud road: it explodes as a supernova and leaves behind something much stranger, a neutron star or a black hole. Same universe, very different retirement plans. / Sun-like → white dwarf · Massive → neutron star or black hole
- **Tested by:**
  - L67 `question.astronomy.067.q1`: What is a planetary nebula? → **A glowing shell of gas shed by a dying Sun-like star**
  - L70 `question.astronomy.070.q5`: Chapter 7 recap: which order follows a Sun-like star through its life? → **Cloud of gas → main sequence → red giant → white dwarf**
- **Fact-check (2026-09-24):** corroborated. Near the end of life, low-mass Sun-like stars shed outer layers forming a glowing shell of gas (planetary nebula) around a white dwarf. Sources: https://www.britannica.com/science/planetary-nebula, https://esahubble.org/wordbank/planetary-nebula/

### ☐ `fact.astronomy.polynesian_wayfinding`: unverified

> **Claim:** Polynesian navigators crossed thousands of kilometers of open Pacific Ocean using the rising and setting points of stars, along with ocean swells and wildlife.

- **Concept:** Navigating by the stars (`concept.astronomy.star_navigation`)
- **Appears in:**
  - L21 “The First Sky-Watchers” · `card.astronomy.021.c4` (fact): Polynesian navigators crossed thousands of kilometers of open Pacific Ocean by reading the sky. / They knew the points on the horizon where particular stars rise and set, and steered by them, with ocean swells and wildlife as backup clues. Stars rise in the east because Earth spins (Level 11), so a known star coming up over the water points the way. The whole night sky, used as a compass.
  - L21 “The First Sky-Watchers” · `card.astronomy.021.c1` (text): Long before clocks, the sky was the clock. / No watches, no wall calendars, no GPS. So how did people know when to plant, when to celebrate, or which way lay home across the sea? They read the sky, the same patterns you learned in Chapter 2.
- **Tested by:**
  - L21 `question.astronomy.021.q3`: From Level 11: why could Polynesian navigators use a star’s rising point on the horizon to steer? → **Earth’s spin brings each star up in the east the same way every night**

- **Fact-check (2026-09-24):** corroborated. University of Hawaii, Bishop Museum and Hokulea sources describe navigators using rising and setting star positions, ocean swells and birds to cross thousands of km (e.g. Hawaii to Tahiti, ~4,000 km). Sources: https://manoa.hawaii.edu/exploringourfluidearth/physical/navigation-and-transportation/wayfinding-and-navigation, https://hokulea.com/polynesian-wayfinding/, https://www.bishopmuseum.org/online-learning-center/voyaging-in-the-pacific/

### ☐ `fact.astronomy.epicycles`: unverified

> **Claim:** Ptolemy’s model explained retrograde motion by having each planet move on a small circle (an epicycle) that itself moved around Earth.

- **Concept:** The Earth-centered model (`concept.astronomy.geocentric_model`)
- **Appears in:**
  - L23 “An Earth-Centered Universe” · `card.astronomy.023.c2` (text): Ptolemy's universe / Around 150 CE, Ptolemy described it in a book later known as the Almagest: Earth motionless at the center, with the Sun, Moon, planets and stars all circling it. One snag: planets sometimes seem to go backwards (Level 16's retrograde motion). Ptolemy's fix was to put each planet on a small circle, an epicycle, that itself moved around Earth. Think of a teacup ride: you spin on a platform that is also spinning. / Circles on circles
- **Tested by:**
  - L23 `question.astronomy.023.q1`: In Ptolemy’s model, what sat at the center of the universe? → **Earth**
  - L23 `question.astronomy.023.q3`: From Level 16: how did Ptolemy’s model explain retrograde motion? → **Planets riding epicycles as they circled Earth**
  - L24 `question.astronomy.024.q3`: From Level 23: what was one weakness Copernicus’s model shared with Ptolemy’s? → **It still assumed perfectly circular orbits**
- **Fact-check (2026-09-24):** corroborated. Ptolemy placed each planet on an epicycle whose center moved on a deferent around Earth, producing retrograde loops. Sources: https://www.britannica.com/science/epicycle, https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Deferent_and_epicycle

### ☐ `fact.astronomy.geocentric_lasted`: unverified

> **Claim:** The geocentric model was the dominant view in Europe and the Middle East for about 1,400 years.

- **Concept:** The Earth-centered model (`concept.astronomy.geocentric_model`)
- **Appears in:**
  - L23 “An Earth-Centered Universe” · `card.astronomy.023.c1` (text): For 1,400 years, scholars agreed: Earth sits still at the center. / Before you laugh, step outside. The ground feels perfectly still, and the whole sky seems to turn around you. The idea fit what people could actually see.
  - L24 “Copernicus Moves the Sun” · `card.astronomy.024.c4` (fact): Copernicus wasn't the first to put the Sun in the middle. / Aristarchus of Samos proposed a Sun-centered arrangement around 1,800 years earlier. It didn't win: the Earth-centered view ruled for about 1,400 years instead. Being right, it turns out, isn't the same as being believed.
- **Fact-check (2026-09-24):** corroborated. Ptolemy's Almagest (2nd century CE) geocentric system remained dominant until after Copernicus (1543), roughly 1,400 years. Sources: https://www.teachastronomy.com/textbook/The-Copernican-Revolution/Ptolemy-and-the-Geocentric-Model/, https://oshermaps.org/special-map-exhibits/philosophy-religion-and-the-center-of-the-universe/ptolemy-vs-copernicus/

### ☐ `fact.astronomy.no_feeling_motion`: unverified

> **Claim:** A major reason the geocentric model seemed sensible is that people cannot feel Earth moving.

- **Concept:** Why an Earth-centered view made sense (`concept.astronomy.geocentric_reasons`)
- **Appears in:**
  - L23 “An Earth-Centered Universe” · `card.astronomy.023.c3` (text): Why it made sense / First, you can't feel Earth moving. Second, there was a sharp test. If Earth orbited the Sun, nearby stars should shift slightly over a year, the way a nearby tree shifts against distant hills as you walk past. Nobody could detect that shift, called parallax. No shift, so no motion, it seemed. And the model predicted planet positions well enough to run calendars for centuries. Wrong, but very useful.
  - L23 “An Earth-Centered Universe” · `card.astronomy.023.c1` (text): For 1,400 years, scholars agreed: Earth sits still at the center. / Before you laugh, step outside. The ground feels perfectly still, and the whole sky seems to turn around you. The idea fit what people could actually see.
  - L30 “Checkpoint: How We Know” · `card.astronomy.030.c3` (fact): In 1838 Friedrich Bessel finally measured a star shifting as Earth orbits. / The star, 61 Cygni, moved by a tiny angle over the year. That shift, parallax, is exactly what the doubters of Level 23 had looked for and never found. It gave the first measured distance to a star, and direct proof that Earth goes around the Sun, just as Copernicus argued. The ground still feels still. It just isn't.
- **Tested by:**
  - L23 `question.astronomy.023.q2`: Why did the lack of an observed stellar shift (parallax) seem to support an Earth-centered universe? → **A moving Earth should shift nearby stars, and none moved**
  - L30 `question.astronomy.030.q4`: Why was Bessel’s 1838 measurement of stellar parallax so important? → **It showed Earth really does move around the Sun**
- **Fact-check (2026-09-24):** corroborated. Geocentrists argued that if Earth moved we would feel it; many astronomers found this a powerful argument before inertia was understood. Sources: https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Geocentric_model, https://nerd.wwnorton.com/ebooks/epub/astro7/EPUB/content/3.1-chapter03.xhtml

### ☐ `fact.astronomy.ptolemy_almagest`: unverified

> **Claim:** Around 150 CE, Ptolemy described an Earth-centered (geocentric) model of the universe in a book later known as the Almagest.

- **Concept:** The Earth-centered model (`concept.astronomy.geocentric_model`)
- **Appears in:**
  - L23 “An Earth-Centered Universe” · `card.astronomy.023.c2` (text): Ptolemy's universe / Around 150 CE, Ptolemy described it in a book later known as the Almagest: Earth motionless at the center, with the Sun, Moon, planets and stars all circling it. One snag: planets sometimes seem to go backwards (Level 16's retrograde motion). Ptolemy's fix was to put each planet on a small circle, an epicycle, that itself moved around Earth. Think of a teacup ride: you spin on a platform that is also spinning. / Circles on circles
  - L30 “Checkpoint: How We Know” · `card.astronomy.030.c2` (timeline): How we figured it out: ~240 BCE: Eratosthenes measures Earth with the noon Sun; ~150 CE: Ptolemy puts a still Earth at the center; 1543: Copernicus hands the center to the Sun; 1610: Galileo finds moons circling Jupiter; 1687: Newton's gravity explains why planets move; 1838: Bessel measures stellar parallax
  - L24 “Copernicus Moves the Sun” · `card.astronomy.024.c1` (text): In 1543, one book quietly demoted Earth to “just another planet.” / Remember Ptolemy's motionless Earth at the center? Copernicus took the center seat away from us and gave it to the Sun.
  - L25 “Galileo’s Telescope” · `card.astronomy.025.c3` (text): Moons that don't orbit Earth / In 1610 Galileo found four large moons circling Jupiter: Io, Europa, Ganymede and Callisto. That was bad news for Ptolemy. His model said everything circles Earth, and here were four worlds plainly orbiting Jupiter, not Earth. Earth wasn't the center of every orbit. It wasn't even the only planet with a moon. / Io · Europa · Ganymede · Callisto
- **Tested by:**

It set out the geocentric Ptolemaic system. Sources: https://www.britannica.com/topic/Almagest, https://www.britannica.com/science/Ptolemaic-system

### ☐ `fact.astronomy.ptolemy_predictions`: unverified

> **Claim:** Ptolemy’s model predicted the positions of the planets accurately enough to be used for calendars and astronomy for centuries.

- **Concept:** The Earth-centered model (`concept.astronomy.geocentric_model`)
- **Appears in:**
  - L23 “An Earth-Centered Universe” · `card.astronomy.023.c3` (text): Why it made sense / First, you can't feel Earth moving. Second, there was a sharp test. If Earth orbited the Sun, nearby stars should shift slightly over a year, the way a nearby tree shifts against distant hills as you walk past. Nobody could detect that shift, called parallax. No shift, so no motion, it seemed. And the model predicted planet positions well enough to run calendars for centuries. Wrong, but very useful.
- **Fact-check (2026-09-24):** corroborated. Ptolemy's Almagest predicted planetary positions to within about a degree or two and remained the standard astronomy text for about 1,400 years. > **Claim:** A pulsar is a spinning neutron star that sends out beams of radio waves; when a beam sweeps past Earth, we see a pulse, like a lighthouse.

  - L72 “Pulsars: Cosmic Lighthouses” · `card.astronomy.072.c2` (text): Lighthouses in space / Remember Level 71’s spinning neutron stars? A pulsar is one of them, sending out beams of radio waves. The beam doesn’t switch on and off; the star spins. Each time a beam sweeps past Earth, we catch a pulse, exactly like a lighthouse flashing across a dark sea. Some pulsars spin hundreds of times every second. That’s a city-sized star turning like a kitchen blender. / Spin + beam = pulse
  - L80 “Checkpoint: Extreme Objects and Other Worlds” · `card.astronomy.080.c3` (text): Listening and looking / Each discovery in this chapter came from reading a different signal. And tiny dips and wobbles in starlight have uncovered thousands of worlds around other stars, some, like TRAPPIST-1’s, in their habitable zones. Dead stars and new worlds, found the same way: by paying very close attention.
  - L72 “Pulsars: Cosmic Lighthouses” · `card.astronomy.072.c1` (text): A signal so regular it was nicknamed “LGM”: little green men. / In 1967 a radio telescope picked up a pulse that ticked like a clock. It wasn’t aliens. It was something almost as strange: a star keeping time.

  - L72 “Pulsars: Cosmic Lighthouses” · `card.astronomy.072.c4` (fact): The Crab Nebula has a pulsar spinning at its heart. / The Crab is the wreckage of a supernova that Chinese astronomers recorded in 1054. ### ☐ `fact.astronomy.quasar_1963`: unverified

> **Claim:** The first quasar was identified in 1963, when Maarten Schmidt found that the object 3C 273 was extremely distant and luminous.

- **Concept:** Quasars and active galaxies (`concept.astronomy.quasars`)
- **Appears in:**
  - L87 “Quasars and Active Galaxies” · `card.astronomy.087.c4` (fact): The first quasar was identified in 1963. / In 1963 Maarten Schmidt worked out that the object 3C 273 was extremely distant and enormously luminous. To shine that bright from that far away, it had to be one of the most powerful lights in the universe. That's a quasar.
- **Fact-check (2026-09-24):** corroborated. On Feb. 5, 1963, Maarten Schmidt recognized redshifted hydrogen lines in 3C 273, implying a distance of billions of light-years and huge luminosity; published in Nature March 1963 as the first quasar. Sources: https://www.caltech.edu/about/news/fifty-years-quasars-38937, https://www.aps.org/apsnews/2021/02/maarten-schmidt-discovers-first-quasar

### ☐ `fact.astronomy.quasar_accretion`: unverified

> **Claim:** When a supermassive black hole pulls in large amounts of gas, the gas heats up in a swirling disk and can outshine the entire galaxy; such an object is called a quasar.

- **Concept:** Quasars and active galaxies (`concept.astronomy.quasars`)
- **Appears in:**
  - L87 “Quasars and Active Galaxies” · `card.astronomy.087.c1` (text): Some of the brightest objects in the universe are powered by black holes. / Black holes are famous for trapping light. So how can one sit behind some of the brightest things in the universe? The secret is in how it eats.
  - L87 “Quasars and Active Galaxies” · `card.astronomy.087.c2` (text): Monsters at the center / Most large galaxies have a supermassive black hole at their center. When one pulls in large amounts of gas, the gas spirals in, heats up in a swirling disk and glows fiercely, all before it crosses the event horizon. Nothing escapes from inside, so the light comes from the gas, not the black hole. It's the meal that glows, not the diner.
  - L87 “Quasars and Active Galaxies” · `card.astronomy.087.c3` (text): Brighter than a galaxy / How bright? A feeding black hole's glowing disk can outshine all the stars of its galaxy combined. The brightest of these are called quasars, and they're among the most distant and luminous objects known. One hot disk of gas beats a whole galaxy of stars. The darkest objects in the universe run some of its brightest lights. / Feeding black hole → quasar
- **Tested by:**
  - L87 `question.astronomy.087.q1`: What powers a quasar? → **Gas heating up as it spirals toward a supermassive black hole**
  - L87 `question.astronomy.087.q2`: Why can a quasar outshine an entire galaxy? → **Gas heated around its black hole glows more brightly than all the stars combined**
  - L87 `question.astronomy.087.q3`: From Level 75: our galaxy has Sagittarius A*. Why isn’t the Milky Way a quasar? → **Sagittarius A* isn’t pulling in large amounts of gas right now**
- **Fact-check (2026-09-24):** corroborated. Gas falling into a supermassive black hole forms a hot accretion disk that can outshine all the stars in its galaxy; this is observed as a quasar. Sources: https://keckobservatory.org/earliest-quasar/, https://www.space.com/supermassive-black-hole-quasar-record-breaking-observation-virtual-telescope-project

### ☐ `fact.astronomy.red_giant_swell`: unverified

> **Claim:** When a Sun-like star’s core runs low on hydrogen, its core contracts and heats up while its outer layers swell into a red giant.

- **Concept:** Red giants and planetary nebulae (`concept.astronomy.red_giants`)
- **Appears in:**
  - L67 “Red Giants and Planetary Nebulae” · `card.astronomy.067.c2` (text): Swelling up / When a Sun-like star’s core runs low on hydrogen, the core contracts and heats up while the outer layers swell enormously. The star becomes a red giant: cool but very luminous, so it moves up and to the right on the H–R diagram. In about 5 billion years the Sun should grow large enough to engulf Mercury and Venus, and possibly Earth. Inner-planet real estate: not a long-term investment. / Core shrinks · outer layers swell
  - L70 “Checkpoint: The Lives of Stars” · `card.astronomy.070.c3` (timeline): A Sun-like star’s life, start to finish: Birth: Collapses from a cold, dense cloud of gas and dust; Main sequence: Fuses hydrogen into helium for billions of years; Red giant: Core runs low on hydrogen; outer layers swell; The end: Sheds a planetary nebula; a white dwarf remains and slowly cools
  - L9 “The Life of a Star” · `card.astronomy.009.c3` (timeline): The Sun's life, in four stops: ~4.6 billion years ago: Born from a collapsing cloud of gas and dust; Now: Middle-aged, fusing hydrogen into helium in its core; ~5 billion years from now: Runs low on hydrogen in its core and swells into a red giant; After that: Sheds its outer layers and ends as a white dwarf
  - L70 “Checkpoint: The Lives of Stars” · `card.astronomy.070.c2` (text): Two paths / A star’s final fate depends mainly on its mass. A Sun-like star takes the gentle road: it swells into a red giant, sheds a planetary nebula and settles down as a white dwarf. A star far more massive than the Sun takes the loud road: it explodes as a supernova and leaves behind something much stranger, a neutron star or a black hole. Same universe, very different retirement plans. / Sun-like → white dwarf · Massive → neutron star or black hole
- **Tested by:**
  - L67 `question.astronomy.067.q2`: What happens to a Sun-like star when its core runs low on hydrogen? → **Its core contracts while its outer layers swell into a red giant**
  - L67 `question.astronomy.067.q3`: From Level 63: where on the H–R diagram would the Sun move as it becomes a red giant? → **Up and to the right: cooler but more luminous**
  - L70 `question.astronomy.070.q5`: Chapter 7 recap: which order follows a Sun-like star through its life? → **Cloud of gas → main sequence → red giant → white dwarf**
- **Fact-check (2026-09-24):** corroborated. When core hydrogen is exhausted the core contracts and heats, shell hydrogen fusion begins, and the outer layers expand into a red giant. ### ☐ `fact.astronomy.redshift_def`: unverified

> **Claim:** Redshift is the stretching of light to longer, redder wavelengths, seen as a shift of spectral lines toward the red.

- **Concept:** Redshift (`concept.astronomy.redshift`)
- **Appears in:**
  - L89 “Redshift” · `card.astronomy.089.c1` (text): Distant galaxies look a little redder than they should. / It isn't the color of their stars. Something happens to their light on the long trip here, and that change tells astronomers how far it came.
  - L89 “Redshift” · `card.astronomy.089.c2` (text): Stretched light / Remember Level 88: space is expanding. As light crosses expanding space, its wavelength gets stretched, like a wave drawn on a rubber band as you pull it. Red has the longest wavelength of visible light, so stretched light shifts toward the red end of the spectrum. Astronomers call this redshift: light stretched to longer, redder wavelengths. / Stretched light → redder
  - L89 “Redshift” · `card.astronomy.089.c3` (text): Reading the shift / How do you spot a stretch? With spectral lines. Each element absorbs and emits light only at its own known wavelengths, like a barcode. In a distant galaxy, those familiar lines show up shifted toward the red. And the more distant the galaxy, the bigger the shift. Same barcode, slid toward the red. / Farther → redder
- **Tested by:**
  - L89 `question.astronomy.089.q1`: What is redshift? → **Light stretched to longer, redder wavelengths**
  - L89 `question.astronomy.089.q2`: Galaxy X’s spectral lines are shifted much farther toward the red than galaxy Y’s. What can you conclude? → **X is probably farther away than Y**
  - L89 `question.astronomy.089.q3`: From Level 32: why are spectral lines so useful for measuring redshift? → **Each element’s lines have known wavelengths**
  - L90 `question.astronomy.090.q4`: A faraway galaxy shows a very large redshift. What does that tell you? → **It is very far away and receding fast**
- **Fact-check (2026-09-24):** corroborated. Redshift is the displacement of an object's spectrum toward longer (redder) wavelengths; light is stretched and spectral lines appear at longer wavelengths than expected. Sources: https://www.britannica.com/science/redshift, https://www.esa.int/Science_Exploration/Space_Science/What_is_red_shift

### ☐ `fact.astronomy.redshift_distance`: unverified

  - L89 “Redshift” · `card.astronomy.089.c3` (text): Reading the shift / How do you spot a stretch? With spectral lines. Each element absorbs and emits light only at its own known wavelengths, like a barcode. In a distant galaxy, those familiar lines show up shifted toward the red. And the more distant the galaxy, the bigger the shift. Same barcode, slid toward the red. / Farther → redder

  - L90 “Checkpoint: A Universe of Galaxies” · `card.astronomy.090.c3` (text): The cosmic web / At the top of the ladder, galaxies and clusters form a vast web of long filaments and sheets wrapped around huge, nearly empty voids. Picture the foam in a bubble bath: galaxies in the soapy film, emptiness in the bubbles. And the whole web is stretching as the universe expands, which is why distant galaxies look redshifted. The largest pattern in the universe is still stretching. / Filaments · sheets · voids
  - L97 “Integration: How We Know What We Know” · `card.astronomy.097.c3` (text): New windows / Some discoveries needed brand-new kinds of clues. And in 2015 LIGO first detected gravitational waves from merging black holes: not light at all, but ripples in spacetime. Astronomy learned to feel the universe shake.
  - L89 “Redshift” · `card.astronomy.089.c1` (text): Distant galaxies look a little redder than they should. / It isn't the color of their stars. Something happens to their light on the long trip here, and that change tells astronomers how far it came.

Red has the longest wavelength of visible light, so stretched light shifts toward the red end of the spectrum. Astronomers call this redshift: light stretched to longer, redder wavelengths. / Stretched light → redder
  - L92 “The Afterglow: Cosmic Microwave Background” · `card.astronomy.092.c3` (text): Stretched to microwaves / The CMB started as the glow of hot gas. Then it spent billions of years crossing expanding space, and as space expanded, its light was stretched to longer and longer wavelengths, all the way to microwaves. Today it’s only about 2.7 degrees above absolute zero. Same light, same journey: a fiery glow, stretched until it’s barely warm at all. / ≈ 2.7 K
  - L89 “Redshift” · `card.astronomy.089.c1` (text): Distant galaxies look a little redder than they should. / It isn't the color of their stars. Something happens to their light on the long trip here, and that change tells astronomers how far it came.
  - L90 “Checkpoint: A Universe of Galaxies” · `card.astronomy.090.c3` (text): The cosmic web / At the top of the ladder, galaxies and clusters form a vast web of long filaments and sheets wrapped around huge, nearly empty voids. Picture the foam in a bubble bath: galaxies in the soapy film, emptiness in the bubbles. And the whole web is stretching as the universe expands, which is why distant galaxies look redshifted. The largest pattern in the universe is still stretching. / Filaments · sheets · voids
- **Tested by:**
  - L89 `question.astronomy.089.q1`: What is redshift? → **Light stretched to longer, redder wavelengths**
  - L92 `question.astronomy.092.q2`: Why does the CMB reach us as microwaves rather than visible light? → **Expanding space stretched its wavelengths**
- **Fact-check (2026-09-24):** corroborated. Cosmological redshift: light traveling through expanding space is stretched to longer, redder wavelengths; more distant galaxies show larger redshifts. Sources: https://www.skyatnightmagazine.com/space-science/redshift, https://astronomy.swin.edu.au/cosmos/c/cosmological+redshift

### ☐ `fact.astronomy.sirius_b`: unverified

> **Claim:** Sirius has a small, faint companion, Sirius B, which is a white dwarf.

- **Concept:** Binary stars (`concept.astronomy.binary_stars`)

The Sun is single, and that’s no scandal: most stars are small red dwarfs, and most of those have no companion either. Our nearest neighbor, Alpha Centauri, is a family of three stars, including Proxima Centauri.   - L68 “White Dwarfs” · `card.astronomy.068.c3` (text): Slowly fading / A white dwarf no longer fuses anything. It shines only with leftover heat, like an oven after it’s switched off, and slowly cools over billions of years. That’s why it sits in the lower left of the H–R diagram: hot, but dim. A famous example is Sirius B, the faint partner of Sirius, the brightest star in the night sky. It’s also the most common ending: most stars in the Milky Way, the Sun included, will finish as white dwarfs.

### ☐ `fact.astronomy.element_lines`: unverified

> **Claim:** Each chemical element absorbs and emits light only at its own specific set of wavelengths, called spectral lines.

- **Concept:** Spectral fingerprints (`concept.astronomy.spectral_lines`)
- **Appears in:**
  - L32 “Starlight’s Fingerprints” · `card.astronomy.032.c2` (text): Lines like barcodes / Spread starlight into a spectrum, a rainbow of colors, and you'll find thin dark lines crossing it. Each chemical element absorbs and emits light only at its own set of wavelengths, so each element leaves its own pattern of lines, like a barcode. Spot the pattern and you know which elements are there. It's a checkout scanner for the universe. / Element → unique set of lines
  - L89 “Redshift” · `card.astronomy.089.c3` (text): Reading the shift / How do you spot a stretch? With spectral lines. Each element absorbs and emits light only at its own known wavelengths, like a barcode. In a distant galaxy, those familiar lines show up shifted toward the red. And the more distant the galaxy, the bigger the shift. Same barcode, slid toward the red. / Farther → redder
  - L97 “Integration: How We Know What We Know” · `card.astronomy.097.c2` (comparison): Every discovery has a method: What stars are made of (Spectral lines in their light: each element leaves its own set) vs Distance to nearby stars (Parallax: a star’s slight shift as Earth orbits the Sun) vs Planets around other stars (Transits (a dip in starlight) and wobbles (a star tugged back and forth))
  - L77 “Finding Exoplanets” · `card.astronomy.077.c3` (text): Dips and wobbles / Two tricks do the finding. The transit method watches for the small, regular dip in a star’s light when a planet passes in front of it: a tiny blink, again on every orbit. The wobble method watches the star move. A planet’s gravity tugs it slightly back and forth, and that motion shows up as shifts in the star’s spectral lines, Level 32’s barcodes. One trick catches the shadow; the other catches the tug. / Transit = dip · Wobble = spectral lines shift
- **Tested by:**
  - L32 `question.astronomy.032.q2`: How can astronomers tell which elements are in a distant star? → **By matching the lines in its spectrum to known elements**
  - L40 `question.astronomy.040.q1`: What do spectral lines tell astronomers about a star? → **Which elements the star contains**
  - L69 `question.astronomy.069.q3`: From Level 32: how do astronomers know which elements a supernova blasted out? → **They read the spectral lines in its light**
  - L77 `question.astronomy.077.q3`: From Level 32: how do spectra help find planets with the wobble method? → **The star’s spectral lines shift as it wobbles**
  - L89 `question.astronomy.089.q3`: From Level 32: why are spectral lines so useful for measuring redshift? → **Each element’s lines have known wavelengths**
  - L97 `question.astronomy.097.q1`: Which pairing of method and discovery is correct? → **Spectral lines → what stars are made of**
- **Fact-check (2026-09-24):** corroborated. Each element's electron energy levels are unique, so it absorbs and emits at a characteristic set of wavelengths (spectral lines), used as atomic fingerprints. Sources: https://www.sas.upenn.edu/~rachelmr/speclines2.htm, https://scienceinsights.org/what-are-spectral-lines-emission-absorption-more/

- **Concept:** Spectral fingerprints (`concept.astronomy.spectral_lines`)
- **Appears in:**
  - L32 “Starlight’s Fingerprints” · `card.astronomy.032.c1` (text): We know what stars are made of without ever touching one. / You can't visit a star, let alone scoop out a sample. Yet astronomers can read a star's ingredients list. It's written in the starlight.
  - L32 “Starlight’s Fingerprints” · `card.astronomy.032.c3` (text): Reading a star / Astronomers match the lines in a star's spectrum to each element's known pattern, and out comes the ingredients list. The answer, for star after star: mostly hydrogen and helium. That shouldn't surprise you. Remember Level 2? The Sun's core fuses hydrogen into helium. The ingredients list and the recipe match.
  - L97 “Integration: How We Know What We Know” · `card.astronomy.097.c2` (comparison): Every discovery has a method: What stars are made of (Spectral lines in their light: each element leaves its own set) vs Distance to nearby stars (Parallax: a star’s slight shift as Earth orbits the Sun) vs Planets around other stars (Transits (a dip in starlight) and wobbles (a star tugged back and forth))
  - L32 “Starlight’s Fingerprints” · `card.astronomy.032.c2` (text): Lines like barcodes / Spread starlight into a spectrum, a rainbow of colors, and you'll find thin dark lines crossing it. Each chemical element absorbs and emits light only at its own set of wavelengths, so each element leaves its own pattern of lines, like a barcode. Spot the pattern and you know which elements are there. It's a checkout scanner for the universe. / Element → unique set of lines
- **Tested by:**
  - L32 `question.astronomy.032.q1`: What are stars made of, mostly? → **Hydrogen and helium**
  - L32 `question.astronomy.032.q2`: How can astronomers tell which elements are in a distant star? → **By matching the lines in its spectrum to known elements**
  - L32 `question.astronomy.032.q3`: From Level 2: why does it make sense that stars are mostly hydrogen and helium? → **Stars fuse hydrogen into helium in their cores**
  - L40 `question.astronomy.040.q1`: What do spectral lines tell astronomers about a star? → **Which elements the star contains**
  - L69 `question.astronomy.069.q3`: From Level 32: how do astronomers know which elements a supernova blasted out? → **They read the spectral lines in its light**
  - L77 `question.astronomy.077.q3`: From Level 32: how do spectra help find planets with the wobble method? → **The star’s spectral lines shift as it wobbles**
  - L89 `question.astronomy.089.q3`: From Level 32: why are spectral lines so useful for measuring redshift? → **Each element’s lines have known wavelengths**
  - L91 `question.astronomy.091.q3`: From Level 32: stars are made mostly of hydrogen and helium. Where did those elements first come from? → **The first minutes after the Big Bang**
  - L97 `question.astronomy.097.q1`: Which pairing of method and discovery is correct? → **Spectral lines → what stars are made of**
- **Fact-check (2026-09-24):** corroborated. Absorption line wavelengths are unique to each element, so matching lines in a stellar spectrum reveals which elements are present. Sources: https://www.teachastronomy.com/textbook/Properties-of-Stars/Stellar-Composition/, https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Stellar_chemistry

### ☐ `fact.astronomy.sun_brightening`: unverified

> **Claim:** The Sun is slowly getting brighter; in roughly 1 billion years it is expected to make Earth too hot for liquid-water oceans.

- **Concept:** Futures of the Sun, Earth and universe (`concept.astronomy.cosmic_futures`)
- **Appears in:**
  - L98 “Integration: Futures of the Sun, Earth and Universe” · `card.astronomy.098.c2` (timeline): The long forecast: Over ~26,000 years: Earth’s axis wobbles through a full circle, so Polaris won’t stay the pole star; In ~1 billion years: A slowly brightening Sun makes Earth too hot for oceans; In ~4.5 billion years, maybe: The Milky Way and Andromeda may merge; a 2025 study put it at about a coin flip; In ~5 billion years: The Sun swells into a red giant, later becoming a white dwarf
- **Tested by:**
  - L98 `question.astronomy.098.q1`: Which of these is expected to happen first? → **Earth becomes too hot for oceans**
  - L98 `question.astronomy.098.q2`: Why won’t Polaris always be the North Star? → **Earth’s axis slowly wobbles in a circle**
- **Pre-check:** Commonly cited ~1 billion years; confirm the page, or cite a NASA source instead.
- **Fact-check (2026-09-24):** corroborated. The Sun brightens about 10% per billion years; common estimates put loss of Earth's oceans around 1 billion years out, though some models give 1 to 1.75 billion years, consistent with 'roughly'. Sources: https://theconversation.com/the-sun-wont-die-for-5-billion-years-so-why-do-humans-have-only-1-billion-years-left-on-earth-37379, https://www.science.org/content/article/earth-wont-die-soon-thought

### ☐ `fact.astronomy.solar_cycle_11`: unverified

> **Claim:** The number of sunspots rises and falls in a solar cycle that lasts about 11 years.

- **Concept:** Sunspots and the solar cycle (`concept.astronomy.sunspots_cycle`)
- **Appears in:**
  - L57 “Sunspots and the Solar Cycle” · `card.astronomy.057.c1` (text): The Sun has spots, and they come and go on a schedule. / Dark blotches on the Sun's face grow in number, fade away, then come back, over and over. Why are they dark, and who set the schedule?
  - L57 “Sunspots and the Solar Cycle” · `card.astronomy.057.c3` (text): An 11-year rhythm / Sunspots keep a calendar. Their number rises and falls in a solar cycle that lasts about 11 years. At the busy end, the Sun is freckled with spots. Near solar minimum, it can go for days with no visible sunspots at all. Then the count climbs again. Call it the Sun's very slow breathing.
- **Tested by:**
  - L57 `question.astronomy.057.q1`: About how long is the solar cycle? → **About 11 years**
- **Fact-check (2026-09-24):** corroborated. NOAA and NASA describe sunspot numbers rising and falling in a cycle of roughly 11 years (range about 8 to 14). Sources: https://www.swpc.noaa.gov/news/sunspots-and-solar-cycles, https://spaceplace.nasa.gov/solar-cycles/en/

### ☐ `fact.astronomy.sunspots_cooler`: unverified

> **Claim:** Sunspots are regions of the Sun’s surface that are cooler, and so darker, than their surroundings, caused by intense magnetic fields.

- **Concept:** Sunspots and the solar cycle (`concept.astronomy.sunspots_cycle`)
- **Appears in:**
  - L57 “Sunspots and the Solar Cycle” · `card.astronomy.057.c2` (text): Dark, but not cold / Sunspots are patches of the Sun's surface where intense magnetic fields make them cooler than their surroundings. Cooler means darker, so they show up as dark blotches. But cool is relative: the surface around them is about 5,500 °C (Level 56). A sunspot only looks dark because it sits next to something brighter, like a candle held up in front of a floodlight. / Cooler → darker
  - L57 “Sunspots and the Solar Cycle” · `card.astronomy.057.c1` (text): The Sun has spots, and they come and go on a schedule. / Dark blotches on the Sun's face grow in number, fade away, then come back, over and over. Why are they dark, and who set the schedule?

### ☐ `fact.astronomy.core_collapse`: unverified

> **Claim:** Stars more than about eight times as massive as the Sun end their lives when their cores collapse, triggering a supernova.

- **Concept:** Supernovae and star stuff (`concept.astronomy.supernovae_elements`)
- **Appears in:**
  - L69 “Supernovae and Star Stuff” · `card.astronomy.069.c2` (text): Going out with a bang / Stars more than about eight times as massive as the Sun don’t fade quietly like the white dwarfs of Level 68. Their cores collapse, and the collapse triggers a supernova, an explosion that can briefly outshine an entire galaxy. For a moment, one dying star can be brighter than all its neighbors put together. / Over ~8 Suns → core collapse
- **Tested by:**
