- **Fact-check (2026-09-24):** corroborated. Stars above about 8 solar masses build iron cores that collapse, producing core-collapse supernovae (some of the most massive may collapse with little visible explosion). Sources: https://lco.global/spacebook/stars/supernova/, https://www.pnas.org/doi/10.1073/pnas.1920319116

### ☐ `fact.astronomy.fate_by_mass`: unverified

> **Claim:** A star’s final fate depends mainly on its mass: Sun-like stars end as white dwarfs, while the most massive stars explode and leave behind neutron stars or black holes.

- **Concept:** Mass decides a star’s fate (`concept.astronomy.stellar_fates`)
- **Appears in:**
  - L70 “Checkpoint: The Lives of Stars” · `card.astronomy.070.c2` (text): Two paths / A star’s final fate depends mainly on its mass. A Sun-like star takes the gentle road: it swells into a red giant, sheds a planetary nebula and settles down as a white dwarf. A star far more massive than the Sun takes the loud road: it explodes as a supernova and leaves behind something much stranger, a neutron star or a black hole. Same universe, very different retirement plans. / Sun-like → white dwarf · Massive → neutron star or black hole
  - L70 “Checkpoint: The Lives of Stars” · `card.astronomy.070.c1` (text): Chapter 7 finale. One number decides a star’s whole life story. / That number is its mass. Everything in this chapter, from nursery to final glow, follows from it.
  - L73 “Black Holes” · `card.astronomy.073.c2` (text): Gravity wins / A black hole is a region of space where gravity is so strong that nothing, not even light, can escape. In Level 37’s terms, its escape velocity is faster than the speed of light, and nothing can go that fast. That’s why it’s black: no light comes out for you to see. Stellar black holes form when the core of a very massive star collapses at the end of its life. Same kind of ending as a neutron star, with an even stranger leftover.
  - L80 “Checkpoint: Extreme Objects and Other Worlds” · `card.astronomy.080.c1` (text): Chapter 8 finale. From dead stars to living worlds. / Level 70 said mass decides how a star ends. Here’s what each ending leaves behind, and how we learned to find things that barely want to be found.
- **Tested by:**
  - L70 `question.astronomy.070.q2`: How will a Sun-like star end its life? → **As a white dwarf**
  - L70 `question.astronomy.070.q4`: What decides whether a star ends as a white dwarf or explodes as a supernova? → **Its mass**
  - L100 `question.astronomy.100.q7`: A star far more massive than the Sun forms. Compared with the Sun, what should you expect? → **It burns out sooner and may explode**
- **Fact-check (2026-09-24):** corroborated. Low and intermediate mass stars leave white dwarfs; stars above about 8 solar masses explode as supernovae leaving neutron stars or black holes. Sources: https://www.britannica.com/science/The-Life-Cycle-Of-A-Star, https://www.cfa.harvard.edu/research/topic/neutron-stars-and-white-dwarfs

```sh
npm run verify:record -- fact.astronomy.fate_by_mass source.brit_supernova --status verified --by "Your Name" --quote "…"
```

### ☐ `fact.astronomy.aperture_light`: unverified

> **Claim:** A telescope with a larger mirror or lens collects more light, so it can see fainter objects and finer detail.

- **Concept:** Bigger is better (`concept.astronomy.telescope_aperture`)
- **Appears in:**
  - L33 “How Telescopes Work” · `card.astronomy.033.c1` (text): A telescope is a light bucket. Bigger bucket, more universe. / Catching light is the whole game. The more a telescope catches, the fainter the things it can show you. So how do you build a better bucket?
  - L33 “How Telescopes Work” · `card.astronomy.033.c3` (text): Why size matters / Think of a bucket in the rain: the wider the opening, the more it catches. A wider mirror or lens collects more light, so it can see fainter objects and show finer detail. That's why astronomers keep building bigger. And the giants are all reflectors, because a huge lens sags under its own weight, while a mirror can be supported from behind. Big lenses droop. Big mirrors get a backrest.
  - L35 “Listening to the Sky: Radio Astronomy” · `card.astronomy.035.c3` (text): Why the dishes are huge / Remember the light bucket from Level 33? Radio waves are very long, so seeing fine detail takes an enormous dish. And when one dish isn't enough, astronomers link many dishes into an array that works together like one much larger telescope. Can't build a bigger bucket? Build a team of buckets. / Longer waves → bigger dishes
- **Tested by:**
  - L33 `question.astronomy.033.q3`: From Level 25: Galileo turned his telescopes to the sky in 1609. Why could later, bigger telescopes see much fainter objects? → **Their larger mirrors or lenses collect more light**
  - L35 `question.astronomy.035.q3`: From Level 33: a bigger aperture shows finer detail. How do radio astronomers get an aperture bigger than any single dish? → **By linking many dishes into one array**
- **Fact-check (2026-09-24):** corroborated. A larger aperture gathers more light (fainter objects) and gives higher resolving power (finer detail). Sources: https://www.celestron.com/blogs/knowledgebase/benefits-of-a-bigger-aperture, https://astrobackyard.com/telescope-aperture/

### ☐ `fact.astronomy.big_telescopes_mirrors`: unverified

> **Claim:** The largest telescopes are reflectors, because big lenses sag under their own weight while mirrors can be supported from behind.

- **Concept:** Refractors and reflectors (`concept.astronomy.telescope_types`)
- **Appears in:**
  - L33 “How Telescopes Work” · `card.astronomy.033.c3` (text): Why size matters / Think of a bucket in the rain: the wider the opening, the more it catches. A wider mirror or lens collects more light, so it can see fainter objects and show finer detail. That's why astronomers keep building bigger. And the giants are all reflectors, because a huge lens sags under its own weight, while a mirror can be supported from behind. Big lenses droop. Big mirrors get a backrest.
- **Tested by:**
  - L33 `question.astronomy.033.q2`: Why are the world’s largest telescopes all reflectors? → **Huge lenses sag, but mirrors can be supported from behind**
- **Fact-check (2026-09-24):** corroborated. Large lenses sag and can only be held at their edges; mirrors can be supported across the back, so the largest telescopes are reflectors (largest refractor: Yerkes 40-inch). Sources: https://www.astronomy.ohio-state.edu/pogge.1/Ast161/Au06/Unit4/telescopes.html, https://courses.ems.psu.edu/astro801/content/l3_p7.html

Circles never quite matched them. Ellipses did. His first law: planets orbit the Sun in ellipses, with the Sun at one focus rather than the center. Picture a slightly squashed hoop with the Sun sitting off to one side. Not perfect, just right. / Law 1: orbits are ellipses
  - L26 “Kepler’s Ellipses” · `card.astronomy.026.c1` (text): Planets don’t move in circles. Proving it took the best data of the age. / Copernicus kept perfect circles, and they never quite fit. Fixing that took years of careful sky-watching and one very stubborn mathematician.

### ☐ `fact.astronomy.obs_93`: unverified

> **Claim:** The observable universe is about 93 billion light-years across.

- **Concept:** The observable universe (`concept.astronomy.observable_universe`)
- **Appears in:**

The regions that sent it are now much farther away than 13.8 billion light-years. It’s like getting a postcard from a friend who has moved far away since mailing it. That’s why the observable universe is about 93 billion light-years across. / ≈ 93 billion light-years across
  - L95 “How Big Is the Observable Universe?” · `card.astronomy.095.c1` (text): How can we see farther than light has had time to travel? / The universe is about 13.8 billion years old, yet the part we can see is far wider than 13.8 billion light-years. And no, light didn’t cheat.

- **Fact-check (2026-09-24):** corroborated. The observable universe has a comoving radius of about 46.5 billion light-years, giving a diameter of about 93 billion light-years. ## Vera Rubin (Encyclopaedia Britannica)

- **Source:** `source.brit_vera_rubin`
- **URL:** https://www.britannica.com/biography/Vera-Rubin
- **License:** reference_only · **Accessed:** 2026-09-24 · **Source record checked (URL/licence):** no

### ☐ `fact.astronomy.rotation_curves`: unverified

> **Claim:** Stars at the edges of galaxies orbit so fast that the visible matter alone could not hold them in; extra unseen mass must be present.

- **Concept:** Dark matter (`concept.astronomy.dark_matter`)
- **Appears in:**
  - L85 “Dark Matter” · `card.astronomy.085.c2` (text): Galaxies spin too fast / Remember Kepler from Level 26: the farther a planet is from the Sun, the longer its orbit takes. You'd expect a galaxy to work the same way. But in the 1970s Vera Rubin measured how fast stars orbit in spiral galaxies, and stars far from the center were moving far too fast for the visible matter to hold them. They should have flown off, like mud from a spinning bike wheel. Something unseen had to be adding gravity. / Too fast → hidden mass
  - L97 “Integration: How We Know What We Know” · `card.astronomy.097.c3` (text): New windows / Some discoveries needed brand-new kinds of clues. Stars at galaxies’ edges orbit too fast for the visible matter to hold them, which gave away dark matter, something no one has ever seen. Redshift in galaxies’ light revealed the expanding universe. And in 2015 LIGO first detected gravitational waves from merging black holes: not light at all, but ripples in spacetime. Astronomy learned to feel the universe shake.
  - L85 “Dark Matter” · `card.astronomy.085.c1` (text): Most of the matter in the universe is invisible. / Stars, gas, planets, you: everything you can see is the minority. Astronomers found out because galaxies were breaking the rules.
- **Tested by:**
  - L85 `question.astronomy.085.q2`: What did Vera Rubin find about stars far from the centers of spiral galaxies? → **They orbit much faster than visible matter can explain**
  - L85 `question.astronomy.085.q3`: From Level 26: planets far from the Sun orbit more slowly. Why was it surprising that outer stars in galaxies don’t slow down? → **It means far more mass is present than we can see**
  - L90 `question.astronomy.090.q3`: A galaxy’s outer stars orbit much faster than its visible matter can explain. What is the most likely reason? → **Unseen dark matter is adding gravity**
  - L97 `question.astronomy.097.q3`: From Level 85: no one has ever seen dark matter. What evidence convinced astronomers it exists? → **Stars orbiting faster than visible mass allows**
- **Fact-check (2026-09-24):** corroborated. Flat rotation curves show outer stars moving as fast as inner ones; visible mass could not hold them, implying unseen (dark) matter. Sources: https://www.astronomy.com/science/how-vera-rubin-confirmed-dark-matter/, https://womenshistory.si.edu/blog/new-quarter-honors-vera-rubin-astronomer-who-revealed-universes-hidden-mass


## White dwarf star (Encyclopaedia Britannica)

### ☐ `fact.astronomy.most_stars_white_dwarfs`: unverified

> **Claim:** Most stars in the Milky Way, including the Sun, will end their lives as white dwarfs.

- **Concept:** White dwarfs (`concept.astronomy.white_dwarfs`)
- **Appears in:**
  - L68 “White Dwarfs” · `card.astronomy.068.c3` (text): Slowly fading / A white dwarf no longer fuses anything. It shines only with leftover heat, like an oven after it’s switched off, and slowly cools over billions of years. That’s why it sits in the lower left of the H–R diagram: hot, but dim. A famous example is Sirius B, the faint partner of Sirius, the brightest star in the night sky. It’s also the most common ending: most stars in the Milky Way, the Sun included, will finish as white dwarfs.
- **Tested by:**
  - L68 `question.astronomy.068.q2`: Why does a white dwarf slowly get dimmer? → **It no longer fuses, so it just cools off**
  - L68 `question.astronomy.068.q3`: From Level 65: which famous star has a white dwarf as its companion? → **Sirius, the brightest star in the night sky**
- **Pre-check:** Often quoted as ~97%. The card avoids a number.
- **Fact-check (2026-09-24):** corroborated. White dwarfs are the end state of stars below about 8 solar masses, which include the Sun and over 97% of Milky Way stars. Sources: https://www.space.com/23756-white-dwarf-stars.html, https://www.lunduniversity.lu.se/article/researchers-map-movement-white-dwarfs-milky-way

### ☐ `fact.astronomy.white_dwarf_dense`: unverified

> **Claim:** White dwarf material is so dense that a teaspoonful would weigh several tons on Earth.

- **Concept:** White dwarfs (`concept.astronomy.white_dwarfs`)

  - L68 “White Dwarfs” · `card.astronomy.068.c1` (text): A teaspoon of this star would weigh several tons. / When the planetary nebula from Level 67 drifts away, something is left behind. It’s small, it’s hot, and it’s astonishingly heavy for its size.
  - L68 “White Dwarfs” · `card.astronomy.068.c2` (text): A star-sized core in an Earth-sized ball / What’s left is the star’s exposed core: a white dwarf. It’s about the size of Earth, but with a mass comparable to the Sun’s. Picture roughly a star’s worth of matter packed into a ball the size of our planet. The result is absurdly dense: a teaspoonful would weigh several tons on Earth. Your kitchen scale would not survive the experiment. / Earth-sized · Sun-massive
  - L71 “Neutron Stars” · `card.astronomy.071.c3` (text): Crushed to neutrons / How do you fit a sun into a city? Gravity crushes the collapsing core so hard that its matter becomes mostly neutrons, packed tightly together. Remember Level 68’s white dwarf, where a teaspoon weighed several tons? A teaspoon of neutron star would weigh billions of tons on Earth. Your sugar spoon has never worked so hard. And many neutron stars spin many times per second, with magnetic fields far stronger than Earth’s. Hold on to that spin.
  - L80 “Checkpoint: Extreme Objects and Other Worlds” · `card.astronomy.080.c2` (comparison): What’s left when a star dies: White dwarf (About the size of Earth; A teaspoon: several tons) vs Neutron star (About the size of a city; A teaspoon: billions of tons) vs Black hole (No surface at all; Only an event horizon, the point of no return)

  - L68 `question.astronomy.068.q1`: How big is a typical white dwarf? → **About the size of Earth**

  - L71 `question.astronomy.071.q3`: From Level 68: a white dwarf teaspoon weighs several tons. How does a neutron star compare? → **A teaspoon weighs billions of tons**

### ☐ `fact.astronomy.eht_2019`: unverified

> **Claim:** In 2019 the Event Horizon Telescope released the first image of a black hole, the one at the center of the galaxy M87.

- **Concept:** The event horizon (`concept.astronomy.event_horizon`)
- **Appears in:**
  - L74 “The Event Horizon” · `card.astronomy.074.c1` (text): In 2019, humanity saw a black hole for the first time. Sort of. / How do you photograph something that lets no light out? It took a telescope as wide as the planet.

The Event Horizon Telescope pushes both to the limit. It links radio telescopes around the world so they work like one Earth-sized telescope. In 2019 it released the first image of a black hole, the one at the center of the galaxy M87. You can’t build a dish the size of a planet, so astronomers borrowed the planet.

- **Fact-check (2026-09-24):** corroborated. EHT press release of 10 April 2019 announced the first image of a black hole, the supermassive black hole at the center of galaxy M87. Sources: https://eventhorizontelescope.org/press-release-april-10-2019-astronomers-capture-first-image-black-hole, https://news.mit.edu/2019/eht-astronomers-direct-image-black-hole-0410

## What are Gravitational Waves? (LIGO Caltech)

  - L97 “Integration: How We Know What We Know” · `card.astronomy.097.c3` (text): New windows / Some discoveries needed brand-new kinds of clues. ### ☐ `fact.astronomy.apollo_footprints`: unverified

> **Claim:** Astronauts’ footprints on the Moon can last for a very long time because the Moon has no wind or rain to wear them away.

- **Concept:** The Apollo Moon landings (`concept.astronomy.apollo_landings`)
- **Appears in:**
  - L39 “Humans on the Moon” · `card.astronomy.039.c4` (fact): The last Moonwalkers left in December 1972. / They left their footprints behind. The Moon has no wind and no rain to wear footprints away, so those tracks can last a very long time. Nobody up there had to wipe their feet.
- **Pre-check:** Confirm on the page, or cite the Moon facts page (no atmosphere/weather).
- **Fact-check (2026-09-24):** corroborated. The Moon has no atmosphere, so there is no wind or water erosion; bootprints are expected to last millions of years, worn down only slowly by micrometeorites. Sources: https://www.space.com/14740-footprints-moon.html, https://www.nbcnews.com/news/amp/wbna44421662

### ☐ `fact.astronomy.apollo_samples`: unverified

Apollo brought home 382 kg of Moon rocks and soil, and scientists study them to learn how the Moon formed. Their big clue: chemically, Moon rocks are very similar to rocks from Earth's mantle. Remember the giant impact from Level 18? The Moon seems to be made of Earth's own stuff. The best souvenir ever turned out to be evidence.

### ☐ `fact.astronomy.asteroid_composition`: unverified

> **Claim:** Most asteroids are made of rock, and some are made largely of metal.

- **Concept:** The asteroid belt (`concept.astronomy.asteroid_belt`)
- **Appears in:**
  - L45 “The Asteroid Belt” · `card.astronomy.045.c2` (text): The planet that never happened / The main asteroid belt lies between the orbits of Mars and Jupiter. Most asteroids are rock; some are largely metal. It's building material that never got built. Blame the neighbor. Remember from Level 4 that Jupiter is more than twice as massive as all the other planets combined? Its strong gravity stirred up the material there and kept it from ever gathering into a planet. Hard to build a sandcastle while someone keeps kicking it.
  - L52 “The Kuiper Belt” · `card.astronomy.052.c1` (text): Past Neptune, the solar system keeps going, into the deep freeze. / Pluto isn't out there on its own. It's one member of a whole second belt, bigger than the one between Mars and Jupiter and made of ice instead of rock.
  - L52 “The Kuiper Belt” · `card.astronomy.052.c2` (text): A second belt / The Kuiper Belt is a doughnut-shaped region of icy bodies beyond the orbit of Neptune. Think of it as the asteroid belt's big cousin who lives in the freezer. The asteroid belt is mostly rock; the Kuiper Belt is mostly ice, and it's about 20 times as wide. Pluto is one of its largest known members, which explains Level 51: Pluto orbits among many other icy bodies of its own zone. It also sends us visitors. Many comets that return every few years are thought to come from here and the scattered disk just beyond.
- **Tested by:**
  - L45 `question.astronomy.045.q1`: Where is the main asteroid belt? → **Between Mars and Jupiter**
  - L45 `question.astronomy.045.q2`: Why didn’t the asteroid belt form a planet? → **Jupiter’s gravity kept stirring it up**
  - L45 `question.astronomy.045.q3`: From Level 4: Jupiter outweighs all the other planets combined. How does that connect to the asteroid belt? → **Its huge gravity kept the belt’s material from forming a planet**
  - L52 `question.astronomy.052.q2`: What is the biggest difference between the Kuiper Belt and the asteroid belt? → **The Kuiper Belt is mostly ice; the asteroid belt is mostly rock**
  - L60 `question.astronomy.060.q5`: Chapter 6 recap: which list goes from nearest to farthest from the Sun? → **Asteroid belt → Kuiper Belt → Oort Cloud**
- **Fact-check (2026-09-24):** corroborated. Most asteroids are C-type (clay and silicate rock) or S-type (stony); M-type asteroids are mostly metallic iron and nickel. Sources: https://science.nasa.gov/solar-system/asteroids/facts/, https://www.planetary.org/articles/what-are-asteroids-made-of

### ☐ `fact.astronomy.belt_empty`: unverified

> **Claim:** The asteroid belt is mostly empty space; spacecraft have crossed it without hitting anything.

- **Concept:** The asteroid belt (`concept.astronomy.asteroid_belt`)
- **Appears in:**
  - L45 “The Asteroid Belt” · `card.astronomy.045.c1` (text): The asteroid belt isn’t like the movies. It’s mostly empty. / Forget the movie scene where a ship dodges tumbling boulders. Spacecraft have flown straight through the belt without hitting anything. So what is it really, and why is it there?
  - L45 “The Asteroid Belt” · `card.astronomy.045.c3` (text): Less than a Moon / And there isn't much of it. All the asteroids in the main belt together have less mass than Earth's Moon. - **Fact-check (2026-09-24):** corroborated. Asteroids are on average hundreds of thousands of km apart; Pioneer 10, Voyagers, Cassini, New Horizons, Juno and others crossed the belt without collisions. Sources: https://www.astronomy.com/science/how-do-spacecraft-safely-navigate-the-asteroid-belt/, https://www.skyatnightmagazine.com/space-missions/spaceship-flying-through-asteroid-belt

## Black Holes (NASA Science)

- **Source:** `source.nasa_black_holes`
- **URL:** https://science.nasa.gov/universe/black-holes/
- **License:** US_gov_public_domain · **Accessed:** 2026-09-24 · **Source record checked (URL/licence):** no

### ☐ `fact.astronomy.black_hole_definition`: unverified

> **Claim:** A black hole is a region of space where gravity is so strong that nothing, not even light, can escape.

- **Concept:** Black holes (`concept.astronomy.black_holes`)
- **Appears in:**
  - L73 “Black Holes” · `card.astronomy.073.c1` (text): A place where escape velocity is faster than light. / Level 37 said you need about 11.2 km per second to escape Earth. Now picture a place where even light, the fastest thing there is, isn’t fast enough.
  - L73 “Black Holes” · `card.astronomy.073.c2` (text): Gravity wins / A black hole is a region of space where gravity is so strong that nothing, not even light, can escape. In Level 37’s terms, its escape velocity is faster than the speed of light, and nothing can go that fast. That’s why it’s black: no light comes out for you to see. Stellar black holes form when the core of a very massive star collapses at the end of its life. Same kind of ending as a neutron star, with an even stranger leftover.
  - L87 “Quasars and Active Galaxies” · `card.astronomy.087.c1` (text): Some of the brightest objects in the universe are powered by black holes. / Black holes are famous for trapping light. So how can one sit behind some of the brightest things in the universe? The secret is in how it eats.
  - L87 “Quasars and Active Galaxies” · `card.astronomy.087.c3` (text): Brighter than a galaxy / How bright? A feeding black hole's glowing disk can outshine all the stars of its galaxy combined. The brightest of these are called quasars, and they're among the most distant and luminous objects known. One hot disk of gas beats a whole galaxy of stars. The darkest objects in the universe run some of its brightest lights. / Feeding black hole → quasar
  - L73 “Black Holes” · `card.astronomy.073.c3` (text): Not a cosmic vacuum cleaner / Black holes have a reputation for slurping up everything nearby. They don’t deserve it. From a safe distance, a black hole’s gravity is the same as that of any object with the same mass. Swap the Sun for a black hole of equal mass and the planets would keep orbiting just as they do now. Your days would get a lot darker, but Earth’s path wouldn’t budge. / Same mass → same pull at a distance
  - L74 “The Event Horizon” · `card.astronomy.074.c1` (text): In 2019, humanity saw a black hole for the first time. Sort of. / How do you photograph something that lets no light out? It took a telescope as wide as the planet.
- **Tested by:**
  - L73 `question.astronomy.073.q1`: What makes a black hole “black”? → **Nothing, not even light, can escape its gravity**
  - L73 `question.astronomy.073.q2`: If the Sun were replaced by a black hole of the same mass, what would happen to Earth’s orbit? → **It would stay the same**
  - L73 `question.astronomy.073.q3`: From Level 37: escape velocity is the speed needed to break free of gravity. What is true at a black hole? → **Its escape velocity exceeds the speed of light**
  - L80 `question.astronomy.080.q3`: Why can’t a black hole itself be photographed directly? → **No light escapes from inside its event horizon**
- **Fact-check (2026-09-24):** corroborated. NASA: a black hole has gravity so strong that nothing, not even light, can escape its event horizon. Sources: https://science.nasa.gov/universe/black-holes/, https://www.nasa.gov/universe/what-are-black-holes/

```sh
npm run verify:record -- fact.astronomy.black_hole_definition source.nasa_black_holes --status verified --by "Your Name" --quote "…"
```

- **Appears in:**
  - L73 “Black Holes” · `card.astronomy.073.c2` (text): Gravity wins / A black hole is a region of space where gravity is so strong that nothing, not even light, can escape. In Level 37’s terms, its escape velocity is faster than the speed of light, and nothing can go that fast. That’s why it’s black: no light comes out for you to see. Stellar black holes form when the core of a very massive star collapses at the end of its life. Same kind of ending as a neutron star, with an even stranger leftover.

### ☐ `fact.astronomy.black_hole_not_vacuum`: unverified

> **Claim:** From a safe distance, a black hole’s gravity is the same as that of any object with the same mass; it does not suck in everything around it.

- **Concept:** Black holes (`concept.astronomy.black_holes`)
- **Appears in:**
  - L73 “Black Holes” · `card.astronomy.073.c3` (text): Not a cosmic vacuum cleaner / Black holes have a reputation for slurping up everything nearby. They don’t deserve it. From a safe distance, a black hole’s gravity is the same as that of any object with the same mass. Swap the Sun for a black hole of equal mass and the planets would keep orbiting just as they do now. Your days would get a lot darker, but Earth’s path wouldn’t budge. / Same mass → same pull at a distance
- **Tested by:**
  - L73 `question.astronomy.073.q2`: If the Sun were replaced by a black hole of the same mass, what would happen to Earth’s orbit? → **It would stay the same**
- **Fact-check (2026-09-24):** corroborated. NASA: black holes do not suck in matter; from far enough away their gravitational effects are like those of any object of the same mass. ### ☐ `fact.astronomy.event_horizon_def`: unverified

> **Claim:** A black hole’s event horizon is the boundary beyond which nothing, not even light, can escape.

- **Concept:** The event horizon (`concept.astronomy.event_horizon`)
- **Appears in:**
  - L74 “The Event Horizon” · `card.astronomy.074.c2` (text): The point of no return / A black hole has an edge, but it isn’t a surface. It’s the event horizon: the boundary beyond which nothing, not even light, can escape. Outside it, light and matter can still get away. Cross it, and there is no way back. Think of a one-way door with no handle on the inside.
  - L80 “Checkpoint: Extreme Objects and Other Worlds” · `card.astronomy.080.c2` (comparison): What’s left when a star dies: White dwarf (About the size of Earth; A teaspoon: several tons) vs Neutron star (About the size of a city; A teaspoon: billions of tons) vs Black hole (No surface at all; Only an event horizon, the point of no return)
  - L87 “Quasars and Active Galaxies” · `card.astronomy.087.c2` (text): Monsters at the center / Most large galaxies have a supermassive black hole at their center. When one pulls in large amounts of gas, the gas spirals in, heats up in a swirling disk and glows fiercely, all before it crosses the event horizon. Nothing escapes from inside, so the light comes from the gas, not the black hole. It's the meal that glows, not the diner.
- **Tested by:**
  - L74 `question.astronomy.074.q1`: What is a black hole’s event horizon? → **The boundary beyond which nothing can escape**
  - L80 `question.astronomy.080.q5`: Chapter 8 recap: which stellar remnant has no surface at all? → **A black hole**
- **Fact-check (2026-09-24):** corroborated. NASA describes the event horizon as the boundary inside which escape velocity exceeds light speed, so nothing, not even light, escapes. Sources: https://science.nasa.gov/universe/black-holes/anatomy/, https://www.britannica.com/topic/event-horizon-black-hole

  - L51 “Pluto and the Dwarf Planets” · `card.astronomy.051.c3` (text): Five dwarf planets / Pluto has company. The IAU recognizes five dwarf planets: Ceres, Pluto, Eris, Haumea and Makemake. ### ☐ `fact.astronomy.comet_coma_tails`: unverified

- **Concept:** Comets (`concept.astronomy.comets`)
- **Appears in:**

Many of the ones that return every few years are thought to come from the Kuiper Belt you met in Level 52. Far from the Sun, a comet is just that: a dirty snowball. As one nears the Sun, its ice turns to gas, forming a glowing cloud called a coma and long tails. A comet only puts on a show when it gets close enough for its ice to start turning to gas.

### ☐ `fact.astronomy.comet_tails_away`: unverified

> **Claim:** A comet’s tails always point away from the Sun, pushed by sunlight and the solar wind.

- **Concept:** Comets (`concept.astronomy.comets`)
- **Appears in:**
  - L53 “Comets: Dirty Snowballs” · `card.astronomy.053.c1` (text): A comet’s tail can point ahead of it. / Picture a comet and you probably see a tail streaming out behind it, like hair in the wind. Sometimes that tail is out in front. The comet doesn't get a vote: the Sun decides which way it points.
  - L53 “Comets: Dirty Snowballs” · `card.astronomy.053.c3` (text): Tails that point away / Sunlight and the solar wind push a comet's tails away from the Sun, always. So a tail isn't exhaust trailing behind the comet. It's more like a windsock, and the Sun is the wind. On the way in, the tail trails behind. On the way back out, it leads the way. Many comets actually have two tails: one of dust and one of charged gas. / Tails point away from the Sun
- **Tested by:**
  - L53 `question.astronomy.053.q2`: Which way does a comet’s tail point? → **Always away from the Sun**
- **Fact-check (2026-09-24):** corroborated. The ion tail is pushed by the solar wind and the dust tail by radiation pressure, so tails point away from the Sun (the dust tail curves somewhat). Sources: https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Comet_tail, https://lasp.colorado.edu/outerplanets/kbos_comets.php

Dry river valleys and ancient lake beds show that liquid water once flowed there. Then we sent a robot to check in person. NASA's Curiosity rover, which landed in 2012, found that Gale Crater once held a lake. Picture a crater as dry as a parking lot that used to be a place you could have gone for a swim. / Curiosity, 2012: Gale Crater once held a lake

If you want to know what an old lake left behind, you go and pick up its stones.

### ☐ `fact.astronomy.dark_matter_invisible`: unverified

> **Claim:** Dark matter does not give off, absorb or reflect light; it is detected only through its gravity.

- **Concept:** Dark matter (`concept.astronomy.dark_matter`)
- **Appears in:**
  - L85 “Dark Matter” · `card.astronomy.085.c4` (fact): Dark matter doesn't shine, absorb or reflect light. / No glow, no shadow, no reflection. We detect it only through its gravity, the way you notice wind by watching the trees bend. What it's actually made of is still unknown. Most of the matter in the universe, and nobody knows what it is.
  - L86 “Galaxy Clusters and Gravitational Lenses” · `card.astronomy.086.c4` (fact): Most of a cluster's lens is invisible. / With roughly five times as much dark matter as ordinary matter, most of the mass bending that light gives off, absorbs and reflects no light at all. So the lens is mostly something we can't see. Astronomers are peering through an invisible magnifying glass.
  - L94 “Dark Energy” · `card.astronomy.094.c3` (text): Dark energy / Whatever is pressing the accelerator has a name, dark energy, and that’s about all we have: nobody knows what it is. Don’t mix it up with dark matter, which adds gravity. Dark energy speeds up the expansion. Yet dark energy makes up roughly 68% of the universe’s energy content, and dark matter about 27%. Everything made of ordinary atoms, every star, planet and person, is only about 5%. We’re the sprinkles on a very mysterious cake. / 68% · 27% · 5%
  - L97 “Integration: How We Know What We Know” · `card.astronomy.097.c3` (text): New windows / Some discoveries needed brand-new kinds of clues. Stars at galaxies’ edges orbit too fast for the visible matter to hold them, which gave away dark matter, something no one has ever seen. Redshift in galaxies’ light revealed the expanding universe. And in 2015 LIGO first detected gravitational waves from merging black holes: not light at all, but ripples in spacetime. Astronomy learned to feel the universe shake.

  - L97 `question.astronomy.097.q3`: From Level 85: no one has ever seen dark matter. What evidence convinced astronomers it exists? → **Stars orbiting faster than visible mass allows**
- **Fact-check (2026-09-24):** corroborated. NASA: dark matter does not emit, reflect or absorb light; scientists detect it through its gravity, e.g. gravitational lensing.   - L86 “Galaxy Clusters and Gravitational Lenses” · `card.astronomy.086.c4` (fact): Most of a cluster's lens is invisible. / With roughly five times as much dark matter as ordinary matter, most of the mass bending that light gives off, absorbs and reflects no light at all. And we're not at the top yet. / Galaxy → group → cluster
  - L85 “Dark Matter” · `card.astronomy.085.c4` (fact): Dark matter doesn't shine, absorb or reflect light. / No glow, no shadow, no reflection. ### ☐ `fact.astronomy.rotation_curves`: unverified

> **Claim:** Stars at the edges of galaxies orbit so fast that the visible matter alone could not hold them in; extra unseen mass must be present.

- **Concept:** Dark matter (`concept.astronomy.dark_matter`)
- **Appears in:**
  - L85 “Dark Matter” · `card.astronomy.085.c2` (text): Galaxies spin too fast / Remember Kepler from Level 26: the farther a planet is from the Sun, the longer its orbit takes. You'd expect a galaxy to work the same way. But in the 1970s Vera Rubin measured how fast stars orbit in spiral galaxies, and stars far from the center were moving far too fast for the visible matter to hold them. They should have flown off, like mud from a spinning bike wheel. Something unseen had to be adding gravity. / Too fast → hidden mass
  - L97 “Integration: How We Know What We Know” · `card.astronomy.097.c3` (text): New windows / Some discoveries needed brand-new kinds of clues. Stars at galaxies’ edges orbit too fast for the visible matter to hold them, which gave away dark matter, something no one has ever seen. Redshift in galaxies’ light revealed the expanding universe. And in 2015 LIGO first detected gravitational waves from merging black holes: not light at all, but ripples in spacetime. Astronomy learned to feel the universe shake.
  - L85 “Dark Matter” · `card.astronomy.085.c1` (text): Most of the matter in the universe is invisible. / Stars, gas, planets, you: everything you can see is the minority. Astronomers found out because galaxies were breaking the rules.
- **Tested by:**
  - L85 `question.astronomy.085.q2`: What did Vera Rubin find about stars far from the centers of spiral galaxies? → **They orbit much faster than visible matter can explain**
  - L85 `question.astronomy.085.q3`: From Level 26: planets far from the Sun orbit more slowly. Why was it surprising that outer stars in galaxies don’t slow down? → **It means far more mass is present than we can see**
  - L90 `question.astronomy.090.q3`: A galaxy’s outer stars orbit much faster than its visible matter can explain. What is the most likely reason? → **Unseen dark matter is adding gravity**
  - L97 `question.astronomy.097.q3`: From Level 85: no one has ever seen dark matter. What evidence convinced astronomers it exists? → **Stars orbiting faster than visible mass allows**
- **Fact-check (2026-09-24):** corroborated. Flat rotation curves show outer stars moving as fast as inner ones; visible mass could not hold them, implying unseen (dark) matter. Sources: https://www.astronomy.com/science/how-vera-rubin-confirmed-dark-matter/, https://womenshistory.si.edu/blog/new-quarter-honors-vera-rubin-astronomer-who-revealed-universes-hidden-mass

### ☐ `fact.astronomy.auroras_poles`: unverified

> **Claim:** Charged particles from the Sun that reach the upper atmosphere near the poles make the sky glow as auroras.

- **Concept:** What protects life on Earth (`concept.astronomy.earth_protection`)
- **Appears in:**
  - L43 “Earth: The Planet We Know Best” · `card.astronomy.043.c2` (text): A magnetic shield / The first layer starts deep underground. Molten iron moving in Earth's outer core generates a magnetic field. That field deflects most of the charged particles streaming from the Sun, like an umbrella in sideways rain. A few slip through near the poles, where they reach the upper atmosphere and make the sky glow as auroras. The northern lights are the armor showing where it gets hit.
  - L58 “Solar Storms and Auroras” · `card.astronomy.058.c3` (text): When storms arrive / Earth carries an umbrella: its magnetic field deflects most of those particles (Level 43). Without it, far more would reach the atmosphere directly. The few that get through near the poles reach the upper atmosphere, and the sky there glows as auroras. Strong storms can also disrupt satellites, radio communications and power grids. The prettiest weather on Earth has a mean streak.
  - L58 “Solar Storms and Auroras” · `card.astronomy.058.c1` (text): The Sun has weather, and it reaches all the way to us. / Not rain or snow: a wind made of charged particles, plus the occasional storm that can light up your sky or knock out your power.
  - L58 “Solar Storms and Auroras” · `card.astronomy.058.c4` (fact): The Carrington Event of 1859 is the strongest solar storm on record. / It produced auroras seen far from the poles, well outside their usual home. Now picture a storm like that hitting today's world of satellites, radio and power grids. Strong storms can disrupt all three.
- **Tested by:**
  - L43 `question.astronomy.043.q2`: What generates Earth’s magnetic field? → **Molten iron moving in Earth’s outer core**
  - L58 `question.astronomy.058.q2`: Why are auroras usually seen near the poles? → **Solar particles slip past Earth’s magnetic shield near the poles**
  - L58 `question.astronomy.058.q3`: From Level 43: what would happen to more of the solar wind’s particles without Earth’s magnetic field? → **They would reach Earth’s atmosphere directly**
- **Pre-check:** Confirm on the Earth facts page or attach a NASA aurora page.
- **Fact-check (2026-09-24):** corroborated. NASA and UCAR explain that solar charged particles, channeled by Earth's magnetic field, strike the upper atmosphere near the poles and make gases glow as auroras. Sources: https://www.earthobservatory.nasa.gov/images/152063/vibrant-auroras, https://scied.ucar.edu/learning-zone/sun-space-weather/aurora

### ☐ `fact.astronomy.earth_spins_eastward`: unverified

> **Claim:** Earth rotates from west to east, so the Sun, Moon, planets and stars appear to rise in the east and set in the west.

- **Concept:** Why everything rises in the east (`concept.astronomy.sky_rises_east`)
- **Appears in:**
  - L11 “Why the Sky Turns” · `card.astronomy.011.c1` (text): The sky isn't turning. You are. / Every sunrise looks like the Sun climbing up to meet you. It isn't going anywhere. Your patch of Earth is rolling toward it, and all night long the stars play the very same trick.
  - L11 “Why the Sky Turns” · `card.astronomy.011.c3` (text): Why everything rises in the east / Earth spins from west to east, so your eastern horizon turns toward the Sun first. From where you stand, the Sun seems to climb up in the east and sink in the west. The Moon, the planets and the stars do exactly the same, for exactly the same reason. They aren't parading around us. Earth's spin sweeps them all across the sky the same way, like scenery sliding past a train window.
  - L20 “Checkpoint: Reading the Sky” · `card.astronomy.020.c2` (text): Spin, orbit, pull / Earth's spin gives day and night and sweeps the Sun and stars from east to west. Earth's orbit gives the year and a night sky that changes with the seasons. The Moon, circling us, brings phases, eclipses and, with its gravity, tides. And the planets wander along the ecliptic, with Mars looping back whenever Earth overtakes it. A few motions, one very busy sky. / Spin → day · Orbit → year · Moon → tides
  - L21 “The First Sky-Watchers” · `card.astronomy.021.c4` (fact): Polynesian navigators crossed thousands of kilometers of open Pacific Ocean by reading the sky. / They knew the points on the horizon where particular stars rise and set, and steered by them, with ocean swells and wildlife as backup clues. Stars rise in the east because Earth spins (Level 11), so a known star coming up over the water points the way. The whole night sky, used as a compass.
  - L42 “Venus: Earth’s Evil Twin” · `card.astronomy.042.c3` (text): Backwards and slow / Earth spins from west to east, which is why our Sun rises in the east. Venus spins backwards compared with most planets, so there the Sun rises in the west. It's also in no hurry. One rotation takes 243 Earth days, but one trip around the Sun takes only 225. It turns so slowly that it finishes an orbit before it finishes a single spin. That's how a day ends up longer than a year. / Day: 243 Earth days · Year: 225
- **Tested by:**
  - L11 `question.astronomy.011.q2`: Why does the Sun rise in the east? → **Earth spins eastward, turning the east toward the Sun first**
  - L11 `question.astronomy.011.q3`: From Level 1, stars are distant suns. Why do they rise in the east and set in the west, just like the Sun? → **Earth’s spin sweeps them all across the sky the same way**
  - L14 `question.astronomy.014.q3`: From Level 11: most stars rise in the east and set in the west. Why don’t the circumpolar stars set? → **Their circles around the pole stay above the horizon**
  - L21 `question.astronomy.021.q3`: From Level 11: why could Polynesian navigators use a star’s rising point on the horizon to steer? → **Earth’s spin brings each star up in the east the same way every night**
  - L42 `question.astronomy.042.q3`: From Level 11: on Earth the Sun rises in the east because Earth spins west to east. What does a western sunrise on Venus tell you? → **Venus spins the opposite way to Earth**
- **Fact-check (2026-09-24):** corroborated. Earth rotates west to east, so Sun, Moon and stars appear to rise in the east and set in the west. Sources: https://www.ck12.org/flexi/earth-science/earths-rotations/due-to-earth's-rotation-the-sun-moon-and-the-stars-appear-to-rise-in-the-east-and-set-in-the-west/, https://griffithobservatory.lacity.gov/exhibits/ahmanson-hall-of-the-sky/sun-stars-paths/

### ☐ `fact.astronomy.earth_year_length`: unverified

> **Claim:** Earth takes about 365.25 days to orbit the Sun once; that orbit is a year.

- **Concept:** A year is one orbit (`concept.astronomy.earth_orbit_year`)
- **Appears in:**
  - L12 “A Year Around the Sun” · `card.astronomy.012.c1` (text): A year is just one lap. / Every birthday, you've ridden once around the Sun since the last one. The candles on your cake are really a lap counter.
  - L12 “A Year Around the Sun” · `card.astronomy.012.c2` (text): One lap, one year / Earth takes about 365.25 days to orbit the Sun once, and that lap is what we call a year. It's no gentle stroll, either. Earth races along its orbit at about 30 km every second. Count to ten and you've traveled about 300 km through space, without ever leaving your chair. / Orbit = year · Spin = day
  - L20 “Checkpoint: Reading the Sky” · `card.astronomy.020.c2` (text): Spin, orbit, pull / Earth's spin gives day and night and sweeps the Sun and stars from east to west. Earth's orbit gives the year and a night sky that changes with the seasons. The Moon, circling us, brings phases, eclipses and, with its gravity, tides. And the planets wander along the ecliptic, with Mars looping back whenever Earth overtakes it. A few motions, one very busy sky. / Spin → day · Orbit → year · Moon → tides
  - L61 “Parallax: Measuring the Stars” · `card.astronomy.061.c2` (text): Earth is the head that moves / Your eyes see your finger from two spots a few centimeters apart. Astronomers use two spots on Earth’s orbit around the Sun. Look at a nearby star, then look again half a year later from the far side of the orbit, and the star seems to shift slightly against the more distant stars behind it. That shift is called parallax. The star didn’t move. You did.

- **Fact-check (2026-09-24):** corroborated. Earth takes roughly 365.25 days (365.2422) to orbit the Sun, which is why leap years exist. Sources: https://spaceplace.nasa.gov/leap-year/en/, https://www.gatech.edu/news/2024/02/23/leap-years-explained

### ☐ `fact.astronomy.perihelion_january`: unverified

> **Claim:** Earth is closest to the Sun in early January.

- **Concept:** Seasons aren't about distance (`concept.astronomy.seasons_not_distance`)
- **Appears in:**
  - L5 “Why We Have Seasons” · `card.astronomy.005.c1` (text): Most people get this one wrong: summer isn't when Earth is closest to the Sun. / It's the obvious guess: summer is hot because Earth swings closer to the Sun. Nice guess. The calendar disagrees.
  - L5 “Why We Have Seasons” · `card.astronomy.005.c2` (fact): Earth is closest to the Sun in early January. / That's the middle of winter in the Northern Hemisphere. If distance ran the seasons, New Year's Day would be beach weather in New York. Instead, northern summer arrives when Earth is farther from the Sun. So distance can't be what makes summer. Something else is in charge.
  - L26 “Kepler’s Ellipses” · `card.astronomy.026.c3` (text): Faster when closer / His second law says a line from the Sun to a planet sweeps out equal areas in equal times. In plain terms: a planet moves fastest when it's closest to the Sun and slowest when farthest, like a skateboarder who's quickest at the bottom of the ramp. Earth is closest in early January (Level 5), so that's when it moves fastest. His third law adds that the farther out a planet orbits, the longer its year. / Law 2: faster near the Sun · Law 3: farther = longer year
- **Tested by:**
  - L5 `question.astronomy.005.q3`: Venus is hotter than Mercury (Level 3), and northern summer happens when Earth is farther from the Sun. What do both show? → **Distance alone doesn’t set temperature**
  - L26 `question.astronomy.026.q3`: From Level 5: Earth is closest to the Sun in early January. By Kepler’s second law, what follows? → **Earth moves fastest along its orbit in January**
  - L100 `question.astronomy.100.q2`: Earth is closest to the Sun in early January. Why is January winter in the Northern Hemisphere? → **The north is tilted away from the Sun then**
- **Pre-check:** Source is the Earth facts page; confirm it states perihelion is in early January (about Jan 3–5).
- **Fact-check (2026-09-24):** corroborated. Earth reaches perihelion around January 3 each year (range roughly January 2 to 5). Sources: https://earthsky.org/tonight/earth-comes-closest-to-sun-every-year-in-early-january/, https://apod.nasa.gov/apod/ap250104.html

> **Claim:** Because Earth also moves along its orbit, each star rises about 4 minutes earlier each night, adding up to a different night sky over the year.

- **Fact-check (2026-09-24):** corroborated. Because Earth moves about 1 degree per day in its orbit, the sidereal day is ~23 h 56 min and stars rise about 4 minutes earlier each night, shifting the visible sky over the year.   - L7 “Eclipses and a Cosmic Coincidence” · `card.astronomy.007.c2` (comparison): Two kinds of eclipse: who's blocking whom?: Solar eclipse (The Moon passes between the Sun and Earth; The Moon's shadow falls on Earth; Only happens at new Moon, when the Moon sits on the Sun's side of us) vs Lunar eclipse (Earth passes between the Sun and Moon; Earth's shadow falls on the Moon; Only happens at full Moon, when the Moon is on the far side of us)
  - L22 “Eratosthenes Measures the Earth” · `card.astronomy.022.c4` (fact): Ancient Greek thinkers such as Aristotle already had evidence that Earth is round. / Remember lunar eclipses from Level 7? Earth's shadow falls on the Moon, and its edge is always curved. Only a sphere casts a curved shadow every time, from every angle. So Eratosthenes could start from a round Earth and ask the next question: how big?
- **Tested by:**
  - L7 `question.astronomy.007.q1`: During a lunar eclipse, what is happening? → **Earth's shadow falls on the Moon**
  - L7 `question.astronomy.007.q3`: From Level 6: why can a lunar eclipse only happen at full Moon? → **Only then does Earth sit between the Sun and Moon**
  - L15 `question.astronomy.015.q3`: From Level 7: there’s a new Moon every month. Why isn’t there a solar eclipse every month? → **The Moon usually passes just above or below the Sun’s path**
  - L22 `question.astronomy.022.q3`: From Level 7: during a lunar eclipse, why does Earth’s curved shadow on the Moon show that Earth is round? → **Only a sphere casts a curved shadow every time, from every angle**
- **Fact-check (2026-09-24):** corroborated. NASA: a lunar eclipse happens when Earth lies between the Sun and Moon and Earth's shadow falls on the Moon. Sources: https://science.nasa.gov/moon/eclipses/, https://spaceplace.nasa.gov/eclipses/en/

Earth's orbit gives the year and a night sky that changes with the seasons. The Moon, circling us, brings phases, eclipses and, with its gravity, tides. And the planets wander along the ecliptic, with Mars looping back whenever Earth overtakes it. A few motions, one very busy sky. / Spin → day · Orbit → year · Moon → tides

### ☐ `fact.astronomy.sun_moon_400`: unverified

> **Claim:** The Sun is about 400 times wider than the Moon and about 400 times farther away, so both look about the same size in our sky.

- **Concept:** The 400× coincidence (`concept.astronomy.eclipse_coincidence`)
- **Appears in:**
  - L7 “Eclipses and a Cosmic Coincidence” · `card.astronomy.007.c1` (text): By pure luck, the Moon is exactly the right size to blot out the Sun. / The Sun is enormous and the Moon is small, yet the Moon can cover the Sun completely. It isn't design. It's a coincidence of two numbers.
  - L7 “Eclipses and a Cosmic Coincidence” · `card.astronomy.007.c3` (fact): The Sun is about 400 times wider than the Moon, and about 400 times farther away. / Those two numbers cancel out, so the Sun and Moon look almost exactly the same size in our sky. It's the same trick as hiding a faraway building behind your thumb. That's why, in a total solar eclipse, the Moon can cover the Sun's bright disc and reveal its faint outer glow, the corona. The Sun is 400 times bigger. It's also 400 times farther. Tie.
- **Tested by:**
  - L7 `question.astronomy.007.q2`: Why can the Moon exactly cover the Sun during a total solar eclipse? → **The Sun is ~400× wider and ~400× farther away**
  - L56 `question.astronomy.056.q3`: From Level 7: when can we most easily see the Sun’s corona from Earth? → **During a total solar eclipse**
- **Fact-check (2026-09-24):** corroborated. The Sun is about 400 times the Moon's diameter and about 400 times farther away, so both span about half a degree in our sky. Sources: https://courses.lumenlearning.com/suny-astronomy/chapter/eclipses-of-the-sun-and-moon/, https://earthsky.org/space/coincidence-that-sun-and-moon-seem-same-size/

```sh
npm run verify:record -- fact.astronomy.sun_moon_400 source.nasa_eclipses --status verified --by "Your Name" --quote "…"
```

### ☐ `fact.astronomy.total_eclipse_corona`: unverified

> **Claim:** During a total solar eclipse the Moon covers the Sun's bright disc, revealing its faint outer atmosphere (the corona).

- **Concept:** The 400× coincidence (`concept.astronomy.eclipse_coincidence`)
- **Appears in:**
  - L7 “Eclipses and a Cosmic Coincidence” · `card.astronomy.007.c3` (fact): The Sun is about 400 times wider than the Moon, and about 400 times farther away. / Those two numbers cancel out, so the Sun and Moon look almost exactly the same size in our sky. It's the same trick as hiding a faraway building behind your thumb. That's why, in a total solar eclipse, the Moon can cover the Sun's bright disc and reveal its faint outer glow, the corona. The Sun is 400 times bigger. It's also 400 times farther. Tie.
  - L56 “Inside the Sun” · `card.astronomy.056.c4` (fact): The Sun's surface is about 5,500 °C. Its corona tops 1 million °C. / Back to the campfire. The core is about 15 million °C, and moving out to the surface it cools, as you'd expect. Then the corona is more than 180 times hotter than the surface below it. Why? Nobody fully knows yet. You can see this puzzle yourself during a total solar eclipse (Level 7), when the Moon covers the Sun's bright disc and reveals the corona.
- **Tested by:**
  - L7 `question.astronomy.007.q2`: Why can the Moon exactly cover the Sun during a total solar eclipse? → **The Sun is ~400× wider and ~400× farther away**
  - L56 `question.astronomy.056.q3`: From Level 7: when can we most easily see the Sun’s corona from Earth? → **During a total solar eclipse**
- **Pre-check:** Card says "faint outer glow"; confirm the eclipses page names the corona.
- **Fact-check (2026-09-24):** corroborated. NASA: in totality the Moon blocks the Sun's bright face, leaving the much dimmer corona, the Sun's outer atmosphere, visible. Sources: https://science.nasa.gov/eclipses/types/, https://spaceplace.nasa.gov/sun-corona/en/

```sh
npm run verify:record -- fact.astronomy.total_eclipse_corona source.nasa_eclipses --status verified --by "Your Name" --quote "…"
```

### ☐ `fact.astronomy.all_light_same_speed`: unverified

> **Claim:** All kinds of electromagnetic radiation, from radio waves to gamma rays, travel through space at the speed of light.

- **Concept:** The electromagnetic spectrum (`concept.astronomy.em_spectrum`)
- **Appears in:**
  - L31 “More Than Meets the Eye: The Spectrum” · `card.astronomy.031.c3` (text): Same speed, every kind / Here's what doesn't change: speed. Every kind of light, from radio to gamma rays, crosses space at the speed of light. So a light-year (Level 8) is the same distance whether you measure it in radio waves or in starlight. The wavelength rule even runs inside your own little window: red is the longest visible wavelength and violet the shortest. Red and violet are the edges of what you can see.
  - L38 “Robot Explorers” · `card.astronomy.038.c4` (fact): Voyager 1’s radio signals take more than 20 hours to reach Earth. / Radio waves travel at the speed of light, so every message from Voyager 1 is almost a day old when it lands. Remember the 8-minute-old Sun from Level 1? Voyager makes that look like live TV.
- **Tested by:**
  - L31 `question.astronomy.031.q3`: From Level 8: light-years are based on the speed of light. Does a light-year mean the same distance for radio waves? → **Yes: every kind of light travels at the same speed**
- **Fact-check (2026-09-24):** corroborated. All electromagnetic radiation travels through a vacuum at c (about 300,000 km/s) regardless of wavelength. Sources: https://science.nasa.gov/ems/02_anatomy/, https://science.nasa.gov/learn/basics-of-space-flight/chapter6-1/

```sh
npm run verify:record -- fact.astronomy.all_light_same_speed source.nasa_ems --status verified --by "Your Name" --quote "…"
```

### ☐ `fact.astronomy.animals_uv`: unverified

Anything longer, like infrared, or shorter, like ultraviolet, slips past you unseen. Some animals do better: bees can see ultraviolet light that's invisible to you. On this one point, the bee wins.

### ☐ `fact.astronomy.atmosphere_blocks`: unverified

> **Claim:** Earth’s atmosphere blocks most ultraviolet light and X-rays, and much infrared, from reaching the ground.

- **Concept:** The atmosphere gets in the way (`concept.astronomy.atmosphere_blurs`)
- **Appears in:**
  - L34 “Why Put Telescopes in Space?” · `card.astronomy.034.c2` (text): The trouble with air / Stars twinkle because moving pockets of air in Earth's atmosphere bend their light back and forth. It's like looking at a coin at the bottom of a swimming pool while the water ripples. Telescopes get that blur too. Worse, the atmosphere blocks most ultraviolet light and X-rays, and much infrared, before they ever reach the ground. Some of the sky's light simply never arrives.
  - L34 “Why Put Telescopes in Space?” · `card.astronomy.034.c3` (text): Hubble, above it all / The fix: get above the air. The Hubble Space Telescope launched in 1990 and orbits above the atmosphere, so its images are free of that blurring. Up there, the light the atmosphere blocks is no longer out of reach either. And Hubble is the only space telescope designed to be repaired and upgraded by astronauts in orbit. Now that's a house call. / Launched 1990
  - L36 “Seeing Heat: Infrared and Webb” · `card.astronomy.036.c3` (text): The James Webb Space Telescope / Earth's atmosphere blocks much infrared (Level 34), so this infrared telescope works from space. Webb launched on December 25, 2021: quite a Christmas present. Its main mirror is 6.5 meters across, made of 18 gold-coated segments. It orbits the Sun far from Earth, behind a five-layer sunshield about the size of a tennis court that keeps it extremely cold. A telescope that sees heat, parked in the shade. / 6.5 m mirror · 18 segments
  - L40 “Checkpoint: The Tools of Astronomy” · `card.astronomy.040.c2` (text): X-rays: the hottest things / X-ray telescopes reveal extremely hot gas, like the gas around black holes. There's a catch, and you met it in Level 34: Earth's atmosphere absorbs X-rays. So X-ray telescopes must work in space. NASA's Chandra X-ray Observatory launched in 1999 to do exactly that. The same air that blinds X-ray astronomers on the ground is shielding you from X-rays right now. / Chandra · launched 1999
- **Tested by:**
  - L34 `question.astronomy.034.q1`: Why do stars twinkle? → **Moving air in Earth’s atmosphere bends their light**
  - L34 `question.astronomy.034.q2`: Besides sharper images, what else do telescopes gain by going to space? → **Access to light the atmosphere blocks, like ultraviolet and X-rays**
  - L34 `question.astronomy.034.q3`: From Level 31: which kinds of light are mostly blocked before they reach the ground? → **Ultraviolet and X-rays**
  - L36 `question.astronomy.036.q3`: From Level 34: why does it help that Webb works in space? → **Much infrared light never reaches the ground**
- **Fact-check (2026-09-24):** corroborated. The atmosphere blocks X-rays, gamma rays, most ultraviolet and most infrared radiation, so these are observed largely from space or high sites. Sources: https://courses.lumenlearning.com/suny-astronomy/chapter/observations-outside-earths-atmosphere/, https://www.ipac.caltech.edu/Outreach/Edu/Windows/irwindows.html

### ☐ `fact.astronomy.red_longest_violet_shortest`: unverified

> **Claim:** In visible light, red light has the longest wavelength and violet light the shortest.

- **Concept:** The electromagnetic spectrum (`concept.astronomy.em_spectrum`)
- **Appears in:**
  - L31 “More Than Meets the Eye: The Spectrum” · `card.astronomy.031.c3` (text): Same speed, every kind / Here's what doesn't change: speed. Every kind of light, from radio to gamma rays, crosses space at the speed of light. So a light-year (Level 8) is the same distance whether you measure it in radio waves or in starlight. The wavelength rule even runs inside your own little window: red is the longest visible wavelength and violet the shortest. Red and violet are the edges of what you can see.
  - L89 “Redshift” · `card.astronomy.089.c2` (text): Stretched light / Remember Level 88: space is expanding. As light crosses expanding space, its wavelength gets stretched, like a wave drawn on a rubber band as you pull it. Red has the longest wavelength of visible light, so stretched light shifts toward the red end of the spectrum. Astronomers call this redshift: light stretched to longer, redder wavelengths. / Stretched light → redder
- **Tested by:**
  - L31 `question.astronomy.031.q3`: From Level 8: light-years are based on the speed of light. Does a light-year mean the same distance for radio waves? → **Yes: every kind of light travels at the same speed**
- **Fact-check (2026-09-24):** corroborated. NASA: in the visible spectrum red has the longest wavelength (about 700 nm) and violet the shortest (about 380 nm). Sources: https://science.nasa.gov/ems/09_visiblelight/
- **Weak evidence:** one-page (revisit first)

- **Appears in:**
  - L47 “Ocean Worlds: Europa and Enceladus” · `card.astronomy.047.c1` (text): Some of the solar system’s biggest oceans are on moons, under ice. / Out past the asteroid belt, far from the Sun, you'd expect every drop of water to be frozen solid. Out at Saturn, the moon Enceladus gives its secret away: NASA's Cassini spacecraft saw plumes of water ice spraying from cracks near its south pole. Some oceans hide.   - L79 “The Search for Life” · `card.astronomy.079.c2` (text): Close to home / In our own solar system, the search follows the water. Why there? Liquid water, a source of energy and the right chemistry are the key ingredients for life as we know it. Call it life’s recipe card.

Apart from Earth, it's the only world known to have stable liquid on its surface. Even Europa, with all its water, keeps its ocean hidden under a shell of ice. A place like Titan deserved a visit, and in 2005 it got one: the Huygens probe landed there, the first landing ever on a world in the outer solar system. Of all the far-off places to touch down first, we picked the one with lakes. / Huygens landed 2005

### ☐ `fact.astronomy.exoplanet_definition`: unverified

> **Claim:** An exoplanet is a planet outside our solar system. Most orbit stars other than the Sun, though some drift freely without a star.

- **Concept:** Finding exoplanets (`concept.astronomy.exoplanet_detection`)
- **Appears in:**
  - L77 “Finding Exoplanets” · `card.astronomy.077.c2` (text): Worlds beyond the Sun / An exoplanet is simply a planet outside our solar system. Most orbit other stars, though some drift freely with no star at all. The first found around a Sun-like star was 51 Pegasi b, in 1995, by Michel Mayor and Didier Queloz. It earned them half of the 2019 Nobel Prize in Physics. Today more than 6,000 exoplanets are confirmed. Our solar system is one house on a very long street. / 1995: the first around a Sun-like star
- **Tested by:**
  - L77 `question.astronomy.077.q1`: What is an exoplanet? → **A planet outside our solar system**
- **Fact-check (2026-09-24):** corrected. NASA defines an exoplanet as any planet outside our solar system; most orbit other stars, but some free-floating 'rogue' planets orbit no star at all. Sources: https://science.nasa.gov/exoplanets/facts/, https://www.esa.int/Science_Exploration/Space_Science/Exoplanets/What_are_exoplanets
- **Previous wording:** An exoplanet is a planet orbiting a star other than the Sun.

### ☐ `fact.astronomy.hz_definition`: unverified

> **Claim:** The habitable zone is the range of distances from a star where a planet’s surface could be the right temperature for liquid water.

- **Concept:** The habitable zone (`concept.astronomy.habitable_zone`)
- **Appears in:**
  - L78 “The Habitable Zone” · `card.astronomy.078.c1` (text): Not too hot, not too cold. Planets have a Goldilocks zone. / Around every star there’s a band where a planet could be just right for liquid water. Where that band sits depends on the star.
  - L78 “The Habitable Zone” · `card.astronomy.078.c2` (text): Just right for water / The habitable zone is the range of distances from a star where a planet’s surface could be the right temperature for liquid water. Where it lies depends on the star. Around dim, cool stars like red dwarfs, the zone sits much closer in; around bright stars, it’s farther out. It’s like a campfire: with a small fire, you pull your chair in close. / Dim star → close zone · Bright star → far zone
- **Tested by:**

- **Fact-check (2026-09-24):** corroborated. NASA defines the habitable zone as the distance from a star where liquid water could persist on a planet's surface, given a suitable atmosphere. ### ☐ `fact.astronomy.transit_method`: unverified

> **Claim:** The transit method finds planets by the small, regular dip in a star’s light when a planet passes in front of it.

- **Concept:** Finding exoplanets (`concept.astronomy.exoplanet_detection`)
- **Appears in:**
  - L77 “Finding Exoplanets” · `card.astronomy.077.c3` (text): Dips and wobbles / Two tricks do the finding. The transit method watches for the small, regular dip in a star’s light when a planet passes in front of it: a tiny blink, again on every orbit. The wobble method watches the star move. A planet’s gravity tugs it slightly back and forth, and that motion shows up as shifts in the star’s spectral lines, Level 32’s barcodes. One trick catches the shadow; the other catches the tug. / Transit = dip · Wobble = spectral lines shift
  - L77 “Finding Exoplanets” · `card.astronomy.077.c4` (fact): NASA’s Kepler space telescope found thousands of exoplanets by watching for transits. / It used the transit method, watching starlight for those small, regular dips as planets crossed in front of their stars. Thousands of tiny blinks added up to thousands of worlds.
  - L80 “Checkpoint: Extreme Objects and Other Worlds” · `card.astronomy.080.c3` (text): Listening and looking / Each discovery in this chapter came from reading a different signal. Pulsars give away neutron stars with radio beams that sweep past Earth like a lighthouse. Gravitational waves, first caught by LIGO in 2015, revealed two black holes merging. And tiny dips and wobbles in starlight have uncovered thousands of worlds around other stars, some, like TRAPPIST-1’s, in their habitable zones. Dead stars and new worlds, found the same way: by paying very close attention.
  - L97 “Integration: How We Know What We Know” · `card.astronomy.097.c2` (comparison): Every discovery has a method: What stars are made of (Spectral lines in their light: each element leaves its own set) vs Distance to nearby stars (Parallax: a star’s slight shift as Earth orbits the Sun) vs Planets around other stars (Transits (a dip in starlight) and wobbles (a star tugged back and forth))
  - L77 “Finding Exoplanets” · `card.astronomy.077.c1` (text): How do you find a planet you can’t see? Watch its star. / More than 6,000 planets beyond our solar system have been confirmed. Thousands of them were caught by the tiny effects they have on their stars.
- **Tested by:**
  - L77 `question.astronomy.077.q2`: How does the transit method detect a planet? → **By a small, regular dip in the star’s light**
  - L77 `question.astronomy.077.q3`: From Level 32: how do spectra help find planets with the wobble method? → **The star’s spectral lines shift as it wobbles**
  - L80 `question.astronomy.080.q4`: A star dims by a tiny amount every 12 days. What is the most likely explanation? → **A planet is transiting it on a 12-day orbit**
- **Fact-check (2026-09-24):** corroborated. The transit method detects planets by small, periodic dips in a star's brightness as a planet crosses in front of it (used by Kepler and TESS). Sources: https://science.nasa.gov/citizen-science/exoplanet-watch/background/, https://www.planetary.org/articles/down-in-front-the-transit-photometry-method

### ☐ `fact.astronomy.wobble_method`: unverified

> **Claim:** The wobble method finds planets by the slight back-and-forth motion their gravity causes in their star, measured through shifts in the star’s spectrum.

- **Concept:** Finding exoplanets (`concept.astronomy.exoplanet_detection`)
- **Appears in:**
  - L77 “Finding Exoplanets” · `card.astronomy.077.c3` (text): Dips and wobbles / Two tricks do the finding. The transit method watches for the small, regular dip in a star’s light when a planet passes in front of it: a tiny blink, again on every orbit. The wobble method watches the star move. A planet’s gravity tugs it slightly back and forth, and that motion shows up as shifts in the star’s spectral lines, Level 32’s barcodes. One trick catches the shadow; the other catches the tug. / Transit = dip · Wobble = spectral lines shift
  - L80 “Checkpoint: Extreme Objects and Other Worlds” · `card.astronomy.080.c3` (text): Listening and looking / Each discovery in this chapter came from reading a different signal. Pulsars give away neutron stars with radio beams that sweep past Earth like a lighthouse. Gravitational waves, first caught by LIGO in 2015, revealed two black holes merging. And tiny dips and wobbles in starlight have uncovered thousands of worlds around other stars, some, like TRAPPIST-1’s, in their habitable zones. Dead stars and new worlds, found the same way: by paying very close attention.
  - L97 “Integration: How We Know What We Know” · `card.astronomy.097.c2` (comparison): Every discovery has a method: What stars are made of (Spectral lines in their light: each element leaves its own set) vs Distance to nearby stars (Parallax: a star’s slight shift as Earth orbits the Sun) vs Planets around other stars (Transits (a dip in starlight) and wobbles (a star tugged back and forth))
  - L77 “Finding Exoplanets” · `card.astronomy.077.c1` (text): How do you find a planet you can’t see? Watch its star. / More than 6,000 planets beyond our solar system have been confirmed. Thousands of them were caught by the tiny effects they have on their stars.
- **Tested by:**
  - L77 `question.astronomy.077.q2`: How does the transit method detect a planet? → **By a small, regular dip in the star’s light**
  - L77 `question.astronomy.077.q3`: From Level 32: how do spectra help find planets with the wobble method? → **The star’s spectral lines shift as it wobbles**
  - L80 `question.astronomy.080.q4`: A star dims by a tiny amount every 12 days. What is the most likely explanation? → **A planet is transiting it on a 12-day orbit**
- **Fact-check (2026-09-24):** corroborated. The radial velocity or Doppler wobble method detects the star's reflex motion via tiny Doppler shifts in its spectral lines. Sources: https://exoplanetarchive.ipac.caltech.edu/docs/rv.html, https://www.planetary.org/articles/color-shifting-stars-the-radial-velocity-method

```sh
npm run verify:record -- fact.astronomy.wobble_method source.nasa_exoplanets --status verified --by "Your Name" --quote "…"
```

### ☐ `fact.astronomy.andromeda_distance`: unverified

> **Claim:** The Andromeda galaxy, the nearest large spiral galaxy, is about 2.5 million light-years away.

- **Concept:** Andromeda, our big neighbor (`concept.astronomy.andromeda`)
- **Appears in:**
  - L10 “Checkpoint: Your Place in the Universe” · `card.astronomy.010.c3` (fact): Andromeda, the nearest large spiral galaxy, is about 2.5 million light-years away. / You could line up 25 Milky Ways in that gap. Yet on a dark night you can see Andromeda with the naked eye, as a faint smudge. That smudge is a whole galaxy, and the light reaching your eye took about 2.5 million years to get here.
  - L83 “When Galaxies Collide” · `card.astronomy.083.c2` (text): The forecast / Galaxies can collide and merge, pulling each other into new shapes over hundreds of millions of years. Andromeda, about 2.5 million light-years away, is moving toward the Milky Way. For years the forecast was a merger in about 4.5 billion years. Then a 2025 study put the chance of one within the next 10 billion years at only about 50%. The sure thing became a coin flip. / Merger: a coin flip within 10 billion years
  - L88 “Hubble’s Expanding Universe” · `card.astronomy.088.c2` (text): One galaxy among many / Fuzzy patches like the Andromeda "nebula" were once debated. Were they clouds inside the Milky Way, or separate galaxies? In 1924 Edwin Hubble settled it: Andromeda is a separate galaxy about 2.5 million light-years away, far beyond our own. So galaxies exist beyond the Milky Way, and ours is just one of them. Humbling, and a lot more to explore.
  - L95 “How Big Is the Observable Universe?” · `card.astronomy.095.c2` (text): Looking back in time / Level 8 said a light-year is a distance. Light takes time to cover it, so looking far away means looking into the past. We see the Andromeda galaxy as it was about 2.5 million years ago, not as it is today. The most distant light we can see set out about 13.8 billion years ago. Every telescope is a time machine that only goes backward.
  - L83 “When Galaxies Collide” · `card.astronomy.083.c4` (fact): You can see our galactic neighbor with your own eyes. / From a dark site, Andromeda shows up to the naked eye as a faint smudge. That smudge is the galaxy in this story, about 2.5 million light-years away and heading our way. It's the rare forecast you can check from your backyard.
  - L84 “The Local Group” · `card.astronomy.084.c4` (fact): Andromeda is the Local Group's other giant. / It's the other mansion, about 2.5 million light-years away, and from a dark site you can spot it with the naked eye as a faint smudge. Remember Level 83: it's also the neighbor that might, someday, move in.
  - L84 “The Local Group” · `card.astronomy.084.c2` (text): Two mansions, lots of cottages / The Milky Way belongs to the Local Group, about 100 or more galaxies spread across about 10 million light-years. Most of them are small dwarf galaxies. Two big spirals run the place: the Milky Way and Andromeda. Think of a small town with two mansions and a lot of cottages. We live in one of the mansions. / ~100+ galaxies · ~10 million ly
- **Tested by:**
  - L10 `question.astronomy.010.q3`: From Level 8: Andromeda is 2.5 million light-years away. What does that mean? → **Its light took 2.5 million years to reach us**
  - L10 `question.astronomy.010.q5`: Chapter 1 recap: which of these is farthest from Earth? → **The Andromeda galaxy**
  - L83 `question.astronomy.083.q3`: From Level 10: Andromeda is about 2.5 million light-years away. What did a 2025 study say about a merger? → **About a 50% chance within 10 billion years**
  - L88 `question.astronomy.088.q3`: From Level 10: Andromeda is about 2.5 million light-years away. Why did Hubble’s 1924 finding about it matter? → **It proved galaxies exist beyond the Milky Way**
- **Fact-check (2026-09-24):** corroborated. Andromeda is a spiral about 2.5 million light-years away and the closest major galaxy to the Milky Way. Sources: https://www.space.com/15590-andromeda-galaxy-m31.html, https://earthsky.org/clusters-nebulae-galaxies/andromeda-galaxy-closest-spiral-to-milky-way/

### ☐ `fact.astronomy.billions_of_galaxies`: unverified

> **Claim:** The observable universe contains billions of galaxies, many with billions of stars.

  - L10 “Checkpoint: Your Place in the Universe” · `card.astronomy.010.c4` (text): And then everything else / Andromeda is just one galaxy. The observable universe holds billions of galaxies, many with billions of stars each. And the universe itself is about 13.8 billion years old, about three times the Sun's age. So here's the full address: one planet, around one star, in one galaxy among billions.

> **Claim:** The Milky Way is about 100,000 light-years across.

  - L10 “Checkpoint: Your Place in the Universe” · `card.astronomy.010.c2` (text): Our galaxy / The Milky Way is about 100,000 light-years across. A light-year is a distance, so light leaving one edge would take about 100,000 years to reach the other. In Level 8, Proxima Centauri at 4.2 light-years felt far. Our galaxy is more than 20,000 times wider than that. The suburbs are big.

Around it spreads a flat disk about 100,000 light-years across, swirled into spiral arms. Wrapped around the whole thing is a faint halo of old stars and globular star clusters. And at the very center of the bulge sits a black hole, Sagittarius A*. Not your usual breakfast. / Bulge · disk · arms · halo

### ☐ `fact.astronomy.hubble_tension`: unverified

> **Claim:** Different ways of measuring how fast the universe is expanding today give slightly different answers, a puzzle known as the Hubble tension.

- **Concept:** The big open questions (`concept.astronomy.open_questions`)
- **Appears in:**
  - L99 “Integration: The Big Open Questions” · `card.astronomy.099.c3` (text): Unsolved puzzles / Not every mystery is dark. Measure how fast the universe expands today in different ways and the answers disagree slightly, a puzzle called the Hubble tension. The Sun’s corona is far hotter than the surface below it, like air above a campfire being hotter than the flames, and nobody fully knows why. And what, if anything, came before the Big Bang? The theory doesn’t say.
- **Tested by:**
  - L99 `question.astronomy.099.q1`: What is the Hubble tension? → **Expansion measurements that disagree slightly**
- **Pre-check:** Confirm either page covers the tension; otherwise cite a specific NASA Hubble/Webb release.
- **Fact-check (2026-09-24):** corroborated. NASA's Webb/Hubble releases describe the Hubble tension: local distance-ladder measurements (~73 km/s/Mpc) disagree with CMB-based predictions (~67 km/s/Mpc). Sources: https://science.nasa.gov/missions/hubble/nasas-webb-hubble-telescopes-affirm-universes-expansion-rate-puzzle-persists, https://science.nasa.gov/blogs/webb/2023/09/12/webb-confirms-accuracy-of-universes-expansion-rate-measured-by-hubble-deepens-mystery-of-hubble-constant-tension/

### ☐ `fact.astronomy.twinkling`: unverified

> **Claim:** Stars twinkle because turbulence in Earth’s atmosphere bends their light back and forth.

- **Concept:** The atmosphere gets in the way (`concept.astronomy.atmosphere_blurs`)
- **Appears in:**
  - L34 “Why Put Telescopes in Space?” · `card.astronomy.034.c1` (text): Twinkling is beautiful. For astronomers, it’s a problem. / That gentle shimmer isn't the star. It's Earth's air getting in the way. Lovely for lullabies, terrible for photos.
  - L34 “Why Put Telescopes in Space?” · `card.astronomy.034.c2` (text): The trouble with air / Stars twinkle because moving pockets of air in Earth's atmosphere bend their light back and forth. It's like looking at a coin at the bottom of a swimming pool while the water ripples. Telescopes get that blur too. Worse, the atmosphere blocks most ultraviolet light and X-rays, and much infrared, before they ever reach the ground. Some of the sky's light simply never arrives.
- **Tested by:**
  - L34 `question.astronomy.034.q1`: Why do stars twinkle? → **Moving air in Earth’s atmosphere bends their light**
  - L34 `question.astronomy.034.q2`: Besides sharper images, what else do telescopes gain by going to space? → **Access to light the atmosphere blocks, like ultraviolet and X-rays**
  - L34 `question.astronomy.034.q3`: From Level 31: which kinds of light are mostly blocked before they reach the ground? → **Ultraviolet and X-rays**
  - L36 `question.astronomy.036.q3`: From Level 34: why does it help that Webb works in space? → **Much infrared light never reaches the ground**
- **Fact-check (2026-09-24):** corroborated. Twinkling (scintillation) is caused by turbulent air cells of varying density refracting starlight; stars do not twinkle from space. Sources: https://www.hko.gov.hk/en/education/earth-science/general/00356-why-do-stars-twinkle.html
- **Weak evidence:** one-page (revisit first)
