### ☐ `fact.astronomy.astronauts_free_fall`: unverified

> **Claim:** Astronauts float in orbit because they and their spacecraft are falling together, not because there is no gravity.

- **Concept:** An orbit is falling around (`concept.astronomy.orbit_falling`)
- **Appears in:**
  - L37 “Getting to Orbit” · `card.astronomy.037.c1` (text): Astronauts aren’t floating. They’re falling, and missing. / Gravity hasn't let go of the space station. It's falling the whole time. It just never hits the ground.
  - L37 “Getting to Orbit” · `card.astronomy.037.c3` (text): Why astronauts float / Here's the twist: gravity hasn't switched off up there. Astronauts float because they and their spacecraft are falling together, so nothing presses them against the floor. You've felt a flicker of this on a roller coaster's first big drop, when your stomach lifts off your seat. Astronauts get that feeling all day, every day.

### ☐ `fact.astronomy.great_red_spot`: unverified

> **Claim:** Jupiter’s Great Red Spot is a giant storm wider than Earth that has been observed for more than 150 years.

- **Concept:** Jupiter’s storms and moons (`concept.astronomy.jupiter_system`)
- **Appears in:**
  - L46 “Jupiter’s Storms and Moons” · `card.astronomy.046.c1` (text): Jupiter has a storm wider than Earth, and it's been going for 150+ years. / Last level, Jupiter's gravity bullied the asteroid belt. Up close, the giant is just as dramatic: a storm bigger than our whole planet, and a family of moons that are worlds of their own.
  - L46 “Jupiter’s Storms and Moons” · `card.astronomy.046.c2` (text): The Great Red Spot / Jupiter's Great Red Spot is a giant storm wider than Earth. Lay our whole planet across it, and the storm is still wider. Every storm you've ever sat through blew over in a day or two. Astronomers have been watching this one for more than 150 years, and it's still there. It isn't forever, though: it has been shrinking over the past century. Even Jupiter's weather calms down eventually.
- **Tested by:**
  - L46 `question.astronomy.046.q1`: What is Jupiter’s Great Red Spot? → **A storm wider than Earth**
- **Fact-check (2026-09-24):** corroborated. The Great Red Spot is about 1.3 times wider than Earth today and has been tracked continuously since about 1830/1831, i.e. well over 150 years. Sources: https://www.nasa.gov/centers-and-facilities/goddard/jupiters-great-red-spot-a-swirling-mystery/, https://theconversation.com/jupiters-great-red-spot-a-300-year-old-cyclone-persists-but-is-shrinking-112934

### ☐ `fact.astronomy.jupiter_mass`: unverified

> **Claim:** Jupiter is more than twice as massive as all the other planets combined.

- **Concept:** Jupiter outweighs every other planet combined (`concept.astronomy.jupiter_mass`)
- **Appears in:**
  - L4 “Giants of the Outer Dark” · `card.astronomy.004.c3` (fact): Jupiter has more than twice the mass of all the other planets combined. / Remember the crumbs from Level 2, the 0.2% of the solar system that isn't the Sun? Jupiter is the biggest crumb by far. Put it on one side of a giant scale and every other planet on the other side. Jupiter wins. Double the pile and Jupiter still wins. If the planets were a team, Jupiter would be carrying it.
  - L50 “Milestone: The Solar System” · `card.astronomy.050.c3` (comparison): Where the mass is: one giant and a lot of crumbs: The Sun (About 99.8% of all the mass; Its gravity holds every planet and smaller body in orbit) vs Everything else (About 0.2%: planets, moons, asteroids and comets; Jupiter alone is more than twice all the other planets combined)
  - L45 “The Asteroid Belt” · `card.astronomy.045.c2` (text): The planet that never happened / The main asteroid belt lies between the orbits of Mars and Jupiter. Most asteroids are rock; some are largely metal. It's building material that never got built. Blame the neighbor. Remember from Level 4 that Jupiter is more than twice as massive as all the other planets combined? Its strong gravity stirred up the material there and kept it from ever gathering into a planet. Hard to build a sandcastle while someone keeps kicking it.

  - L25 “Galileo’s Telescope” · `card.astronomy.025.c3` (text): Moons that don't orbit Earth / In 1610 Galileo found four large moons circling Jupiter: Io, Europa, Ganymede and Callisto. It wasn't even the only planet with a moon. / Io · Europa · Ganymede · Callisto
  - L30 “Checkpoint: How We Know” · `card.astronomy.030.c2` (timeline): How we figured it out: ~240 BCE: Eratosthenes measures Earth with the noon Sun; ~150 CE: Ptolemy puts a still Earth at the center; 1543: Copernicus hands the center to the Sun; 1610: Galileo finds moons circling Jupiter; 1687: Newton's gravity explains why planets move; 1838: Bessel measures stellar parallax
  - L46 “Jupiter’s Storms and Moons” · `card.astronomy.046.c3` (text): Four worlds of their own / Jupiter has around 100 known moons, and more keep turning up. But the four Galileo spotted in 1610 are by far the largest, and each has a personality. Io is the most volcanically active world in the solar system. Ganymede is the largest moon of all, bigger than the planet Mercury: a moon that outsizes a planet. Callisto is one of the most heavily cratered worlds known. And Europa? It gets the next level to itself. / Io · Europa · Ganymede · Callisto

  - L46 `question.astronomy.046.q3`: From Level 25: Galileo saw Jupiter’s four large moons as tiny dots. What do we know about Io today? → **It is the most volcanically active world in the solar system**
  - L100 `question.astronomy.100.q9`: Which list puts these discoveries in the order they happened? → **Jupiter’s moons → galaxy redshifts → the CMB**

  - L77 “Finding Exoplanets” · `card.astronomy.077.c4` (fact): NASA’s Kepler space telescope found thousands of exoplanets by watching for transits. / It used the transit method, watching starlight for those small, regular dips as planets crossed in front of their stars. Thousands of tiny blinks added up to thousands of worlds.
  - L77 “Finding Exoplanets” · `card.astronomy.077.c1` (text): How do you find a planet you can’t see? Watch its star. / More than 6,000 planets beyond our solar system have been confirmed. Thousands of them were caught by the tiny effects they have on their stars.
  - L80 “Checkpoint: Extreme Objects and Other Worlds” · `card.astronomy.080.c3` (text): Listening and looking / Each discovery in this chapter came from reading a different signal. Pulsars give away neutron stars with radio beams that sweep past Earth like a lighthouse. Gravitational waves, first caught by LIGO in 2015, revealed two black holes merging. And tiny dips and wobbles in starlight have uncovered thousands of worlds around other stars, some, like TRAPPIST-1’s, in their habitable zones. Dead stars and new worlds, found the same way: by paying very close attention.
- **Fact-check (2026-09-24):** corroborated. Kepler (2009-2018) used the transit method and discovered more than 2,600 confirmed exoplanets. Sources: https://science.nasa.gov/mission/kepler/

## Kuiper Belt (NASA Science)

- **Source:** `source.nasa_kuiper_belt`
- **URL:** https://science.nasa.gov/solar-system/kuiper-belt/
- **License:** US_gov_public_domain · **Accessed:** 2026-09-24 · **Source record checked (URL/licence):** no

### ☐ `fact.astronomy.kuiper_comets`: unverified

> **Claim:** Many short-period comets, especially those that return every few years, are thought to come from the Kuiper Belt and the nearby scattered disk.

- **Concept:** The Kuiper Belt (`concept.astronomy.kuiper_belt`)
- **Appears in:**
  - L52 “The Kuiper Belt” · `card.astronomy.052.c2` (text): A second belt / The Kuiper Belt is a doughnut-shaped region of icy bodies beyond the orbit of Neptune. Think of it as the asteroid belt's big cousin who lives in the freezer. The asteroid belt is mostly rock; the Kuiper Belt is mostly ice, and it's about 20 times as wide. Pluto is one of its largest known members, which explains Level 51: Pluto orbits among many other icy bodies of its own zone. It also sends us visitors. Many comets that return every few years are thought to come from here and the scattered disk just beyond.
  - L53 “Comets: Dirty Snowballs” · `card.astronomy.053.c2` (text): Dirty snowballs / Comets are chunks of ice, dust and rock, often described as dirty snowballs. Many of the ones that return every few years are thought to come from the Kuiper Belt you met in Level 52. Far from the Sun, a comet is just that: a dirty snowball. As one nears the Sun, its ice turns to gas, forming a glowing cloud called a coma and long tails. A comet only puts on a show when it gets close enough for its ice to start turning to gas.
  - L55 “The Oort Cloud” · `card.astronomy.055.c3` (text): Known only from comets / No object has ever been directly observed in the Oort Cloud. Astronomers infer it from comets that take thousands of years or more to orbit the Sun: trace their long paths back, and they point to a distant shell all around us. It's like guessing there's a bakery nearby because people keep walking past with fresh bread. The comets that return every few years come from the Kuiper Belt. The ones that take thousands of years come from here.
- **Tested by:**
  - L52 `question.astronomy.052.q1`: Where is the Kuiper Belt? → **Beyond the orbit of Neptune**
  - L52 `question.astronomy.052.q2`: What is the biggest difference between the Kuiper Belt and the asteroid belt? → **The Kuiper Belt is mostly ice; the asteroid belt is mostly rock**
  - L52 `question.astronomy.052.q3`: From Level 51: Pluto hasn’t cleared its orbital neighborhood. What does its home region explain? → **It orbits among many other icy bodies of its own zone**
  - L53 `question.astronomy.053.q3`: From Level 52: where are many comets that return every few years thought to come from? → **The Kuiper Belt**
  - L60 `question.astronomy.060.q5`: Chapter 6 recap: which list goes from nearest to farthest from the Sun? → **Asteroid belt → Kuiper Belt → Oort Cloud**
- **Pre-check:** Short-period comets (<200 yr) are thought to originate in the Kuiper Belt/scattered disk. Confirm wording.
- **Fact-check (2026-09-24):** disputed. Popular sources say short-period (<200 yr) comets come from the Kuiper Belt, but research traces Jupiter-family comets (periods under ~20 yr) to the Kuiper Belt/scattered disk, while Halley-type comets (20 to 200 yr) likely come from the Oort Cloud. Sources: https://www.aanda.org/articles/aa/full_html/2014/03/aa22508-13/aa22508-13.html, https://arxiv.org/pdf/1402.1339
- **Previous wording:** Many comets that return every few decades or centuries come from the Kuiper Belt.

## What Is a Light-Year? (NASA Science)

- **Source:** `source.nasa_light_year`
- **URL:** https://science.nasa.gov/exoplanets/what-is-a-light-year/
- **License:** US_gov_public_domain · **Accessed:** 2026-09-23 · **Source record checked (URL/licence):** no

### ☐ `fact.astronomy.light_year_distance`: unverified

> **Claim:** A light-year is the distance light travels in one year, about 9.46 trillion km.

- **Concept:** A light-year is a distance (`concept.astronomy.light_year`)
- **Appears in:**
  - L8 “Light-Years: Measuring the Unmeasurable” · `card.astronomy.008.c1` (text): A light-year sounds like a unit of time. It isn't. / It has 'year' right there in the name, so it's a fair guess. But a light-year is a ruler, not a clock.
  - L8 “Light-Years: Measuring the Unmeasurable” · `card.astronomy.008.c2` (text): Distance, measured in light / A light-year is how far light travels in one year: about 9.46 trillion kilometers. So it's a distance, the way a mile is. Kilometers are great for a road trip. Out among the stars they pile up into numbers nobody can read, so astronomers switched to a bigger ruler. / 1 light-year ≈ 9.46 trillion km
  - L8 “Light-Years: Measuring the Unmeasurable” · `card.astronomy.008.c3` (text): Why measure space in light? / Light is the fastest thing there is, which makes it a handy measuring tape. It crosses from the Moon to Earth in just over a second. From the Sun, it takes about 8 minutes (Level 1's slightly late Sun). So the Moon is about one light-second away and the Sun about 8 light-minutes. Past the Sun, even light needs years, and the ruler grows to match.
  - L8 “Light-Years: Measuring the Unmeasurable” · `card.astronomy.008.c4` (fact): The closest star after the Sun is Proxima Centauri, about 4.2 light-years away. / So its light takes about 4.2 years to reach us. Moon: a second. Sun: 8 minutes. Next star: more than four years. And that's the closest one; every other star is even farther. Proxima is our next-door neighbor. The street is just very, very long.
  - L10 “Checkpoint: Your Place in the Universe” · `card.astronomy.010.c2` (text): Our galaxy / The Milky Way is about 100,000 light-years across. A light-year is a distance, so light leaving one edge would take about 100,000 years to reach the other. In Level 8, Proxima Centauri at 4.2 light-years felt far. Our galaxy is more than 20,000 times wider than that. The suburbs are big.
  - L10 “Checkpoint: Your Place in the Universe” · `card.astronomy.010.c3` (fact): Andromeda, the nearest large spiral galaxy, is about 2.5 million light-years away. / You could line up 25 Milky Ways in that gap. Yet on a dark night you can see Andromeda with the naked eye, as a faint smudge. That smudge is a whole galaxy, and the light reaching your eye took about 2.5 million years to get here.
  - L13 “Connect the Dots: Constellations” · `card.astronomy.013.c3` (text): A trick of perspective / Stars that look side by side can be very different distances from Earth. Remember light-years from Level 8? One star in a pattern could be a few dozen light-years away, its neighbor hundreds. They only look close together from where we stand, the way a faraway streetlight can seem to sit right next to your neighbor's porch light.
  - L66 “Stellar Nurseries” · `card.astronomy.066.c3` (text): The nearest nursery / The Orion Nebula is the nearest large region of star formation, about 1,300 light-years away. Find Orion’s belt, then the sword hanging from it. On a dark night, part of the sword looks like a fuzzy patch. That fuzz is a nursery. Its light left about 1,300 years ago, so you’re looking at old baby pictures.
  - L31 “More Than Meets the Eye: The Spectrum” · `card.astronomy.031.c3` (text): Same speed, every kind / Here's what doesn't change: speed. Every kind of light, from radio to gamma rays, crosses space at the speed of light. So a light-year (Level 8) is the same distance whether you measure it in radio waves or in starlight. The wavelength rule even runs inside your own little window: red is the longest visible wavelength and violet the shortest. Red and violet are the edges of what you can see.
- **Tested by:**
  - L8 `question.astronomy.008.q1`: What does a light-year measure? → **Distance**
  - L8 `question.astronomy.008.q3`: Proxima Centauri is about 4.2 light-years away. What does that mean? → **Its light takes about 4.2 years to reach us**
  - L10 `question.astronomy.010.q3`: From Level 8: Andromeda is 2.5 million light-years away. What does that mean? → **Its light took 2.5 million years to reach us**
  - L10 `question.astronomy.010.q4`: The Milky Way is about 100,000 light-years across. Roughly how long would light take to cross it? → **About 100,000 years**
  - L13 `question.astronomy.013.q3`: From Level 8: two stars in Orion are 50 and 500 light-years away. What does that mean for their light? → **The farther star’s light left it about 450 years earlier**
  - L31 `question.astronomy.031.q3`: From Level 8: light-years are based on the speed of light. Does a light-year mean the same distance for radio waves? → **Yes: every kind of light travels at the same speed**
  - L95 `question.astronomy.095.q3`: From Level 8: a light-year is a distance. So what do you see when you look at a galaxy 2.5 million light-years away? → **The galaxy as it was long ago**
  - L100 `question.astronomy.100.q3`: Proxima Centauri is about 4.2 light-years away. How old is the light you see from it? → **About 4.2 years old**
- **Fact-check (2026-09-24):** corroborated. NASA: a light-year is the distance light travels in a year, about 5.88 trillion miles (9.46 trillion km). Sources: https://science.nasa.gov/exoplanets/what-is-a-light-year/, https://earthsky.org/astronomy-essentials/how-far-is-a-light-year/

So the Moon is about one light-second away and the Sun about 8 light-minutes. Past the Sun, even light needs years, and the ruler grows to match.
  - L8 “Light-Years: Measuring the Unmeasurable” · `card.astronomy.008.c4` (fact): The closest star after the Sun is Proxima Centauri, about 4.2 light-years away. / So its light takes about 4.2 years to reach us. ### ☐ `fact.astronomy.speed_of_light`: unverified

> **Claim:** Light travels about 300,000 km every second, faster than anything else.

- **Concept:** A light-year is a distance (`concept.astronomy.light_year`)
- **Appears in:**
  - L8 “Light-Years: Measuring the Unmeasurable” · `card.astronomy.008.c3` (text): Why measure space in light? / Light is the fastest thing there is, which makes it a handy measuring tape. It crosses from the Moon to Earth in just over a second. From the Sun, it takes about 8 minutes (Level 1's slightly late Sun). So the Moon is about one light-second away and the Sun about 8 light-minutes. Past the Sun, even light needs years, and the ruler grows to match.
  - L1 “Your Cosmic Address” · `card.astronomy.001.c3` (fact): The Sun you see is 8 minutes old. / Sunlight takes about 8 minutes to reach us, so the Sun in the sky is the Sun from 8 minutes ago. If it vanished right now, you'd keep seeing it for about 8 more minutes. Light is the fastest thing there is. The Sun is just that far away.
  - L73 “Black Holes” · `card.astronomy.073.c1` (text): A place where escape velocity is faster than light. / Level 37 said you need about 11.2 km per second to escape Earth. Now picture a place where even light, the fastest thing there is, isn’t fast enough.
  - L73 “Black Holes” · `card.astronomy.073.c2` (text): Gravity wins / A black hole is a region of space where gravity is so strong that nothing, not even light, can escape. In Level 37’s terms, its escape velocity is faster than the speed of light, and nothing can go that fast. That’s why it’s black: no light comes out for you to see. Stellar black holes form when the core of a very massive star collapses at the end of its life. Same kind of ending as a neutron star, with an even stranger leftover.
- **Fact-check (2026-09-24):** corroborated. Light in vacuum travels exactly 299,792,458 m/s (about 300,000 km/s); per special relativity nothing travels faster. Sources: https://www.space.com/15830-light-speed.html, https://www.wtamu.edu/~cbaird/sq/2024/02/07/why-is-the-speed-of-light-a-random-finite-number/

## Mars: Facts (NASA Science)

### ☐ `fact.astronomy.mars_ancient_water`: unverified

> **Claim:** Dried river valleys and ancient lake beds show that liquid water once flowed on Mars.

- **Concept:** Mars once had water (`concept.astronomy.mars_water_past`)
- **Appears in:**
  - L44 “Mars: A Planet That Lost Its Water” · `card.astronomy.044.c1` (text): Mars has riverbeds. It just doesn’t have rivers anymore. / Dried river valleys and old lake beds are carved into Mars. So where did the water go, and how do we know it was ever there?
  - L44 “Mars: A Planet That Lost Its Water” · `card.astronomy.044.c3` (text): Clues of a wetter past / Mars kept the receipts. Dry river valleys and ancient lake beds show that liquid water once flowed there. Then we sent a robot to check in person. NASA's Curiosity rover, which landed in 2012, found that Gale Crater once held a lake. Picture a crater as dry as a parking lot that used to be a place you could have gone for a swim. / Curiosity, 2012: Gale Crater once held a lake
- **Tested by:**
  - L44 `question.astronomy.044.q2`: What did the Curiosity rover discover at Gale Crater? → **That the crater once held a lake**
- **Fact-check (2026-09-24):** corroborated. Valley networks, deltas and lakebed mudstones (Curiosity at Gale, Perseverance at Jezero) show liquid water once flowed and pooled on Mars. Sources: https://www.jpl.nasa.gov/news/nasas-curiosity-rover-team-confirms-ancient-lakes-on-mars/, https://science.nasa.gov/solar-system/planets/mars/delta-like-fan-on-mars-suggests-ancient-rivers-were-persistent/

### ☐ `fact.astronomy.mars_dust_atmosphere`: unverified

- **Appears in:**

Iron oxide, the same stuff as the rust on an old bike, coats Martian dust and rocks, and fine red dust in the air tints the whole planet. That air is thin, too: less than 1% of Earth's air pressure. Venus's air would crush you. Mars's barely shows up.

- **Fact-check (2026-09-24):** corroborated. NASA: rusted iron in rocks, soil and dust makes Mars red; dust kicked into the atmosphere makes the planet appear red from afar. Sources: https://science.nasa.gov/mars/facts/, https://www.esa.int/Science_Exploration/Space_Science/Mars_Express/Have_we_been_wrong_about_why_Mars_is_red

Most of what Mars has today is frozen, in its polar ice caps and underground. The rivers are gone. The water is in the freezer.
  - L3 “The Rocky Four” · `card.astronomy.003.c4` (fact): Mars is red because it's rusty. / Seriously. Iron oxide, the same stuff as the rust on an old bike, coats Martian dust and rocks, and fine red dust in the air tints the whole planet. That air is thin, too: less than 1% of Earth's air pressure. Venus's air would crush you. Mars's barely shows up.
- **Tested by:**
  - L44 `question.astronomy.044.q1`: Where is most of Mars’s water today? → **Frozen in polar ice caps and underground**
  - L44 `question.astronomy.044.q3`: From Level 3: Mars is the outermost rocky planet. How does its air compare with Earth’s? → **Thin, under 1% of Earth’s pressure**
- **Fact-check (2026-09-24):** corroborated. Mars's atmosphere is about 95% CO2; mean surface pressure is about 600 Pa, roughly 0.6% of Earth's sea-level pressure. Sources: https://nssdc.gsfc.nasa.gov/planetary/factsheet/marsfact.html, https://www.space.com/16903-mars-atmosphere-climate-weather.html

## Mercury: Facts (NASA Science)

- **Source:** `source.nasa_mercury_facts`
- **URL:** https://science.nasa.gov/mercury/facts/
- **License:** US_gov_public_domain · **Accessed:** 2026-09-24 · **Source record checked (URL/licence):** no

### ☐ `fact.astronomy.mercury_no_atmosphere`: unverified

> **Claim:** Mercury has almost no atmosphere to trap heat.

- **Concept:** Mercury: extremes without air (`concept.astronomy.mercury_extremes`)
- **Appears in:**
  - L41 “Mercury: The Scorched Survivor” · `card.astronomy.041.c3` (text): No blanket / Close to the Sun should just mean hot. But Mercury has almost no atmosphere to hold heat in. By day its surface reaches about 430 °C. Once night falls, there's nothing to keep the warmth, and the ground plunges to about −180 °C. Remember Venus from Level 3? Its thick carbon dioxide atmosphere traps heat, so Venus is even hotter than Mercury, despite being farther from the Sun. Venus keeps the covers on. Mercury kicked them off. / Day ~430 °C · Night ~−180 °C
- **Tested by:**
  - L41 `question.astronomy.041.q2`: Why does Mercury get so cold at night despite being so close to the Sun? → **It has almost no atmosphere to hold in heat**
  - L41 `question.astronomy.041.q3`: From Level 3: Venus stays scorching all night, but Mercury freezes. What makes the difference? → **Venus has a thick heat-trapping atmosphere; Mercury has almost none**
- **Fact-check (2026-09-24):** corroborated. Mercury has only a thin exosphere, not a substantial atmosphere to retain heat, causing extreme temperature swings. Sources: https://www.space.com/18644-mercury-atmosphere.html, https://www.nhm.ac.uk/discover/planet-mercury.html

```sh
npm run verify:record -- fact.astronomy.mercury_no_atmosphere source.nasa_mercury_facts --status verified --by "Your Name" --quote "…"
```

### ☐ `fact.astronomy.mercury_polar_ice`: unverified

> **Claim:** Despite its heat, Mercury has water ice in craters near its poles that are always in shadow.

- **Concept:** Mercury: extremes without air (`concept.astronomy.mercury_extremes`)
- **Appears in:**
  - L41 “Mercury: The Scorched Survivor” · `card.astronomy.041.c1` (text): The planet closest to the Sun has ice on it. / No planet sits closer to the Sun than Mercury. It's the last place you'd look for anything frozen. So how does a planet bake and freeze at the same time?
  - L41 “Mercury: The Scorched Survivor” · `card.astronomy.041.c4` (fact): Mercury has water ice in craters near its poles. / Here's the answer to the riddle. The floors of some craters near Mercury's poles are always in shadow. Sunlight never reaches the bottom, so the ice survives there. The planet closest to the Sun keeps its ice the way you keep a drink cold at a picnic: out of the sun, in the shade.
- **Fact-check (2026-09-24):** corroborated. MESSENGER confirmed water ice in permanently shadowed craters near Mercury's poles. Sources: https://www.jpl.nasa.gov/news/nasa-spacecraft-finds-new-mercury-water-ice-evidence/, https://www.nasa.gov/image-article/water-ice-mercury/

## Meteors and Meteorites (NASA Science)

- **Source:** `source.nasa_meteors`
- **URL:** https://science.nasa.gov/solar-system/meteors-meteorites/
- **License:** US_gov_public_domain · **Accessed:** 2026-09-24 · **Source record checked (URL/licence):** no

### ☐ `fact.astronomy.meteor_showers`: unverified

> **Claim:** Meteor showers happen when Earth passes through a trail of debris, usually left by a comet (a few, like the Geminids, come from an asteroid).

- **Concept:** Meteoroids, meteors and meteorites (`concept.astronomy.meteors`)
- **Appears in:**
  - L54 “Shooting Stars and Meteorites” · `card.astronomy.054.c3` (text): Showers from comets / Remember the comets from Level 53? They leave trails of debris behind. When Earth passes through a comet's trail of debris, you get a meteor shower, streak after streak. Most of those meteors come from particles about the size of a grain of sand. A few showers, like the Geminids, come from an asteroid instead. Either way, it's Earth driving through somebody else's dust.
  - L54 “Shooting Stars and Meteorites” · `card.astronomy.054.c4` (fact): The Perseids peak every August. / That's when Earth crosses the debris of Comet Swift–Tuttle. The trail stays put along the comet's path, and Earth reaches it at the same point in its orbit every year. So the Perseids are an annual appointment with a comet's leftovers.
- **Tested by:**
  - L54 `question.astronomy.054.q2`: What causes a meteor shower? → **Earth passing through a comet’s trail of debris**
  - L54 `question.astronomy.054.q3`: From Level 53: why does a comet leave a trail of debris that can later make meteors? → **Near the Sun its ice turns to gas, and it leaves debris behind**
- **Fact-check (2026-09-24):** corrected. NASA: most meteor showers come from comet debris, but the Geminids come from asteroid 3200 Phaethon, so 'a comet' is not always the source. Sources: https://www.nasa.gov/blogs/watch-the-skies/2024/12/12/its-an-asteroid-its-a-comet-its-the-geminids-meteor-shower, https://www.nationalgeographic.com/science/article/geminids-meteor-shower-phaethon-asteroid
- **Previous wording:** Meteor showers happen when Earth passes through the trail of debris left by a comet.

```sh
npm run verify:record -- fact.astronomy.meteor_showers source.nasa_meteors --status verified --by "Your Name" --quote "…"
```

### ☐ `fact.astronomy.meteor_size`: unverified

> **Claim:** Most meteors come from particles about the size of a grain of sand.

- **Concept:** Meteoroids, meteors and meteorites (`concept.astronomy.meteors`)
- **Appears in:**
  - L54 “Shooting Stars and Meteorites” · `card.astronomy.054.c1` (text): Most shooting stars are no bigger than a grain of sand. / That bright streak you made a wish on was probably a speck about the size of a grain of sand. So how does a speck put on a light show?
  - L54 “Shooting Stars and Meteorites” · `card.astronomy.054.c3` (text): Showers from comets / Remember the comets from Level 53? They leave trails of debris behind. When Earth passes through a comet's trail of debris, you get a meteor shower, streak after streak. Most of those meteors come from particles about the size of a grain of sand. A few showers, like the Geminids, come from an asteroid instead. Either way, it's Earth driving through somebody else's dust.
- **Tested by:**
  - L54 `question.astronomy.054.q2`: What causes a meteor shower? → **Earth passing through a comet’s trail of debris**

Most meteors are caused by meteoroids about the size of a grain of sand (1 mm or smaller); NASA notes most comet debris is between sand-grain and pea size. ### ☐ `fact.astronomy.meteor_terms`: unverified

> **Claim:** A meteoroid is a small piece of rock or debris in space; a meteor is the streak of light it makes burning up in Earth’s atmosphere; a meteorite is any piece that reaches the ground.

- **Concept:** Meteoroids, meteors and meteorites (`concept.astronomy.meteors`)
- **Appears in:**
  - L54 “Shooting Stars and Meteorites” · `card.astronomy.054.c2` (text): Three names, one journey / The same bit of space debris gets three names, depending on where it is. Floating in space, a small piece of rock or debris is a meteoroid. When it plunges into Earth's atmosphere and burns up, the streak of light is a meteor: the shooting star you see. If any piece survives to reach the ground, that piece is a meteorite. Handy test: the meteorite is the only one you could ever hold in your hand. / Meteoroid → meteor → meteorite
  - L54 “Shooting Stars and Meteorites” · `card.astronomy.054.c1` (text): Most shooting stars are no bigger than a grain of sand. / That bright streak you made a wish on was probably a speck about the size of a grain of sand. So how does a speck put on a light show?
- **Tested by:**
  - L54 `question.astronomy.054.q1`: What is a meteorite? → **A piece that survives to reach the ground**
- **Fact-check (2026-09-24):** corroborated. NASA: meteoroids are space rocks; a meteor is the light as one vaporizes in the atmosphere; a meteorite is one that survives to reach the ground. ### ☐ `fact.astronomy.galactic_year`: unverified

> **Claim:** The Sun takes about 230 million years to orbit the center of the Milky Way once.

- **Concept:** The structure of the Milky Way (`concept.astronomy.milky_way_structure`)
- **Appears in:**
  - L82 “Inside the Milky Way” · `card.astronomy.082.c3` (text): Our place in it / The Sun lives out in the disk, in a minor spiral arm about 26,000 light-years from the center. That's roughly halfway to the edge: the galactic suburbs. From there it orbits the center, taking about 230 million years for one lap, a "galactic year." The Sun is about 4.6 billion years old, so it has made only about 20 laps. In galactic years, our star is about 20. / 1 galactic year ≈ 230 million years
  - L90 “Checkpoint: A Universe of Galaxies” · `card.astronomy.090.c2` (text): A ladder of scale / Climb it one rung at a time. The Sun circles the Milky Way once every 230 million years or so. The Milky Way is one of about 100 or more galaxies in the Local Group, our small town. Bigger still are clusters of hundreds or thousands of galaxies, cities held together largely by dark matter. And we're not at the top yet. / Galaxy → group → cluster
  - L82 “Inside the Milky Way” · `card.astronomy.082.c1` (text): The Sun has circled the Milky Way only about 20 times since it formed. / Right now the Sun, with Earth in tow, is circling the center of our galaxy. It's a very slow ride. Before timing a lap, here's a map of the track.
  - L90 “Checkpoint: A Universe of Galaxies” · `card.astronomy.090.c1` (text): Chapter 9 finale. Zoom out as far as galaxies go. / Start at home, with the Sun circling the Milky Way. Then keep zooming: our neighborhood, cities of galaxies, and the biggest pattern in the universe.
- **Tested by:**
  - L82 `question.astronomy.082.q1`: How long does the Sun take to orbit the center of the Milky Way? → **About 230 million years**
  - L82 `question.astronomy.082.q2`: Where is the Sun located in the Milky Way? → **In the disk, about 26,000 light-years from the center**
- **Fact-check (2026-09-24):** corroborated. NASA: the solar system takes about 230 million years to orbit the galactic center; estimates range roughly 225 to 250 million years. Sources: https://science.nasa.gov/solar-system/solar-system-facts/, https://public.nrao.edu/ask/how-long-does-it-take-the-sun-to-orbit-the-center-of-our-galaxy/

The Sun is about 4.6 billion years old, so it has made only about 20 laps. In galactic years, our star is about 20. / 1 galactic year ≈ 230 million years
- **Tested by:**
  - L82 `question.astronomy.082.q1`: How long does the Sun take to orbit the center of the Milky Way? → **About 230 million years**
  - L82 `question.astronomy.082.q2`: Where is the Sun located in the Milky Way? → **In the disk, about 26,000 light-years from the center**
- **Pre-check:** Derived: ~4.6 billion years ÷ ~230 million years ≈ 20.
- **Fact-check (2026-09-24):** corroborated. A galactic year is about 225-250 million years (230 million most quoted); with the Sun about 4.6 billion years old, that gives roughly 18-20 orbits, so 'about 20' is fair. Sources: https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Galactic_year, https://public.nrao.edu/ask/orbital-period-of-the-sun-in-the-milky-way-galaxy/

### ☐ `fact.astronomy.crater_sizes`: unverified

> **Claim:** Lunar craters range from tiny pits to basins hundreds of kilometers across.

- **Concept:** Lunar craters (`concept.astronomy.lunar_craters`)
- **Appears in:**
  - L19 “Craters and Seas on the Moon” · `card.astronomy.019.c2` (text): Scars that never heal / The Moon's craters were blasted out by asteroids and comets, and they range from tiny pits to basins hundreds of kilometers across. What's surprising is how long they last. The Moon has almost no atmosphere, no liquid water and no plate tectonics. Nothing wears its craters away, so they survive for billions of years. Every hit the Moon has taken, from the smallest pit to the biggest basin, is still on the record. The Moon never tidies up. / No air, no water, no erasing

- **Fact-check (2026-09-24):** corroborated. Lunar craters range from microscopic pits up to the roughly 2,500 km South Pole-Aitken basin; many basins exceed 300 km. Sources: https://science.nasa.gov/moon/lunar-craters/, https://www.lpi.usra.edu/lunar/missions/orbiter/lunar_orbiter/impact_basin/

- **Fact-check (2026-09-24):** corroborated. The mean Earth-Moon distance (semimajor axis) is 384,400 km, varying between about 363,300 km and 405,500 km. Sources: https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Lunar_distance, https://www.rmg.co.uk/stories/space-astronomy/how-far-away-moon

### ☐ `fact.astronomy.moon_far_side_lit`: unverified

> **Claim:** The far side of the Moon is not permanently dark; it receives as much sunlight as the near side.

- **Concept:** The Moon always shows us the same face (`concept.astronomy.moon_tidal_lock`)
- **Appears in:**
  - L6 “The Moon's Changing Face” · `card.astronomy.006.c3` (fact): We always see the same side of the Moon. / The Moon is tidally locked: it rotates exactly once per orbit, so the same face always points at us. Walk a circle around a chair while always facing it, and you'll have turned all the way around once too. And the 'dark side'? Not dark at all. The far side gets as much sunlight as the near side. We just never get to see it from Earth.
- **Tested by:**
  - L6 `question.astronomy.006.q2`: Why do we always see the same side of the Moon? → **It rotates once for every orbit around Earth**
  - L19 `question.astronomy.019.q3`: From Level 6: why can we see most of the Moon’s maria from Earth? → **Most maria are on the near side, which always faces Earth**
- **Fact-check (2026-09-24):** corroborated. The far side gets the same amount of sunlight as the near side over a lunar cycle; 'dark side' is a misnomer. Sources: https://www.skyatnightmagazine.com/space-science/dark-side-of-the-moon, https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Far_side_of_the_Moon

### ☐ `fact.astronomy.moon_gravity_sixth`: unverified

> **Claim:** Gravity at the Moon’s surface is about one-sixth of Earth’s, so a person would weigh about one-sixth as much there.

- **Concept:** Newton’s law of gravity (`concept.astronomy.newton_gravity`)
- **Appears in:**
  - L27 “Newton’s Gravity” · `card.astronomy.027.c2` (text): One law for everything / In 1687 Isaac Newton published his law of universal gravitation. Every object pulls on every other. The pull grows with their masses and weakens with the square of the distance: twice as far, a quarter of the pull. You'd feel the difference on the Moon, where surface gravity is only about one-sixth of Earth's, so you would weigh about one-sixth as much. Great news for your high jump. / Twice as far → ¼ the pull

  - L62 `question.astronomy.062.q3`: From Level 27: which other effect weakens with the square of distance, just like starlight? → **Gravity**
  - L65 `question.astronomy.065.q3`: From Level 27: which law lets astronomers turn a binary star’s orbit into a mass? → **Newton’s law of universal gravitation**
- **Fact-check (2026-09-24):** corroborated. Lunar surface gravity is about 1.62 m/s2, 16.6% of Earth's, so weight is about one-sixth. Sources: https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Gravitation_of_the_Moon, https://earthhow.com/moon-gravity/

### ☐ `fact.astronomy.moon_light_time`: unverified

> **Claim:** Light takes just over one second to travel from the Moon to Earth.

- **Concept:** A light-year is a distance (`concept.astronomy.light_year`)
- **Appears in:**
  - L8 “Light-Years: Measuring the Unmeasurable” · `card.astronomy.008.c3` (text): Why measure space in light? / Light is the fastest thing there is, which makes it a handy measuring tape. It crosses from the Moon to Earth in just over a second. From the Sun, it takes about 8 minutes (Level 1's slightly late Sun). So the Moon is about one light-second away and the Sun about 8 light-minutes. Past the Sun, even light needs years, and the ruler grows to match.
  - L8 “Light-Years: Measuring the Unmeasurable” · `card.astronomy.008.c4` (fact): The closest star after the Sun is Proxima Centauri, about 4.2 light-years away. / So its light takes about 4.2 years to reach us. Moon: a second. Sun: 8 minutes. Next star: more than four years. And that's the closest one; every other star is even farther. Proxima is our next-door neighbor. The street is just very, very long.
- **Tested by:**

- **Fact-check (2026-09-24):** corroborated. With an average distance of 384,400 km and light traveling at about 300,000 km/s, light takes about 1.3 seconds to get from the Moon to Earth. ## Earth’s Moon: Formation (NASA Science)

- **Source:** `source.nasa_moon_formation`

### ☐ `fact.astronomy.giant_impact`: unverified

> **Claim:** The leading explanation for the Moon’s origin is that a Mars-sized body collided with the young Earth, and the Moon formed from the debris.

- **Concept:** The giant-impact origin of the Moon (`concept.astronomy.moon_giant_impact`)
- **Appears in:**
  - L18 “Where the Moon Came From” · `card.astronomy.018.c1` (text): The Moon may be made of pieces of Earth. / The leading explanation starts with a crash between the young Earth and a whole Mars-sized world. The Moon is what the crash left behind.
  - L18 “Where the Moon Came From” · `card.astronomy.018.c2` (text): The giant impact / About 4.5 billion years ago, a Mars-sized body that scientists call Theia is thought to have slammed into the young Earth. The collision flung debris out around Earth, and that debris gathered to form the Moon. Remember the Sun's age from Level 2, about 4.6 billion years? The Moon showed up early in the story, not long after Earth itself. Earth's worst day, and it got a Moon out of it. / Collision → debris → Moon
  - L39 “Humans on the Moon” · `card.astronomy.039.c3` (text): Bringing the Moon home / The astronauts didn't come back empty-handed. Apollo brought home 382 kg of Moon rocks and soil, and scientists study them to learn how the Moon formed. Their big clue: chemically, Moon rocks are very similar to rocks from Earth's mantle. Remember the giant impact from Level 18? The Moon seems to be made of Earth's own stuff. The best souvenir ever turned out to be evidence.
- **Tested by:**
  - L18 `question.astronomy.018.q1`: What is the leading explanation for how the Moon formed? → **Debris from a giant collision with the young Earth**
  - L18 `question.astronomy.018.q3`: From Level 2: the Sun is about 4.6 billion years old. What does the Moon’s age of about 4.5 billion years suggest? → **It formed early, not long after Earth itself**
- **Fact-check (2026-09-24):** corroborated. The giant impact hypothesis (Mars-sized Theia hitting early Earth, Moon forming from debris) is the leading, widely accepted explanation. Sources: https://science.nasa.gov/moon/formation/, https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Giant-impact_hypothesis

```sh
npm run verify:record -- fact.astronomy.giant_impact source.nasa_moon_formation --status verified --by "Your Name" --quote "…"
```

### ☐ `fact.astronomy.impact_simulations`: unverified

> **Claim:** Computer simulations of a giant impact can produce a Moon with an Earth-like composition and a small iron core.

- **Concept:** Evidence from Moon rocks (`concept.astronomy.moon_rock_evidence`)
- **Appears in:**
  - L18 “Where the Moon Came From” · `card.astronomy.018.c3` (text): The clues / How do you check a crash nobody saw? Start with the Moon rocks Apollo astronauts brought back: they're chemically very similar to rocks from Earth's mantle. The Moon also has only a small iron core compared with Earth. And when scientists run computer simulations of a giant impact, out comes a Moon with an Earth-like makeup and a small iron core. Not proof, but every clue points the same way: back to Earth.
- **Tested by:**
  - L18 `question.astronomy.018.q2`: Which clue supports the idea that the Moon formed from material blasted off Earth? → **Moon rocks are chemically very similar to Earth’s mantle**
  - L39 `question.astronomy.039.q3`: From Level 18: what did Apollo samples reveal about where the Moon came from? → **Moon rocks are chemically very similar to Earth’s mantle**
- **Pre-check:** Confirm the page mentions simulations/models; otherwise attach a better source or drop the sentence.
- **Fact-check (2026-09-24):** corroborated. Published giant-impact simulations (e.g. fast-spinning proto-Earth or near-equal-mass mergers) produce iron-poor disks and Earth-like isotopic Moons, though this remains an active research problem. Sources: https://arxiv.org/pdf/1611.02769, https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Origin_of_the_Moon

```sh

```

  - L18 `question.astronomy.018.q2`: Which clue supports the idea that the Moon formed from material blasted off Earth? → **Moon rocks are chemically very similar to Earth’s mantle**
  - L39 `question.astronomy.039.q3`: From Level 18: what did Apollo samples reveal about where the Moon came from? → **Moon rocks are chemically very similar to Earth’s mantle**
- **Fact-check (2026-09-24):** corroborated. Apollo samples show oxygen and titanium isotope compositions nearly identical to Earth's mantle, though the Moon is depleted in volatiles. Sources: https://www.pnas.org/doi/10.1073/pnas.2321070121, https://science.nasa.gov/moon/formation/

### ☐ `fact.astronomy.moon_small_core`: unverified

> **Claim:** The Moon has only a small iron core compared with Earth.

- **Concept:** Evidence from Moon rocks (`concept.astronomy.moon_rock_evidence`)
- **Appears in:**
  - L18 “Where the Moon Came From” · `card.astronomy.018.c3` (text): The clues / How do you check a crash nobody saw? Start with the Moon rocks Apollo astronauts brought back: they're chemically very similar to rocks from Earth's mantle. The Moon also has only a small iron core compared with Earth. And when scientists run computer simulations of a giant impact, out comes a Moon with an Earth-like makeup and a small iron core. Not proof, but every clue points the same way: back to Earth.
- **Tested by:**
  - L18 `question.astronomy.018.q2`: Which clue supports the idea that the Moon formed from material blasted off Earth? → **Moon rocks are chemically very similar to Earth’s mantle**
  - L39 `question.astronomy.039.q3`: From Level 18: what did Apollo samples reveal about where the Moon came from? → **Moon rocks are chemically very similar to Earth’s mantle**
- **Fact-check (2026-09-24):** corroborated. The Moon's core is only about 1 to 2 percent of its mass, versus about 30 percent for Earth's. Sources: https://science.nasa.gov/moon/formation/, http://www.psrd.hawaii.edu/Sept99/MoonCore.html

## Moon Phases (NASA Science)

- **Source:** `source.nasa_moon_phases`
- **URL:** https://science.nasa.gov/moon/moon-phases/
- **License:** US_gov_public_domain · **Accessed:** 2026-09-23 · **Source record checked (URL/licence):** no

### ☐ `fact.astronomy.moon_half_lit`: unverified

> **Claim:** The Sun always lights half of the Moon; phases depend on how much of that sunlit half we can see from Earth.

- **Concept:** What causes Moon phases (`concept.astronomy.moon_phases`)
- **Appears in:**
  - L6 “The Moon's Changing Face” · `card.astronomy.006.c1` (text): The Moon doesn't change shape. You're just seeing it from a different angle. / Tonight a thin crescent, next week a full circle. The Moon seems to grow and shrink, but it's the same Moon the whole time. What changes is how you're looking at it.
  - L6 “The Moon's Changing Face” · `card.astronomy.006.c2` (text): Half lit, always / The Sun always lights up half of the Moon. As the Moon orbits Earth, we see different amounts of that sunlit half: a thin crescent, a half, the full face, then back again. Nothing is covering the Moon; only our viewing angle changes. Try it with a lamp and a ball held at arm's length, turning slowly, and you'll get phases in your living room. / One full cycle of phases: about 29.5 days
  - L20 “Checkpoint: Reading the Sky” · `card.astronomy.020.c2` (text): Spin, orbit, pull / Earth's spin gives day and night and sweeps the Sun and stars from east to west. Earth's orbit gives the year and a night sky that changes with the seasons. The Moon, circling us, brings phases, eclipses and, with its gravity, tides. And the planets wander along the ecliptic, with Mars looping back whenever Earth overtakes it. A few motions, one very busy sky. / Spin → day · Orbit → year · Moon → tides
  - L25 “Galileo’s Telescope” · `card.astronomy.025.c4` (fact): Venus has phases, just like the Moon. / Galileo watched Venus go through a full set of phases, from crescent to nearly full. Remember Level 6: phases come from seeing different amounts of a sunlit half. Ptolemy's model couldn't produce the full set. A Venus orbiting the Sun could. Second strike for the Earth-centered universe.
- **Tested by:**
  - L6 `question.astronomy.006.q1`: What causes the Moon's phases? → **How much of the Moon's sunlit half we can see**
  - L6 `question.astronomy.006.q3`: The Moon’s phases repeat about every 29.5 days. From Level 5: what drives Earth’s yearly cycle of seasons? → **Earth’s tilted axis as it orbits the Sun**
  - L7 `question.astronomy.007.q3`: From Level 6: why can a lunar eclipse only happen at full Moon? → **Only then does Earth sit between the Sun and Moon**
  - L17 `question.astronomy.017.q3`: From Level 6: spring tides happen at new and full Moon. What do those two phases have in common? → **The Sun, Earth and Moon are in a line**
  - L25 `question.astronomy.025.q3`: From Level 6: our Moon shows phases because it orbits Earth and we see varying amounts of its lit half. What did Venus’s full set of phases suggest? → **Venus orbits the Sun**
- **Fact-check (2026-09-24):** corroborated. NASA: the Sun always illuminates half of the Moon; phases reflect how much of that lit half we see from Earth. Sources: https://science.nasa.gov/moon/moon-phases/, https://spaceplace.nasa.gov/moon-phases/en/


> **Claim:** A full cycle of Moon phases takes about 29.5 days.

- **Concept:** What causes Moon phases (`concept.astronomy.moon_phases`)
- **Appears in:**

As the Moon orbits Earth, we see different amounts of that sunlit half: a thin crescent, a half, the full face, then back again. Nothing is covering the Moon; only our viewing angle changes. Try it with a lamp and a ball held at arm's length, turning slowly, and you'll get phases in your living room. / One full cycle of phases: about 29.5 days
- **Tested by:**
  - L6 `question.astronomy.006.q1`: What causes the Moon's phases? → **How much of the Moon's sunlit half we can see**
  - L6 `question.astronomy.006.q3`: The Moon’s phases repeat about every 29.5 days. From Level 5: what drives Earth’s yearly cycle of seasons? → **Earth’s tilted axis as it orbits the Sun**

### ☐ `fact.astronomy.neap_tides`: unverified

> **Claim:** Neap tides, the smallest tidal ranges, happen around first and last quarter Moon, when the Sun and Moon pull at right angles.

- **Concept:** Spring and neap tides (`concept.astronomy.spring_neap_tides`)
- **Appears in:**
  - L17 “Tides: The Moon’s Pull on the Ocean” · `card.astronomy.017.c3` (text): The Sun joins in / The Sun pulls on the oceans too, just more weakly than the Moon. Around new and full Moon, the Sun, Moon and Earth line up and their pulls combine: extra-high highs and extra-low lows, called spring tides. Around the quarter Moons, the Sun and Moon pull at right angles and the tides are gentler: neap tides. Teamwork makes big tides.
- **Tested by:**
  - L17 `question.astronomy.017.q3`: From Level 6: spring tides happen at new and full Moon. What do those two phases have in common? → **The Sun, Earth and Moon are in a line**
- **Fact-check (2026-09-24):** corroborated. Neap tides have the smallest tidal range and occur at first and last quarter, when Sun and Moon are at right angles to Earth. Sources: https://science.nasa.gov/moon/tides/, https://oceanservice.noaa.gov/facts/springtide.html

### ☐ `fact.astronomy.spring_not_season`: unverified

### ☐ `fact.astronomy.tides_moon_gravity`: unverified

> **Claim:** Ocean tides are caused mainly by the Moon’s gravity, with a smaller contribution from the Sun.

- **Concept:** The Moon drives the tides (`concept.astronomy.tides_moon_gravity`)
- **Appears in:**
  - L17 “Tides: The Moon’s Pull on the Ocean” · `card.astronomy.017.c1` (text): The Moon moves oceans. Twice a day. / Every beach you've ever visited is being tugged by the Moon. The odd part is that most coasts get two high tides a day, not one.
  - L17 “Tides: The Moon’s Pull on the Ocean” · `card.astronomy.017.c2` (text): Two bulges, not one / The Moon's gravity pulls on Earth's oceans and stretches them into two bulges: one facing the Moon and one on the opposite side of Earth. Earth spins through both bulges, so most coastlines get two high tides and two low tides about every 24 hours and 50 minutes. High tide isn't the sea coming to visit. It's your beach passing under a bulge. / Two bulges → two high tides
  - L20 “Checkpoint: Reading the Sky” · `card.astronomy.020.c2` (text): Spin, orbit, pull / Earth's spin gives day and night and sweeps the Sun and stars from east to west. Earth's orbit gives the year and a night sky that changes with the seasons. The Moon, circling us, brings phases, eclipses and, with its gravity, tides. And the planets wander along the ecliptic, with Mars looping back whenever Earth overtakes it. A few motions, one very busy sky. / Spin → day · Orbit → year · Moon → tides
  - L17 “Tides: The Moon’s Pull on the Ocean” · `card.astronomy.017.c3` (text): The Sun joins in / The Sun pulls on the oceans too, just more weakly than the Moon. Around new and full Moon, the Sun, Moon and Earth line up and their pulls combine: extra-high highs and extra-low lows, called spring tides. Around the quarter Moons, the Sun and Moon pull at right angles and the tides are gentler: neap tides. Teamwork makes big tides.
  - L27 “Newton’s Gravity” · `card.astronomy.027.c3` (text): Heaven and Earth, united / Here's the big leap. The gravity that makes an apple fall on Earth is the same force that keeps the Moon in orbit and the planets circling the Sun. One force, from your kitchen to the edge of the solar system. It also explained why Kepler's laws work, turning a description into an explanation. It even reaches the beach: the Moon's gravity pulling on the oceans is what raises the tides (Level 17).
  - L47 “Ocean Worlds: Europa and Enceladus” · `card.astronomy.047.c3` (text): Kept warm by squeezing / The heat comes from gravity. As these moons orbit, their giant planets squeeze and flex them: Jupiter works on Europa, Saturn on Enceladus. All that flexing heats their interiors enough to keep the water liquid. It's a cousin of Level 17's tides, where the Moon's gravity stretches Earth's oceans. Water, energy and the right chemistry are key ingredients for life, which is why NASA launched Europa Clipper in 2024 to study Europa. / Gravity flexes the moon → heat → liquid ocean
- **Tested by:**
  - L17 `question.astronomy.017.q1`: What mainly causes ocean tides? → **The Moon’s gravity**
  - L17 `question.astronomy.017.q2`: Why do most coasts get two high tides a day, not one? → **Earth spins through two tidal bulges each day**
  - L20 `question.astronomy.020.q5`: Chapter 2 recap: which pairing of cause and effect is correct? → **Earth’s spin → stars rising in the east**
  - L27 `question.astronomy.027.q3`: From Level 17: what connects Newton’s law to the ocean tides? → **The Moon’s gravity pulls on Earth’s oceans**
  - L47 `question.astronomy.047.q3`: From Level 17: the Moon’s gravity raises tides on Earth. How is that like what heats Europa? → **Both are gravity stretching a world as bodies orbit**
- **Fact-check (2026-09-24):** corroborated. NOAA: tides are caused mainly by the Moon; the Sun's tide-generating force is about half the Moon's. Sources: https://oceanservice.noaa.gov/education/tutorial_tides/tides02_cause.html, https://science.nasa.gov/moon/tides/

Take the red out of sunlight, and what bounces back to your eye looks blue. Two very different oddballs, painted with the same trick.

- **Fact-check (2026-09-24):** corroborated. Methane in Uranus's and Neptune's atmospheres absorbs red light and reflects blue, giving both their blue color; haze explains their shade difference. Sources: https://science.nasa.gov/solar-system/why-uranus-and-neptune-are-different-colors/

### ☐ `fact.astronomy.neptune_30_au`: unverified

That number is too big to feel, so use light instead. Sunlight reaches Earth in about 8 minutes. To reach Neptune, it needs about 4 hours. Leave the Sun with the light at breakfast, and you'd arrive at Neptune around lunch. / 1 AU → 8 minutes · 30 AU → ~4 hours

Not a doughnut like the Kuiper Belt: a shell, wrapped around everything, like the peel on an orange. It may reach as far as 100,000 astronomical units from the Sun. Neptune orbits at about 30 AU, so the Oort Cloud's outer edge could be more than 3,000 times farther out than the last planet. / Up to ~100,000 AU

  - L29 “New Planets: Uranus and Neptune” · `card.astronomy.029.c3` (text): Found by mathematics / Then Uranus misbehaved. It didn't quite follow its predicted path, as if something unseen were tugging on it. Newton's law says every object pulls on every other, so a mystery tug means a mystery puller. Working from those unexplained tugs, Urbain Le Verrier worked out where that unseen planet should be. In 1846 Johann Galle looked there and found Neptune, within about 1° of the prediction. A planet pinned down with math before anyone had knowingly seen it. / Gravity math → new planet
- **Tested by:**

  - L29 `question.astronomy.029.q3`: From Level 27: which law explains the mystery tug on Uranus that led to Neptune? → **Newton’s law of universal gravitation**
- **Pre-check:** Confirm “within about 1°” on the page.
- **Fact-check (2026-09-24):** corroborated. On 23 September 1846 Galle found Neptune the night he received Le Verrier's letter, within about 1 degree of the predicted position. Sources: https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Johann_Gottfried_Galle, https://earthsky.org/human-world/today-in-science-discovery-of-neptune/

### ☐ `fact.astronomy.neutron_star_dense`: unverified

> **Claim:** Neutron star material is so dense that a teaspoonful would weigh billions of tons on Earth.

- **Concept:** Neutron stars (`concept.astronomy.neutron_stars`)
- **Appears in:**
  - L71 “Neutron Stars” · `card.astronomy.071.c3` (text): Crushed to neutrons / How do you fit a sun into a city? Gravity crushes the collapsing core so hard that its matter becomes mostly neutrons, packed tightly together. Remember Level 68’s white dwarf, where a teaspoon weighed several tons? A teaspoon of neutron star would weigh billions of tons on Earth. Your sugar spoon has never worked so hard. And many neutron stars spin many times per second, with magnetic fields far stronger than Earth’s. Hold on to that spin.
  - L80 “Checkpoint: Extreme Objects and Other Worlds” · `card.astronomy.080.c2` (comparison): What’s left when a star dies: White dwarf (About the size of Earth; A teaspoon: several tons) vs Neutron star (About the size of a city; A teaspoon: billions of tons) vs Black hole (No surface at all; Only an event horizon, the point of no return)
- **Tested by:**
  - L71 `question.astronomy.071.q2`: What makes a neutron star so incredibly dense? → **Gravity crushes its matter into tightly packed neutrons**
  - L71 `question.astronomy.071.q3`: From Level 68: a white dwarf teaspoon weighs several tons. How does a neutron star compare? → **A teaspoon weighs billions of tons**
  - L80 `question.astronomy.080.q1`: Which stellar remnant is about the size of a city? → **A neutron star**
- **Pre-check:** Figures vary by source; the card says “billions of tons”.
- **Fact-check (2026-09-24):** corroborated. NASA Goddard says a teaspoon of neutron star interior would weigh more than a billion tons on Earth; other estimates run to several billion, so 'billions of tons' is within range. Sources: https://www.facebook.com/NASAGoddard/videos/what-is-a-neutron-star/10156141229860898/, https://www.nasa.gov/centers-and-facilities/goddard/new-nasa-mission-to-study-mysterious-neutron-stars-aid-in-deep-space-navigation/

### ☐ `fact.astronomy.neutron_star_origin`: unverified

> **Claim:** A neutron star is the collapsed core left behind when a massive star explodes as a supernova.

The explosion scattered the star’s outer layers, and its collapsed core became the pulsar, still spinning at the center. Nearly a thousand years later, the lighthouse is still on.

### ☐ `fact.astronomy.pluto_heart`: unverified

> **Claim:** In 2015 NASA’s New Horizons flew past Pluto and revealed a huge heart-shaped region covered in nitrogen ice.

- **Concept:** Dwarf planets and Pluto (`concept.astronomy.dwarf_planets`)
- **Appears in:**
  - L51 “Pluto and the Dwarf Planets” · `card.astronomy.051.c4` (fact): Pluto has a heart. / In 2015 NASA's New Horizons flew past Pluto and revealed a huge heart-shaped region covered in nitrogen ice. That's nine years after Pluto was told it wasn't a planet, and it answered by showing us its heart. No hard feelings, apparently.
- **Fact-check (2026-09-24):** corroborated. New Horizons' 2015 flyby revealed Tombaugh Regio, the 'heart'. Its western lobe, Sputnik Planitia, is a vast plain of nitrogen ice; the eastern lobe carries a thinner nitrogen-rich frost. A fair simplification. Sources: https://news.arizona.edu/news/how-pluto-got-its-heart, https://www.kqed.org/science/1956964/a-valentine-from-space-plutos-heart-beats-to-the-tune-of-the-winds

Neptune orbits at about 30 AU, so the Oort Cloud's outer edge could be more than 3,000 times farther out than the last planet. / Up to ~100,000 AU

### ☐ `fact.astronomy.oort_unseen`: unverified

> **Claim:** No object in the Oort Cloud has ever been directly observed there; its existence is inferred from long-period comets.

Astronomers infer it from comets that take thousands of years or more to orbit the Sun: trace their long paths back, and they point to a distant shell all around us. It's like guessing there's a bakery nearby because people keep walking past with fresh bread. The comets that return every few years come from the Kuiper Belt. The ones that take thousands of years come from here.

### ☐ `fact.astronomy.pluto_1930`: unverified

You've met Ceres before: in Level 45 it was the largest object in the asteroid belt. It's round and it orbits the Sun, but it shares its orbit with the rest of the belt. Same test, same result. ### ☐ `fact.astronomy.pluto_heart`: unverified

- **Fact-check (2026-09-24):** corroborated. New Horizons' 2015 flyby revealed Tombaugh Regio, the 'heart'. Sources: https://news.arizona.edu/news/how-pluto-got-its-heart, https://www.kqed.org/science/1956964/a-valentine-from-space-plutos-heart-beats-to-the-tune-of-the-winds

### ☐ `fact.astronomy.no_south_pole_star`: unverified

The nearest naked-eye star to the south celestial pole, Sigma Octantis, is only magnitude 5.5 and of little use for navigation; observers use the Southern Cross to locate the pole. As Earth spins, every other star circles around it while Polaris stays nearly fixed, like the hub of a turning wheel. Face it and you're facing north. One surprise: it isn't the brightest star in the sky. That's Sirius. Polaris is famous for where it sits, not how it shines. / Polaris = north
- **Tested by:**
  - L14 `question.astronomy.014.q1`: Why does Polaris barely move across the night sky? → **It lies almost directly above Earth’s North Pole**
  - L20 `question.astronomy.020.q2`: Which star stays almost fixed above the North Pole? → **Polaris**
- **Fact-check (2026-09-24):** corroborated. Polaris (magnitude about 2.0) ranks around 48th in brightness; Sirius (magnitude -1.46) is the brightest star in the night sky. Sources: https://www.farmersalmanac.com/north-star-brightest-star, https://www.sciencing.com/1863568/myth-north-star-isnt-brightest-star-sky/

```sh
npm run verify:record -- fact.astronomy.polaris_not_brightest source.nasa_polaris --status verified --by "Your Name" --quote "…"
```

### ☐ `fact.astronomy.precession_26000`: unverified

> **Claim:** Earth’s axis slowly wobbles, tracing a circle about every 26,000 years, so Polaris won’t always be the pole star.

- **Concept:** Earth’s wobble changes the pole star (`concept.astronomy.precession`)
- **Appears in:**
  - L14 “The North Star” · `card.astronomy.014.c4` (fact): Polaris won't be the North Star forever. / Earth's axis slowly wobbles like a spinning top, tracing a circle about every 26,000 years, so over the ages the pole points at different stars. The Southern Hemisphere already knows the feeling: it has no bright pole star at all, so navigators there have used the Southern Cross to find south.
  - L98 “Integration: Futures of the Sun, Earth and Universe” · `card.astronomy.098.c2` (timeline): The long forecast: Over ~26,000 years: Earth’s axis wobbles through a full circle, so Polaris won’t stay the pole star; In ~1 billion years: A slowly brightening Sun makes Earth too hot for oceans; In ~4.5 billion years, maybe: The Milky Way and Andromeda may merge; a 2025 study put it at about a coin flip; In ~5 billion years: The Sun swells into a red giant, later becoming a white dwarf
- **Tested by:**
  - L98 `question.astronomy.098.q2`: Why won’t Polaris always be the North Star? → **Earth’s axis slowly wobbles in a circle**
- **Fact-check (2026-09-24):** corroborated. Axial precession makes Earth's axis trace a circle in about 26,000 years (25,770); Vega will be near the pole in about 12,000 to 13,000 years. Sources: https://www.space.com/stargazing/has-polaris-always-been-the-north-star-how-earths-26-000-year-cycle-changes-the-pole-star, https://www.pas.rochester.edu/~blackman/ast104/precession.html

The path the Sun appears to follow across the sky over a year is called the ecliptic. The zodiac constellations lie along it, plus a 13th that tradition leaves out: Ophiuchus. Sorry, Ophiuchus. / Ecliptic = the Sun's path over a year
  - L15 “The Ecliptic: The Planets’ Highway” · `card.astronomy.015.c1` (text): The planets don't roam the whole sky. They stick to one narrow road. / The Sun, the Moon and every planet keep to the same slim strip of sky. It's no coincidence. It's a clue to the shape of the solar system.

### ☐ `fact.astronomy.planet_wanderer`: unverified

> **Claim:** The word “planet” comes from the ancient Greek word for “wanderer,” because planets drift against the background stars.

- **Concept:** Planets wander against the stars (`concept.astronomy.planets_wander`)
- **Appears in:**
  - L16 “Wanderers: How Planets Move Across the Sky” · `card.astronomy.016.c2` (text): The wanderers / Night after night, the stars hold their patterns. Planets don't: they slowly shift position against the background stars as they orbit the Sun, always keeping to the ecliptic road from Level 15. The ancient Greeks noticed these drifting lights, and the word "planet" comes from their word for "wanderer." The stars stay put; the planets go for a stroll.
  - L20 “Checkpoint: Reading the Sky” · `card.astronomy.020.c2` (text): Spin, orbit, pull / Earth's spin gives day and night and sweeps the Sun and stars from east to west. Earth's orbit gives the year and a night sky that changes with the seasons. The Moon, circling us, brings phases, eclipses and, with its gravity, tides. And the planets wander along the ecliptic, with Mars looping back whenever Earth overtakes it. A few motions, one very busy sky. / Spin → day · Orbit → year · Moon → tides
- **Tested by:**
  - L16 `question.astronomy.016.q1`: What does the word “planet” originally mean? → **Wanderer**
- **Fact-check (2026-09-24):** corroborated. 'Planet' derives from Greek planetes, 'wanderer', since planets move against the fixed background stars. Sources: https://www.etymonline.com/word/planet, https://fscj.pressbooks.pub/introductionastronomy/chapter/the-wanderers/

### ☐ `fact.astronomy.planets_near_ecliptic`: unverified

> **Claim:** The Moon and planets always appear close to the ecliptic because their orbits lie in nearly the same flat plane.

- **Concept:** The ecliptic (`concept.astronomy.ecliptic`)
- **Appears in:**
  - L15 “The Ecliptic: The Planets’ Highway” · `card.astronomy.015.c1` (text): The planets don't roam the whole sky. They stick to one narrow road. / The Sun, the Moon and every planet keep to the same slim strip of sky. It's no coincidence. It's a clue to the shape of the solar system.
  - L15 “The Ecliptic: The Planets’ Highway” · `card.astronomy.015.c3` (text): A flat solar system / Why does everything use the same road? The planets all orbit the Sun in nearly the same flat plane, and the Moon's orbit lies close to it too. Hold a dinner plate edge-on at eye level and everything on it lines up in a thin line. We're looking at the solar system edge-on from inside, and that line is the ecliptic. So a bright "star" near where the Sun set may well be a planet.
  - L16 “Wanderers: How Planets Move Across the Sky” · `card.astronomy.016.c2` (text): The wanderers / Night after night, the stars hold their patterns. Planets don't: they slowly shift position against the background stars as they orbit the Sun, always keeping to the ecliptic road from Level 15. The ancient Greeks noticed these drifting lights, and the word "planet" comes from their word for "wanderer." The stars stay put; the planets go for a stroll.
  - L20 “Checkpoint: Reading the Sky” · `card.astronomy.020.c2` (text): Spin, orbit, pull / Earth's spin gives day and night and sweeps the Sun and stars from east to west. Earth's orbit gives the year and a night sky that changes with the seasons. The Moon, circling us, brings phases, eclipses and, with its gravity, tides. And the planets wander along the ecliptic, with Mars looping back whenever Earth overtakes it. A few motions, one very busy sky. / Spin → day · Orbit → year · Moon → tides
  - L59 “How the Solar System Formed” · `card.astronomy.059.c2` (text): A spinning cloud collapses / About 4.6 billion years ago, a cloud of gas and dust began collapsing under its own gravity. As it shrank, it spun faster and flattened into a disk, like pizza dough spun in the air. The young Sun formed at the center. That's why the planets still orbit in nearly the same plane, and why they all stay close to the ecliptic from Level 15. The solar system still has the shape of that one flat, spinning disk. / Cloud → spinning disk → Sun + planets
- **Tested by:**
  - L15 `question.astronomy.015.q2`: Why do the planets always appear near the ecliptic? → **Their orbits lie in nearly the same flat plane as Earth’s**
  - L59 `question.astronomy.059.q3`: From Level 15: the planets all lie near the ecliptic. What does the formation story say about why? → **They formed from one flat, spinning disk**
- **Fact-check (2026-09-24):** corroborated. Planetary orbits are inclined only a few degrees (Mercury about 7 degrees) and the Moon's orbit 5.1 degrees, so they stay near the ecliptic. Sources: https://skyandtelescope.org/astronomy-resources/what-is-the-ecliptic/, https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Ecliptic

```sh
npm run verify:record -- fact.astronomy.planets_near_ecliptic source.nasa_skywatching_planets --status verified --by "Your Name" --quote "…"
```

### ☐ `fact.astronomy.retrograde_apparent`: unverified

> **Claim:** Retrograde motion is only apparent: the planets never actually reverse direction in their orbits.

- **Concept:** Retrograde motion (`concept.astronomy.retrograde_motion`)
- **Appears in:**
  - L16 “Wanderers: How Planets Move Across the Sky” · `card.astronomy.016.c1` (text): Every few years, Mars seems to stop, reverse and loop back across the sky. / It never actually turns around. You're watching from a moving planet, and that changes what you see.
  - L16 “Wanderers: How Planets Move Across the Sky” · `card.astronomy.016.c4` (fact): Retrograde motion is only apparent. / No planet ever reverses direction in its orbit. The backwards loop is a change in our viewpoint, not in the planet's path. Mars has no idea it's doing anything strange.
  - L23 “An Earth-Centered Universe” · `card.astronomy.023.c2` (text): Ptolemy's universe / Around 150 CE, Ptolemy described it in a book later known as the Almagest: Earth motionless at the center, with the Sun, Moon, planets and stars all circling it. One snag: planets sometimes seem to go backwards (Level 16's retrograde motion). Ptolemy's fix was to put each planet on a small circle, an epicycle, that itself moved around Earth. Think of a teacup ride: you spin on a platform that is also spinning. / Circles on circles
- **Tested by:**
  - L23 `question.astronomy.023.q3`: From Level 16: how did Ptolemy’s model explain retrograde motion? → **Planets riding epicycles as they circled Earth**
- **Fact-check (2026-09-24):** corroborated. Apparent retrograde motion results from Earth overtaking or being overtaken by other planets; planets do not physically reverse. Sources: https://starchild.gsfc.nasa.gov/docs/StarChild/questions/question46.html, https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Apparent_retrograde_motion


### ☐ `fact.astronomy.retrograde_explained`: unverified

> **Claim:** Mars and the other outer planets sometimes appear to move backwards against the stars for a while when Earth, on its faster inner orbit, overtakes them.

- **Concept:** Retrograde motion (`concept.astronomy.retrograde_motion`)
- **Appears in:**
  - L16 “Wanderers: How Planets Move Across the Sky” · `card.astronomy.016.c1` (text): Every few years, Mars seems to stop, reverse and loop back across the sky. / It never actually turns around. You're watching from a moving planet, and that changes what you see.
  - L16 “Wanderers: How Planets Move Across the Sky” · `card.astronomy.016.c3` (text): Overtaking on the inside lane / Earth orbits closer to the Sun than Mars does, and it moves faster. Every so often Earth, on its faster inner orbit, catches up with Mars and overtakes it. For a while, Mars appears to slide backwards against the stars, then it carries on forward. You've seen this on the highway: pass a slower car and it seems to drift backwards, though you're both going forward. / Retrograde = an overtaking illusion
  - L20 “Checkpoint: Reading the Sky” · `card.astronomy.020.c2` (text): Spin, orbit, pull / Earth's spin gives day and night and sweeps the Sun and stars from east to west. Earth's orbit gives the year and a night sky that changes with the seasons. The Moon, circling us, brings phases, eclipses and, with its gravity, tides. And the planets wander along the ecliptic, with Mars looping back whenever Earth overtakes it. A few motions, one very busy sky. / Spin → day · Orbit → year · Moon → tides
  - L23 “An Earth-Centered Universe” · `card.astronomy.023.c2` (text): Ptolemy's universe / Around 150 CE, Ptolemy described it in a book later known as the Almagest: Earth motionless at the center, with the Sun, Moon, planets and stars all circling it. One snag: planets sometimes seem to go backwards (Level 16's retrograde motion). Ptolemy's fix was to put each planet on a small circle, an epicycle, that itself moved around Earth. Think of a teacup ride: you spin on a platform that is also spinning. / Circles on circles
  - L24 “Copernicus Moves the Sun” · `card.astronomy.024.c3` (text): Loops without epicycles / In the Sun-centered model, retrograde motion is simply Earth overtaking the slower outer planets, like passing a slower car on the highway (Level 16). No extra circles needed to cause it. But Copernicus kept one old habit: he assumed perfectly circular orbits. Real orbits aren't perfect circles, so his model still needed some small extra circles of its own to match observations. Old habits die hard.
- **Tested by:**
  - L16 `question.astronomy.016.q2`: Why does Mars sometimes appear to move backwards across the sky? → **Earth, on its faster inner orbit, is overtaking it**
  - L16 `question.astronomy.016.q3`: From Level 3: which of these planets can show retrograde motion caused by Earth overtaking it? → **Mars**
  - L20 `question.astronomy.020.q4`: Mars appears to move backwards for a while. What’s really happening? → **Earth is overtaking Mars on its faster inner orbit**
  - L23 `question.astronomy.023.q3`: From Level 16: how did Ptolemy’s model explain retrograde motion? → **Planets riding epicycles as they circled Earth**
- **Fact-check (2026-09-24):** corroborated. Apparent retrograde motion of Mars and other outer planets occurs when faster-moving Earth overtakes them. Sources: https://earthsky.org/astronomy-essentials/what-is-retrograde-motion/, https://apod.nasa.gov/rjn/apod/ap080511.html

### ☐ `fact.astronomy.leap_year_reason`: unverified

> **Claim:** Calendar years have 365 days but an orbit takes about 365.25 days, so calendars add an extra day (February 29) about every four years to stay in step with the seasons.

- **Concept:** Why leap years exist (`concept.astronomy.leap_years`)
- **Appears in:**
  - L12 “A Year Around the Sun” · `card.astronomy.012.c3` (text): Where the quarter-days go / Calendars count 365 days, but each orbit takes about 365.25. That spare quarter of a day looks harmless, yet it piles up: after four years, the calendar is a whole day behind Earth. So about every four years we add February 29 to catch up and keep the calendar in step with the seasons. Leap day is the calendar settling its debts.
- **Tested by:**
  - L12 `question.astronomy.012.q2`: Why do we add a leap day about every four years? → **Each orbit takes about 365.25 days, not 365**
- **Fact-check (2026-09-24):** corroborated. NASA Space Place: an orbit takes about 365.25 days, so a day is added about every four years (Feb 29) to keep the calendar in step. Sources: https://spaceplace.nasa.gov/leap-year/en/, https://www.britannica.com/science/leap-year-calendar

## What Causes the Seasons? (NASA Science)

- **Source:** `source.nasa_spaceplace_seasons`
- **URL:** https://spaceplace.nasa.gov/seasons/
- **License:** US_gov_public_domain · **Accessed:** 2026-09-23 · **Source record checked (URL/licence):** no

### ☐ `fact.astronomy.earth_axial_tilt`: unverified

> **Claim:** Earth's axis is tilted about 23.5 degrees, and this tilt causes the seasons.

- **Concept:** Seasons come from Earth's tilt (`concept.astronomy.seasons_tilt`)
- **Appears in:**
  - L5 “Why We Have Seasons” · `card.astronomy.005.c3` (text): It's the tilt / Earth's axis is tilted about 23.5°. When your half of the planet leans toward the Sun, sunlight hits the ground more directly and days last longer, so more energy lands on each patch of ground. That's summer. Lean away, and the same sunlight arrives at a slant and spreads thin, like a flashlight beam tipped across a table. That's winter. Same Sun, different angle. / Tilt → angle of sunlight → season
  - L5 “Why We Have Seasons” · `card.astronomy.005.c4` (text): Opposite seasons / Here's the proof it's the lean. The two hemispheres tilt opposite ways, so they take turns. In December, New York shivers while Sydney has summer. In July it flips: summer up north, winter in Australia. Same planet, same trip around the Sun, opposite seasons. Somewhere on Earth, someone is always wearing a scarf.
  - L49 “The Tilted and the Windy: Uranus and Neptune” · `card.astronomy.049.c2` (text): A planet on its side / Back in Level 5, Earth's lean of about 23.5° was enough to give you summer and winter. Uranus is tilted about 98°, so it essentially rolls around the Sun on its side. That makes for extreme seasons: each pole gets about 42 years of continuous sunlight, followed by 42 years of darkness. Be born at the start of a polar summer, and you'd be middle-aged by sunset. / Tilt: ~98°
- **Tested by:**
  - L5 `question.astronomy.005.q1`: What mainly causes Earth's seasons? → **The tilt of Earth's axis**
  - L6 `question.astronomy.006.q3`: The Moon’s phases repeat about every 29.5 days. From Level 5: what drives Earth’s yearly cycle of seasons? → **Earth’s tilted axis as it orbits the Sun**
  - L12 `question.astronomy.012.q3`: From Level 5: seasons repeat every year. What happens during one orbit to cause them? → **Each hemisphere tilts toward, then away from, the Sun**
  - L49 `question.astronomy.049.q3`: From Level 5: Earth’s 23.5° tilt gives us seasons. What does Uranus’s ~98° tilt do? → **It gives extreme seasons, with 42-year polar days**
  - L100 `question.astronomy.100.q2`: Earth is closest to the Sun in early January. Why is January winter in the Northern Hemisphere? → **The north is tilted away from the Sun then**
- **Fact-check (2026-09-24):** corroborated. Earth's axis is tilted about 23.5 degrees (23.44) to its orbital plane, and the tilt is the reason for the seasons. Sources: https://www.weather.gov/fsd/season, https://education.nationalgeographic.org/resource/axis/

```sh
npm run verify:record -- fact.astronomy.earth_axial_tilt source.nasa_spaceplace_seasons --status verified --by "Your Name" --quote "…"
```

Somewhere on Earth, someone is always wearing a scarf.

- **Appears in:**
  - L5 “Why We Have Seasons” · `card.astronomy.005.c3` (text): It's the tilt / Earth's axis is tilted about 23.5°. When your half of the planet leans toward the Sun, sunlight hits the ground more directly and days last longer, so more energy lands on each patch of ground. That's summer. Lean away, and the same sunlight arrives at a slant and spreads thin, like a flashlight beam tipped across a table. That's winter. Same Sun, different angle. / Tilt → angle of sunlight → season
