  - L6 `question.astronomy.006.q3`: The Moon’s phases repeat about every 29.5 days. From Level 5: what drives Earth’s yearly cycle of seasons? → **Earth’s tilted axis as it orbits the Sun**

## Stars (NASA Science)

- **Source:** `source.nasa_star_life_cycle`
- **URL:** https://science.nasa.gov/universe/stars/
- **License:** US_gov_public_domain · **Accessed:** 2026-09-23 · **Source record checked (URL/licence):** no

### ☐ `fact.astronomy.cosmic_recycling`: unverified

> **Claim:** Matter is recycled across cosmic history: gas from the Big Bang formed stars, stars forged heavier elements and returned them to space, and that enriched gas formed new stars, planets and eventually people.

- **Concept:** From atoms to galaxies (`concept.astronomy.cosmic_recycling`)
- **Appears in:**
  - L96 “Integration: From Atoms to Galaxies” · `card.astronomy.096.c1` (text): The atoms in your body have been on a 13.8-billion-year journey. / Levels 91 to 93 made the universe’s first ingredients and its first stars. Now follow those ingredients all the way to you.
  - L96 “Integration: From Atoms to Galaxies” · `card.astronomy.096.c3` (text): The great cycle / Think of the galaxy as a kitchen that never throws out leftovers. Stars fuse light elements into heavier ones, just as the Sun turns hydrogen into helium. Massive stars end in supernovae that blast those elements out, scattering them into space. New stars and planets form from that enriched gas, and the cycle repeats. Every generation of stars cooks with what the last one left behind. / Gas → stars → elements → new stars
  - L93 “The First Stars and Galaxies” · `card.astronomy.093.c3` (text): Giants of two elements / The first stars had a short ingredient list: only hydrogen and helium, since that’s nearly all the Big Bang had made. They’re thought to have been very massive and short-lived, the rock stars of the early universe. When they died, they made the first heavier elements and scattered them into space, seeding the gas that later stars and planets formed from. Live fast, die young, leave behind the makings of planets.
  - L96 “Integration: From Atoms to Galaxies” · `card.astronomy.096.c2` (timeline): From the Big Bang to you: First few minutes: Hydrogen, helium and a trace of lithium form; A few hundred million years: The first stars light up; Billions of years: Generations of stars forge heavier elements and scatter them into space; 4.6 billion years ago: The Sun and planets form from that enriched gas
- **Tested by:**
  - L96 `question.astronomy.096.q1`: Which elements did the Big Bang itself produce? → **Hydrogen, helium and a trace of lithium**
  - L96 `question.astronomy.096.q2`: Which sequence is in the right order? → **Hydrogen forms → first stars → the Sun forms**
  - L96 `question.astronomy.096.q3`: From Level 69: the iron in your blood was made inside stars. How did it reach the cloud that formed the solar system? → **Dying stars scattered it into space**
  - L100 `question.astronomy.100.q10`: Which is the best summary of where Earth’s iron came from? → **Forged in stars, scattered when they died**
- **Fact-check (2026-09-24):** corroborated. NASA: apart from Big Bang hydrogen and helium, elements were made in stars and supernovae, enriched gas clouds and were recycled into new stars, planets and life. Sources: https://science.nasa.gov/astrobiology/learning-resources/alp/are-we-really-made-of-star-stuff/, https://www.planetary.org/articles/were-made-of-starstuff-what-does-that-mean

### ☐ `fact.astronomy.fate_by_mass`: unverified

> **Claim:** A star’s final fate depends mainly on its mass: Sun-like stars end as white dwarfs, while the most massive stars explode and leave behind neutron stars or black holes.

- **Concept:** Mass decides a star’s fate (`concept.astronomy.stellar_fates`)
- **Appears in:**
  - L70 “Checkpoint: The Lives of Stars” · `card.astronomy.070.c2` (text): Two paths / A star’s final fate depends mainly on its mass. A Sun-like star takes the gentle road: it swells into a red giant, sheds a planetary nebula and settles down as a white dwarf. A star far more massive than the Sun takes the loud road: it explodes as a supernova and leaves behind something much stranger, a neutron star or a black hole. Same universe, very different retirement plans. / Sun-like → white dwarf · Massive → neutron star or black hole
  - L70 “Checkpoint: The Lives of Stars” · `card.astronomy.070.c1` (text): Chapter 7 finale. One number decides a star’s whole life story. / That number is its mass. Everything in this chapter, from nursery to final glow, follows from it.
  - L73 “Black Holes” · `card.astronomy.073.c2` (text): Gravity wins / A black hole is a region of space where gravity is so strong that nothing, not even light, can escape. In Level 37’s terms, its escape velocity is faster than the speed of light, and nothing can go that fast. That’s why it’s black: no light comes out for you to see. Stellar black holes form when the core of a very massive star collapses at the end of its life. Same kind of ending as a neutron star, with an even stranger leftover.
  - L80 “Checkpoint: Extreme Objects and Other Worlds” · `card.astronomy.080.c1` (text): Chapter 8 finale. From dead stars to living worlds. / Level 70 said mass decides how a star ends. Here’s what each ending leaves behind, and how we learned to find things that barely want to be found.
- **Tested by:**
  - L70 `question.astronomy.070.q2`: How will a Sun-like star end its life? → **As a white dwarf**
  - L70 `question.astronomy.070.q4`: What decides whether a star ends as a white dwarf or explodes as a supernova? → **Its mass**
  - L100 `question.astronomy.100.q7`: A star far more massive than the Sun forms. Compared with the Sun, what should you expect? → **It burns out sooner and may explode**
- **Fact-check (2026-09-24):** corroborated. Low and intermediate mass stars leave white dwarfs; stars above about 8 solar masses explode as supernovae leaving neutron stars or black holes. Sources: https://www.britannica.com/science/The-Life-Cycle-Of-A-Star, https://www.cfa.harvard.edu/research/topic/neutron-stars-and-white-dwarfs

### ☐ `fact.astronomy.star_color_temperature`: unverified

> **Claim:** A star's color shows its surface temperature: blue stars are the hottest and red stars the coolest.

- **Concept:** A star's color is its temperature (`concept.astronomy.star_color_temperature`)
- **Appears in:**
  - L9 “The Life of a Star” · `card.astronomy.009.c2` (text): Color is temperature / Look closely and stars have colors, and each color is a thermometer reading. Blue stars are the hottest; red stars are the coolest. That's backwards from your kitchen tap, where red means hot. The Sun sits in the middle: it shines white from space, and our air scatters away some of its blue light, so it looks yellowish from the ground. / Blue = hot · Red = cool
  - L63 “The H–R Diagram” · `card.astronomy.063.c2` (text): Luminosity versus temperature / The Hertzsprung–Russell diagram, or H–R diagram, plots stars by luminosity and surface temperature. More luminous stars sit higher up. Hotter stars sit farther left, which feels like a map with west on the right. Remember Level 9: blue stars are the hottest, so they land on the left and red ones on the right. Ejnar Hertzsprung and Henry Norris Russell developed the chart independently in the early 1900s. Two people, one great idea. / Up = more luminous · Left = hotter
- **Tested by:**
  - L9 `question.astronomy.009.q1`: A star looks blue-white. What does that tell you? → **It's hotter than the Sun**
  - L63 `question.astronomy.063.q3`: From Level 9: a blue-white star is hotter than a red one. On the H–R diagram, which side is it on? → **The left side, with hotter stars**
- **Fact-check (2026-09-24):** corrected. Star color indicates surface temperature, but the hottest (O-type) stars are blue; blue-white is the A-type class, cooler than O and B stars. Red stars are coolest. Sources: https://courses.lumenlearning.com/suny-astronomy/chapter/colors-of-stars/, https://www.universetoday.com/articles/temperature-of-stars
- **Previous wording:** A star's color shows its surface temperature: blue-white stars are the hottest and red stars the coolest.

  - L9 “The Life of a Star” · `card.astronomy.009.c3` (timeline): The Sun's life, in four stops: ~4.6 billion years ago: Born from a collapsing cloud of gas and dust; Now: Middle-aged, fusing hydrogen into helium in its core; ~5 billion years from now: Runs low on hydrogen in its core and swells into a red giant; After that: Sheds its outer layers and ends as a white dwarf
  - L93 “The First Stars and Galaxies” · `card.astronomy.093.c2` (text): The cosmic dark ages / Picture a universe with no stars at all: just cooling gas, mostly hydrogen and helium, drifting in the dark. Astronomers call this long stretch the cosmic dark ages. Gravity was busy, though, pulling the gas into collapsing clumps. Within a few hundred million years after the Big Bang, the first stars lit up. The longest blackout in history, ended by gravity.
- **Tested by:**
  - L9 `question.astronomy.009.q2`: How will the Sun most likely end its life? → **As a red giant, then a white dwarf**
  - L9 `question.astronomy.009.q3`: From Level 2: when the Sun swells into a red giant, what is it running low on? → **Hydrogen to fuse in its core**
- **Fact-check (2026-09-24):** corroborated. NASA: stars are born from clouds of gas and dust that collapse under their own gravity. Sources: https://science.nasa.gov/universe/stars/, https://www.cfa.harvard.edu/research/topic/star-formation

### ☐ `fact.astronomy.sun_color`: unverified

> **Claim:** The Sun is intermediate in temperature, so it gives off a fairly even mix of colors and looks white from space. From the ground it looks yellowish because our atmosphere scatters away some of its blue light.

- **Concept:** A star's color is its temperature (`concept.astronomy.star_color_temperature`)
- **Appears in:**
  - L9 “The Life of a Star” · `card.astronomy.009.c2` (text): Color is temperature / Look closely and stars have colors, and each color is a thermometer reading. Blue stars are the hottest; red stars are the coolest. That's backwards from your kitchen tap, where red means hot. The Sun sits in the middle: it shines white from space, and our air scatters away some of its blue light, so it looks yellowish from the ground. / Blue = hot · Red = cool
- **Tested by:**
  - L9 `question.astronomy.009.q1`: A star looks blue-white. What does that tell you? → **It's hotter than the Sun**
  - L63 `question.astronomy.063.q3`: From Level 9: a blue-white star is hotter than a red one. On the H–R diagram, which side is it on? → **The left side, with hotter stars**
- **Fact-check (2026-09-24):** corrected. The Sun's ~5,800 K surface emits a broad mix of colors and looks white from space; it appears yellowish from Earth because the atmosphere scatters away some blue light. Temperature alone does not make it yellow. Sources: https://www.skyatnightmagazine.com/space-science/what-colour-sun, https://www.newsweek.com/color-sun-yellow-white-spectrum-1742550
- **Previous wording:** The Sun is intermediate in temperature, which is why it appears white-yellow.

```sh
npm run verify:record -- fact.astronomy.sun_color source.nasa_star_life_cycle --status verified --by "Your Name" --quote "…"
```

### ☐ `fact.astronomy.sun_formed_from_cloud`: unverified

> **Claim:** The Sun formed about 4.6 billion years ago from a collapsing cloud of gas and dust.

- **Concept:** Stars are born, age and die (`concept.astronomy.star_life_cycle`)
- **Appears in:**
  - L9 “The Life of a Star” · `card.astronomy.009.c3` (timeline): The Sun's life, in four stops: ~4.6 billion years ago: Born from a collapsing cloud of gas and dust; Now: Middle-aged, fusing hydrogen into helium in its core; ~5 billion years from now: Runs low on hydrogen in its core and swells into a red giant; After that: Sheds its outer layers and ends as a white dwarf
  - L59 “How the Solar System Formed” · `card.astronomy.059.c2` (text): A spinning cloud collapses / About 4.6 billion years ago, a cloud of gas and dust began collapsing under its own gravity. As it shrank, it spun faster and flattened into a disk, like pizza dough spun in the air. The young Sun formed at the center. That's why the planets still orbit in nearly the same plane, and why they all stay close to the ecliptic from Level 15. The solar system still has the shape of that one flat, spinning disk. / Cloud → spinning disk → Sun + planets
- **Tested by:**

- **Fact-check (2026-09-24):** corroborated. The Sun and planets formed about 4.6 billion years ago from a collapsing cloud of gas and dust, the solar nebula. Sources: https://www.amnh.org/exhibitions/permanent/the-universe/planets/formation-of-our-solar-system, https://theconversation.com/the-sun-was-born-when-a-dense-gas-cloud-collapsed-4-6-billion-years-ago-226862

### ☐ `fact.astronomy.sun_future_red_giant`: unverified

> **Claim:** In about 5 billion years the Sun will swell into a red giant, then shed its outer layers and end as a white dwarf.

- **Concept:** Stars are born, age and die (`concept.astronomy.star_life_cycle`)
- **Appears in:**
  - L9 “The Life of a Star” · `card.astronomy.009.c3` (timeline): The Sun's life, in four stops: ~4.6 billion years ago: Born from a collapsing cloud of gas and dust; Now: Middle-aged, fusing hydrogen into helium in its core; ~5 billion years from now: Runs low on hydrogen in its core and swells into a red giant; After that: Sheds its outer layers and ends as a white dwarf
  - L98 “Integration: Futures of the Sun, Earth and Universe” · `card.astronomy.098.c2` (timeline): The long forecast: Over ~26,000 years: Earth’s axis wobbles through a full circle, so Polaris won’t stay the pole star; In ~1 billion years: A slowly brightening Sun makes Earth too hot for oceans; In ~4.5 billion years, maybe: The Milky Way and Andromeda may merge; a 2025 study put it at about a coin flip; In ~5 billion years: The Sun swells into a red giant, later becoming a white dwarf

Red giants sit in the upper right: cool but very luminous. White dwarfs sit in the lower left: hot but dim. The Sun will visit both corners one day, red giant first, white dwarf last.
  - L67 “Red Giants and Planetary Nebulae” · `card.astronomy.067.c1` (text): Someday the Sun will swallow Mercury and Venus. / Level 63 said the Sun will one day move to the red giant corner of the H–R diagram. Here’s what that trip looks like, and the glowing mess it leaves behind.
  - L67 “Red Giants and Planetary Nebulae” · `card.astronomy.067.c2` (text): Swelling up / When a Sun-like star’s core runs low on hydrogen, the core contracts and heats up while the outer layers swell enormously. The star becomes a red giant: cool but very luminous, so it moves up and to the right on the H–R diagram. In about 5 billion years the Sun should grow large enough to engulf Mercury and Venus, and possibly Earth. Inner-planet real estate: not a long-term investment. / Core shrinks · outer layers swell
  - L98 “Integration: Futures of the Sun, Earth and Universe” · `card.astronomy.098.c1` (text): Astronomy can forecast billions of years ahead. / Your weather app struggles with next Tuesday. Here’s the outlook for the next few billion years: the sky, the Sun, Earth and our galaxy.

### ☐ `fact.astronomy.sun_no_supernova`: unverified

> **Claim:** The Sun is not massive enough to explode as a supernova.

- **Concept:** Stars are born, age and die (`concept.astronomy.star_life_cycle`)
- **Appears in:**
  - L9 “The Life of a Star” · `card.astronomy.009.c4` (text): Bigger stars, bigger endings / Relax: the Sun won't explode. It isn't massive enough. Stars much more massive than the Sun get a far louder exit: a supernova, an explosion that can briefly outshine a whole galaxy. Picture one dying star, for a moment, brighter than every other star in its galaxy put together. The Sun's quiet ending sounds better already.
- **Pre-check:** Confirm the page says the Sun lacks the mass to go supernova (common NASA statement).
- **Fact-check (2026-09-24):** corroborated. Core-collapse supernovae need stars of at least about 8 solar masses; the Sun lacks the mass and will become a red giant and then a white dwarf. Sources: https://www.space.com/23756-white-dwarf-stars.html, https://www.discovermagazine.com/dont-worry-were-not-in-danger-of-the-sun-going-supernova-45761


## Sun: Facts (NASA Science)

- **Source:** `source.nasa_sun_facts`
- **URL:** https://science.nasa.gov/sun/facts/
- **License:** US_gov_public_domain · **Accessed:** 2026-09-23 · **Source record checked (URL/licence):** no

### ☐ `fact.astronomy.corona_hot`: unverified

> **Claim:** The Sun’s outer atmosphere, the corona, is over 1 million °C, far hotter than the surface below it.

- **Concept:** Inside the Sun (`concept.astronomy.sun_layers`)
- **Appears in:**
  - L56 “Inside the Sun” · `card.astronomy.056.c1` (text): The Sun's outer atmosphere is far hotter than the surface beneath it. / Imagine stepping back from a campfire and feeling hotter. That's the Sun. To see how strange it is, go through it layer by layer.
  - L56 “Inside the Sun” · `card.astronomy.056.c4` (fact): The Sun's surface is about 5,500 °C. Its corona tops 1 million °C. / Back to the campfire. The core is about 15 million °C, and moving out to the surface it cools, as you'd expect. Then the corona is more than 180 times hotter than the surface below it. Why? Nobody fully knows yet. You can see this puzzle yourself during a total solar eclipse (Level 7), when the Moon covers the Sun's bright disc and reveals the corona.
  - L99 “Integration: The Big Open Questions” · `card.astronomy.099.c3` (text): Unsolved puzzles / Not every mystery is dark. Measure how fast the universe expands today in different ways and the answers disagree slightly, a puzzle called the Hubble tension. The Sun’s corona is far hotter than the surface below it, like air above a campfire being hotter than the flames, and nobody fully knows why. And what, if anything, came before the Big Bang? The theory doesn’t say.
- **Tested by:**
  - L56 `question.astronomy.056.q2`: What is surprising about the Sun’s corona? → **It is far hotter than the surface beneath it**
  - L56 `question.astronomy.056.q3`: From Level 7: when can we most easily see the Sun’s corona from Earth? → **During a total solar eclipse**
  - L60 `question.astronomy.060.q2`: Which layer of the Sun is over a million degrees Celsius? → **The corona**
- **Fact-check (2026-09-24):** corroborated. The photosphere is about 5,500 to 6,000 C while the corona reaches about 1 to 2 million C or more, hundreds of times hotter. Sources: https://scied.ucar.edu/learning-zone/sun-space-weather/corona, https://www.space.com/17137-how-hot-is-the-sun.html

```sh
npm run verify:record -- fact.astronomy.corona_hot source.nasa_sun_facts --status verified --by "Your Name" --quote "…"
```

- **Concept:** Inside the Sun (`concept.astronomy.sun_layers`)
- **Appears in:**
  - L56 “Inside the Sun” · `card.astronomy.056.c4` (fact): The Sun's surface is about 5,500 °C. Its corona tops 1 million °C. / Back to the campfire. You can see this puzzle yourself during a total solar eclipse (Level 7), when the Moon covers the Sun's bright disc and reveals the corona.
  - L99 “Integration: The Big Open Questions” · `card.astronomy.099.c3` (text): Unsolved puzzles / Not every mystery is dark. Measure how fast the universe expands today in different ways and the answers disagree slightly, a puzzle called the Hubble tension. ### ☐ `fact.astronomy.energy_slow_escape`: unverified

> **Claim:** Energy made in the Sun’s core can take many thousands of years to work its way out to the surface.

- **Concept:** Inside the Sun (`concept.astronomy.sun_layers`)
- **Appears in:**
  - L56 “Inside the Sun” · `card.astronomy.056.c3` (text): A slow journey out / Energy made in the core can take many thousands of years to work its way out to the surface. Then, once it escapes, it crosses all the way to Earth in about 8 minutes. So the sunshine on your face spent ages stuck in traffic inside the Sun, then took the express lane to you.
- **Pre-check:** NASA figures vary (tens of thousands to ~170,000+ years); the card avoids a number.
- **Fact-check (2026-09-24):** corroborated. Estimates for energy to diffuse from the core through the radiative zone range from about 100,000 to 170,000 years (NASA cites ~170,000), consistent with 'many thousands of years'. Sources: https://solarsystem.nasa.gov/genesismission/gm2/science/sunlight_solar-heat/solar_structure.htm, https://scied.ucar.edu/learning-zone/sun-space-weather/inside-sun

```sh

```

  - L56 “Inside the Sun” · `card.astronomy.056.c1` (text): The Sun's outer atmosphere is far hotter than the surface beneath it. / Imagine stepping back from a campfire and feeling hotter. That's the Sun. To see how strange it is, go through it layer by layer.

You can see this puzzle yourself during a total solar eclipse (Level 7), when the Moon covers the Sun's bright disc and reveals the corona.
  - L57 “Sunspots and the Solar Cycle” · `card.astronomy.057.c2` (text): Dark, but not cold / Sunspots are patches of the Sun's surface where intense magnetic fields make them cooler than their surroundings. Cooler means darker, so they show up as dark blotches. But cool is relative: the surface around them is about 5,500 °C (Level 56). A sunspot only looks dark because it sits next to something brighter, like a candle held up in front of a floodlight. / Cooler → darker

A star on it is fusing hydrogen into helium in its core, the same job the Sun is doing right now. Stars spend most of their lives this way, which is why most of them land on that band: it’s where they spend their working years. The Sun gets about 10 billion years there in total. / Sun: ~10 billion years
  - L2 “The Sun, Up Close” · `card.astronomy.002.c4` (text): Middle-aged, not old / 4.6 billion years sounds ancient, until you learn that a star like ours can keep fusing hydrogen for about 10 billion years.   - L70 “Checkpoint: The Lives of Stars” · `card.astronomy.070.c3` (timeline): A Sun-like star’s life, start to finish: Birth: Collapses from a cold, dense cloud of gas and dust; Main sequence: Fuses hydrogen into helium for billions of years; Red giant: Core runs low on hydrogen; outer layers swell; The end: Sheds a planetary nebula; a white dwarf remains and slowly cools

At that heat, hydrogen atoms slam together so hard that they fuse into helium, and every crash releases a tiny spark of energy. One spark is nothing. But the core does this an almost unimaginable number of times every second, and all those sparks add up to every bit of sunshine you've ever felt. / Hydrogen → helium + energy
  - L56 “Inside the Sun” · `card.astronomy.056.c4` (fact): The Sun's surface is about 5,500 °C. Its corona tops 1 million °C. / Back to the campfire. The core is about 15 million °C, and moving out to the surface it cools, as you'd expect. Then the corona is more than 180 times hotter than the surface below it. Why? Nobody fully knows yet. You can see this puzzle yourself during a total solar eclipse (Level 7), when the Moon covers the Sun's bright disc and reveals the corona.
- **Tested by:**
  - L2 `question.astronomy.002.q1`: What powers the Sun? → **Fusing hydrogen into helium**
  - L2 `question.astronomy.002.q3`: Why is it misleading to say the Sun is "on fire"? → **Its energy comes from nuclear fusion, not burning**
  - L9 `question.astronomy.009.q3`: From Level 2: when the Sun swells into a red giant, what is it running low on? → **Hydrogen to fuse in its core**
  - L32 `question.astronomy.032.q3`: From Level 2: why does it make sense that stars are mostly hydrogen and helium? → **Stars fuse hydrogen into helium in their cores**
- **Fact-check (2026-09-24):** corroborated. The Sun's core temperature is about 15 million C (27 million F). Sources: https://coolcosmos.ipac.caltech.edu/ask/7-how-hot-is-the-sun-, https://www.space.com/17137-how-hot-is-the-sun.html

### ☐ `fact.astronomy.sun_fusion_hydrogen_helium`: unverified

> **Claim:** The Sun generates energy by fusing hydrogen into helium in its core.

- **Concept:** The Sun runs on nuclear fusion (`concept.astronomy.sun_fusion`)
- **Appears in:**
  - L2 “The Sun, Up Close” · `card.astronomy.002.c2` (text): A star that squeezes atoms / Deep in the Sun's core it's about 15 million °C. At that heat, hydrogen atoms slam together so hard that they fuse into helium, and every crash releases a tiny spark of energy. One spark is nothing. But the core does this an almost unimaginable number of times every second, and all those sparks add up to every bit of sunshine you've ever felt. / Hydrogen → helium + energy
  - L96 “Integration: From Atoms to Galaxies” · `card.astronomy.096.c3` (text): The great cycle / Think of the galaxy as a kitchen that never throws out leftovers. Stars fuse light elements into heavier ones, just as the Sun turns hydrogen into helium. Massive stars end in supernovae that blast those elements out, scattering them into space. New stars and planets form from that enriched gas, and the cycle repeats. Every generation of stars cooks with what the last one left behind. / Gas → stars → elements → new stars
  - L9 “The Life of a Star” · `card.astronomy.009.c3` (timeline): The Sun's life, in four stops: ~4.6 billion years ago: Born from a collapsing cloud of gas and dust; Now: Middle-aged, fusing hydrogen into helium in its core; ~5 billion years from now: Runs low on hydrogen in its core and swells into a red giant; After that: Sheds its outer layers and ends as a white dwarf
  - L32 “Starlight’s Fingerprints” · `card.astronomy.032.c3` (text): Reading a star / Astronomers match the lines in a star's spectrum to each element's known pattern, and out comes the ingredients list. The answer, for star after star: mostly hydrogen and helium. That shouldn't surprise you. Remember Level 2? The Sun's core fuses hydrogen into helium. The ingredients list and the recipe match.
  - L56 “Inside the Sun” · `card.astronomy.056.c2` (text): Layer by layer / Peel the Sun like an onion, from the inside out. First the core, where fusion happens. Then the radiative zone and the convective zone. Next comes the photosphere, the visible surface: the part you actually see when you look at the Sun (please don't). Then the chromosphere, and finally the outermost layer, the corona. / Core → radiative → convective → photosphere → chromosphere → corona
  - L56 “Inside the Sun” · `card.astronomy.056.c3` (text): A slow journey out / Energy made in the core can take many thousands of years to work its way out to the surface. Then, once it escapes, it crosses all the way to Earth in about 8 minutes. So the sunshine on your face spent ages stuck in traffic inside the Sun, then took the express lane to you.
- **Tested by:**
  - L2 `question.astronomy.002.q1`: What powers the Sun? → **Fusing hydrogen into helium**
  - L2 `question.astronomy.002.q3`: Why is it misleading to say the Sun is "on fire"? → **Its energy comes from nuclear fusion, not burning**
  - L9 `question.astronomy.009.q3`: From Level 2: when the Sun swells into a red giant, what is it running low on? → **Hydrogen to fuse in its core**
  - L32 `question.astronomy.032.q3`: From Level 2: why does it make sense that stars are mostly hydrogen and helium? → **Stars fuse hydrogen into helium in their cores**
- **Pre-check:** Card 002.c2 says 'trillions of them every second'. The real rate is ~10^38 fusion reactions per second, so 'trillions' is a severe understatement. Suggest rewording to 'an enormous number every second' in a new revision.
- **Fact-check (2026-09-24):** corroborated. The claim is accurate: the Sun fuses about 600 million tons of hydrogen into helium each second in its core. (Separately, the card's 'trillions' is far too low; the rate is ~10^38 reactions per second.) Sources: https://www.ipp.mpg.de/15047/kernfusion, https://solarsystem.nasa.gov/genesismission/gm2/science/sunlight_solar-heat/solar_structure.htm

### ☐ `fact.astronomy.sunlight_eight_minutes`: unverified

> **Claim:** Sunlight takes about 8 minutes to travel from the Sun to Earth.

- **Concept:** Sunlight takes about 8 minutes to reach Earth (`concept.astronomy.sunlight_travel_time`)
- **Appears in:**
  - L1 “Your Cosmic Address” · `card.astronomy.001.c3` (fact): The Sun you see is 8 minutes old. / Sunlight takes about 8 minutes to reach us, so the Sun in the sky is the Sun from 8 minutes ago. If it vanished right now, you'd keep seeing it for about 8 more minutes. Light is the fastest thing there is. The Sun is just that far away.
  - L50 “Milestone: The Solar System” · `card.astronomy.050.c2` (text): How big is our neighborhood? / Neptune orbits about 30 astronomical units from the Sun, about 4.5 billion km. That number is too big to feel, so use light instead. Sunlight reaches Earth in about 8 minutes. To reach Neptune, it needs about 4 hours. Leave the Sun with the light at breakfast, and you'd arrive at Neptune around lunch. / 1 AU → 8 minutes · 30 AU → ~4 hours
  - L56 “Inside the Sun” · `card.astronomy.056.c3` (text): A slow journey out / Energy made in the core can take many thousands of years to work its way out to the surface. Then, once it escapes, it crosses all the way to Earth in about 8 minutes. So the sunshine on your face spent ages stuck in traffic inside the Sun, then took the express lane to you.
  - L8 “Light-Years: Measuring the Unmeasurable” · `card.astronomy.008.c3` (text): Why measure space in light? / Light is the fastest thing there is, which makes it a handy measuring tape. It crosses from the Moon to Earth in just over a second. From the Sun, it takes about 8 minutes (Level 1's slightly late Sun). So the Moon is about one light-second away and the Sun about 8 light-minutes. Past the Sun, even light needs years, and the ruler grows to match.
  - L8 “Light-Years: Measuring the Unmeasurable” · `card.astronomy.008.c4` (fact): The closest star after the Sun is Proxima Centauri, about 4.2 light-years away. / So its light takes about 4.2 years to reach us. Moon: a second. Sun: 8 minutes. Next star: more than four years. And that's the closest one; every other star is even farther. Proxima is our next-door neighbor. The street is just very, very long.
  - L28 “Measuring the Solar System” · `card.astronomy.028.c4` (fact): 1 astronomical unit (AU) is about 150 million km. / That's roughly the average distance between Earth and the Sun, and it's the solar system's standard yardstick. Sunlight crosses it in about 8 minutes (Level 1's slightly late Sun). Mars sits at about 1.5 AU. The map finally has its scale bar.
  - L38 “Robot Explorers” · `card.astronomy.038.c4` (fact): Voyager 1’s radio signals take more than 20 hours to reach Earth. / Radio waves travel at the speed of light, so every message from Voyager 1 is almost a day old when it lands. Remember the 8-minute-old Sun from Level 1? Voyager makes that look like live TV.
- **Tested by:**
  - L8 `question.astronomy.008.q2`: From Level 1: about how long does sunlight take to reach Earth? → **About 8 minutes**
  - L28 `question.astronomy.028.q3`: From Level 1: sunlight takes about 8 minutes to reach Earth. What distance does that light cover? → **About 150 million km (1 AU)**
  - L50 `question.astronomy.050.q5`: Neptune is about 30 AU from the Sun. About how long does sunlight take to reach it? → **About 4 hours**
- **Fact-check (2026-09-24):** corroborated. Sunlight takes about 8 minutes 20 seconds on average to reach Earth (about 8 min 10 s to 8 min 27 s over the year). Sources: https://phys.org/news/2013-04-sunlight-earth.html, https://www.skyatnightmagazine.com/space-science/how-take-light-from-sun-reach-earth

> **Claim:** Titan has lakes and seas of liquid methane and ethane, and it rains methane.

It's also the only moon with a thick atmosphere, made mostly of nitrogen, just like the air you breathe. Its clouds rain methane, which gathers in lakes and seas of liquid methane and ethane. - **Source:** `source.nasa_transit_venus`

- **License:** US_gov_public_domain · **Accessed:** 2026-09-24 · **Source record checked (URL/licence):** no

### ☐ `fact.astronomy.venus_transits_au`: unverified

> **Claim:** Astronomers observed the transits of Venus across the Sun in 1761 and 1769 from around the world to measure the Earth–Sun distance.

- **Concept:** The astronomical unit (`concept.astronomy.astronomical_unit`)
- **Appears in:**
  - L28 “Measuring the Solar System” · `card.astronomy.028.c3` (text): Chasing Venus / The distance to pick was Earth to Sun, and the tool was Venus. In 1761 and 1769, astronomers spread out around the world to watch Venus cross the face of the Sun. Comparing their observations let them work out the Earth–Sun distance. Today radar and spacecraft pin it down very precisely, no round-the-world expedition required.
- **Fact-check (2026-09-24):** corroborated. International expeditions (e.g. Cook to Tahiti, Chappe to Tobolsk) observed the 1761 and 1769 transits following Halley's method to determine the astronomical unit. Sources: https://skyandtelescope.org/astronomy-news/transits-of-venus-in-history-1761/, https://www.astronomy.ohio-state.edu/pogge.1/Ast161/Unit4/venussun.html

- **Source:** `source.nasa_trappist1`

- **License:** US_gov_public_domain · **Accessed:** 2026-09-24 · **Source record checked (URL/licence):** no

### ☐ `fact.astronomy.trappist1`: unverified

> **Claim:** TRAPPIST-1, a red dwarf about 40 light-years away, has seven Earth-sized planets, several of them in its habitable zone.

- **Concept:** The habitable zone (`concept.astronomy.habitable_zone`)
- **Appears in:**
  - L78 “The Habitable Zone” · `card.astronomy.078.c4` (fact): TRAPPIST-1 has seven Earth-sized planets. / This red dwarf, about 40 light-years away, is one of the small, cool stars whose habitable zone hugs it closely, and several of its planets sit inside that zone. One dim star, seven Earth-sized worlds, and more than one pulled up close to the campfire.
  - L80 “Checkpoint: Extreme Objects and Other Worlds” · `card.astronomy.080.c3` (text): Listening and looking / Each discovery in this chapter came from reading a different signal. Pulsars give away neutron stars with radio beams that sweep past Earth like a lighthouse. Gravitational waves, first caught by LIGO in 2015, revealed two black holes merging. And tiny dips and wobbles in starlight have uncovered thousands of worlds around other stars, some, like TRAPPIST-1’s, in their habitable zones. Dead stars and new worlds, found the same way: by paying very close attention.
  - L79 “The Search for Life” · `card.astronomy.079.c3` (text): Reading alien air / Around other stars, scientists read the air instead. They look for biosignatures: gases, such as oxygen and methane together, that living things could produce. The James Webb Space Telescope can analyze starlight passing through some exoplanets’ atmospheres to detect the gases they contain. It’s a sniff test done with light, from light-years away.
- **Fact-check (2026-09-24):** corroborated. TRAPPIST-1 is a red dwarf about 40 light-years away with seven roughly Earth-size planets; three (e, f, g) lie in its habitable zone. Sources: https://www.spitzer.caltech.edu/explore/trappist-1, https://science.nasa.gov/exoplanets/trappist1

### ☐ `fact.astronomy.herschel_1781`: unverified

> **Claim:** William Herschel discovered Uranus in 1781, the first planet discovered using a telescope.

- **Concept:** The discovery of Uranus (`concept.astronomy.uranus_discovery`)
- **Appears in:**
  - L29 “New Planets: Uranus and Neptune” · `card.astronomy.029.c1` (text): One planet was found with a telescope. The next was found with a pencil. / This chapter handed astronomers two new tools: the telescope and Newton's gravity. Each one bagged a planet.
  - L29 “New Planets: Uranus and Neptune” · `card.astronomy.029.c2` (text): A planet hiding as a star / In 1781, William Herschel discovered a new planet: Uranus. It was the first planet discovered with a telescope, which means every planet before it had been found without one. The funny part: astronomers had already recorded Uranus several times before 1781. They had just written it down as a star. It was hiding in plain sight, in their own notes.
  - L38 “Robot Explorers” · `card.astronomy.038.c2` (text): The only visitor / Voyager 1 and Voyager 2 launched in 1977. Voyager 2 went where no spacecraft has gone before or since: it's still the only one to have visited Uranus, in 1986, and Neptune, in 1989. Remember Level 29? Herschel found Uranus as a faint dot in 1781. About two centuries later, Voyager 2 flew right past it. Every close-up of those two worlds comes from that single trip. / Launched 1977
- **Tested by:**
  - L29 `question.astronomy.029.q2`: What was special about Herschel’s discovery of Uranus in 1781? → **It was the first planet discovered with a telescope**
  - L38 `question.astronomy.038.q3`: From Level 29: Herschel found Uranus with a telescope in 1781. How did Voyager 2 add to that? → **It gave humanity its only close-up visit to Uranus**
- **Fact-check (2026-09-24):** corroborated. Herschel found Uranus on 13 March 1781 with his own telescope; Science Museum and RMG describe it as the first planet discovered with a telescope. Sources: https://www.sciencemuseum.org.uk/objects-and-stories/uranus-first-planet-discovered-telescope, https://www.rmg.co.uk/stories/space-astronomy/astronomy/discovery-uranus

### ☐ `fact.astronomy.methane_blue`: unverified

> **Claim:** Methane in the atmospheres of Uranus and Neptune absorbs red light, giving them their blue color.

Take the red out of sunlight, and what bounces back to your eye looks blue. Two very different oddballs, painted with the same trick.
- **Tested by:**
  - L49 `question.astronomy.049.q2`: What gives Uranus and Neptune their blue color? → **Methane in their atmospheres absorbing red light**
- **Fact-check (2026-09-24):** corroborated. Methane in Uranus's and Neptune's atmospheres absorbs red light and reflects blue, giving both their blue color; haze explains their shade difference. Sources: https://science.nasa.gov/solar-system/why-uranus-and-neptune-are-different-colors/

### ☐ `fact.astronomy.uranus_seasons`: unverified

> **Claim:** Because of its tilt, each of Uranus’s poles gets about 42 years of continuous sunlight followed by 42 years of darkness.

- **Concept:** Uranus and Neptune up close (`concept.astronomy.ice_giant_extremes`)
- **Appears in:**
  - L49 “The Tilted and the Windy: Uranus and Neptune” · `card.astronomy.049.c1` (text): On Uranus, a single day at the pole can last 42 years. / Uranus and Neptune, the two ice giants, are the oddballs of the family. One rolls around the Sun on its side. The other has the fastest winds in the solar system.
  - L49 “The Tilted and the Windy: Uranus and Neptune” · `card.astronomy.049.c2` (text): A planet on its side / Back in Level 5, Earth's lean of about 23.5° was enough to give you summer and winter. Uranus is tilted about 98°, so it essentially rolls around the Sun on its side. That makes for extreme seasons: each pole gets about 42 years of continuous sunlight, followed by 42 years of darkness. Be born at the start of a polar summer, and you'd be middle-aged by sunset. / Tilt: ~98°
- **Tested by:**

- **Fact-check (2026-09-24):** corroborated. Uranus's 97 degree tilt and 84-year orbit give each pole about 42 years of sunlight followed by 42 years of darkness (NASA Webb, SSEC). Sources: https://x.com/NASAWebb/status/1643979296590168070?lang=en, https://www.ssec.wisc.edu/planetary/uranus/science/

Venus spins backwards compared with most planets, so there the Sun rises in the west. It's also in no hurry. One rotation takes 243 Earth days, but one trip around the Sun takes only 225. It turns so slowly that it finishes an orbit before it finishes a single spin. That's how a day ends up longer than a year. / Day: 243 Earth days · Year: 225
- **Tested by:**
  - L42 `question.astronomy.042.q2`: Why is a day on Venus longer than its year? → **It rotates so slowly that one spin takes longer than one orbit**

- **Fact-check (2026-09-24):** corroborated. Venus has retrograde rotation, opposite to most planets, so the Sun rises in the west and sets in the east. Sources: https://starchild.gsfc.nasa.gov/docs/StarChild/solar_system_level2/venus_answer.html, https://www.scientificamerican.com/article/why-venus-spins-the-wrong/

Its big trick: it passes through clouds of dust that block visible light, revealing stars forming inside them. Think of a nursery with the curtains drawn, and infrared lets you peek in. There's a second prize. Light from the most distant galaxies has been stretched by the expanding universe into infrared. To see that far away, you need a pair of infrared eyes.

  - L93 “The First Stars and Galaxies” · `card.astronomy.093.c4` (text): Finding the first galaxies / How do you photograph the dawn? Light from these early galaxies has crossed so much expanding space that it has been stretched into infrared. So to find them, you hunt in infrared. Webb has already seen galaxies as they were only a few hundred million years after the Big Bang. Those are baby pictures of the universe, taken in a color your eyes can’t see. / Early light → infrared → Webb

  - L36 `question.astronomy.036.q2`: Why is infrared ideal for watching stars being born? → **Infrared passes through the dust clouds where stars form**

### ☐ `fact.astronomy.infrared_dust`: unverified

> **Claim:** Infrared light passes through clouds of dust that block visible light, revealing stars forming inside them.

- **Concept:** Infrared astronomy (`concept.astronomy.infrared_astronomy`)
- **Appears in:**
  - L36 “Seeing Heat: Infrared and Webb” · `card.astronomy.036.c2` (text): Seeing through dust / Infrared is the light you feel as heat. Its big trick: it passes through clouds of dust that block visible light, revealing stars forming inside them. Think of a nursery with the curtains drawn, and infrared lets you peek in. There's a second prize. Light from the most distant galaxies has been stretched by the expanding universe into infrared. To see that far away, you need a pair of infrared eyes.
  - L40 “Checkpoint: The Tools of Astronomy” · `card.astronomy.040.c3` (comparison): Match the light to the job: Radio (Passes through dust and clouds; Huge dishes, linked into arrays) vs Infrared (Star birth inside dust; The most distant galaxies) vs X-ray (Extremely hot gas; Only from space)
  - L66 “Stellar Nurseries” · `card.astronomy.066.c2` (text): From cloud to star / Stars form inside cold, dense clouds of gas and dust. Gravity pulls clumps of the cloud together, and as a clump shrinks, its center gets hotter. Eventually the core is hot enough for fusion to begin, and the clump becomes a star. The dust has a catch: it hides the action. That’s why the infrared light from Level 36 is so handy. It passes through the dust and lets us peek inside. / Cloud → clump → fusion ignites

- **Fact-check (2026-09-24):** corroborated. NASA: infrared's longer wavelengths pass through dust more easily than visible light, letting Webb see protostars inside dusty clouds. Sources: https://science.nasa.gov/mission/webb/science-overview/science-explainers/infrared-astronomy/, https://science.nasa.gov/mission/webb/science-overview/science-explainers/webbs-star-formation-discoveries/

The James Webb Space Telescope can analyze starlight passing through some exoplanets’ atmospheres to detect the gases they contain. It’s a sniff test done with light, from light-years away.
  - L99 “Integration: The Big Open Questions” · `card.astronomy.099.c4` (fact): Is anyone out there? / No life has yet been confirmed anywhere beyond Earth. ### ☐ `fact.astronomy.dark_ages`: unverified

> **Claim:** After the CMB was released, the universe went through the “cosmic dark ages,” a long stretch before the first stars formed.

- **Concept:** The first stars and galaxies (`concept.astronomy.first_stars`)
- **Appears in:**
  - L93 “The First Stars and Galaxies” · `card.astronomy.093.c1` (text): After its first light, the universe went dark for a long time. / When the CMB broke free, there were no stars yet to shine. So who turned the lights on?
  - L93 “The First Stars and Galaxies” · `card.astronomy.093.c2` (text): The cosmic dark ages / Picture a universe with no stars at all: just cooling gas, mostly hydrogen and helium, drifting in the dark. Astronomers call this long stretch the cosmic dark ages. Gravity was busy, though, pulling the gas into collapsing clumps. Within a few hundred million years after the Big Bang, the first stars lit up. The longest blackout in history, ended by gravity.
- **Fact-check (2026-09-24):** corroborated. NASA: the cosmic dark ages followed the universe becoming transparent (about 380,000 years) and lasted until the first stars formed, some 100 to 200 million years later. Sources: https://science.nasa.gov/mission/webb/early-universe/, https://jwst.nasa.gov/content/science/firstLight.html

### ☐ `fact.astronomy.first_stars_hh`: unverified

> **Claim:** The first stars formed from only hydrogen and helium and are thought to have been very massive and short-lived.

  - L93 “The First Stars and Galaxies” · `card.astronomy.093.c3` (text): Giants of two elements / The first stars had a short ingredient list: only hydrogen and helium, since that’s nearly all the Big Bang had made. They’re thought to have been very massive and short-lived, the rock stars of the early universe. When they died, they made the first heavier elements and scattered them into space, seeding the gas that later stars and planets formed from. Live fast, die young, leave behind the makings of planets.

- **Fact-check (2026-09-24):** corroborated. Population III stars are hypothesized to have formed almost entirely of hydrogen and helium and to have been very massive and short-lived. Sources: https://www.britannica.com/topic/Population-III, https://arxiv.org/pdf/2302.09763

## Tests of Big Bang: The CMB (NASA Goddard Space Flight Center (WMAP))

- **Source:** `source.nasa_wmap_cmb`
- **URL:** https://map.gsfc.nasa.gov/universe/bb_tests_cmb.html

### ☐ `fact.astronomy.cmb_380k`: unverified

> **Claim:** The CMB was released about 380,000 years after the Big Bang, when the universe had cooled enough for atoms to form; before then it was too hot and dense for light to travel far.

- **Concept:** The cosmic microwave background (`concept.astronomy.cmb`)
- **Appears in:**
  - L92 “The Afterglow: Cosmic Microwave Background” · `card.astronomy.092.c2` (text): When light broke free / For roughly its first 380,000 years, the universe was too hot and dense for light to travel far, like headlights in thick fog. Then it cooled enough for atoms to form, and light could finally travel freely. That released light is still here. We call it the cosmic microwave background, or CMB: leftover light from the early universe, filling the whole sky. Every direction you look holds a baby picture of the universe. / Released ~380,000 years after the Big Bang
- **Tested by:**
  - L92 `question.astronomy.092.q1`: What is the cosmic microwave background? → **Leftover light from the early universe**
- **Fact-check (2026-09-24):** corroborated. About 380,000 years after the Big Bang the universe cooled to about 3000 K, electrons and protons combined into atoms, and photons could travel freely. Sources: https://www.astro.ucla.edu/~wright/CMB.html, https://www.cfa.harvard.edu/research/topic/cosmic-microwave-background

Then it cooled enough for atoms to form, and light could finally travel freely. That released light is still here. We call it the cosmic microwave background, or CMB: leftover light from the early universe, filling the whole sky. - **Fact-check (2026-09-24):** corroborated. Alpher and Herman predicted relic radiation in 1948; Penzias and Wilson's 1965 detection was key evidence for the Big Bang over the steady state model. > **Claim:** In 1998 two teams studying distant supernovae found them fainter than expected, showing that the expansion of the universe is speeding up.

- **Concept:** Dark energy (`concept.astronomy.dark_energy`)
- **Appears in:**
  - L94 “Dark Energy” · `card.astronomy.094.c1` (text): The universe’s expansion is speeding up, and no one knows why. / Everyone expected the opposite. Then, in 1998, the universe turned out to have its foot on the gas.
  - L94 “Dark Energy” · `card.astronomy.094.c2` (text): Expecting a slowdown / Toss a ball upward and gravity slows it down. Gravity pulls everything together, so astronomers expected the expansion of the universe to be slowing the same way. In 1998 two teams checked, using distant supernovae. The supernovae were fainter than a slowing universe allowed. The expansion is speeding up. The ball wasn’t slowing down. It was taking off. / Expected: slowing · Found: speeding up
  - L98 “Integration: Futures of the Sun, Earth and Universe” · `card.astronomy.098.c3` (text): The universe’s fate / Now zoom all the way out. Level 94 showed dark energy speeding up the expansion. If it keeps doing so, galaxies beyond our local neighborhood will eventually drift out of view. Picture a party where the guests slowly back out the door until only your closest neighbors are left. The Sun’s own story ends quietly: a white dwarf that no longer fuses anything, shining with leftover heat and slowly cooling. No grand finale, just a very long fade.

- **Fact-check (2026-09-24):** corroborated. In 1998 two teams studying distant Type Ia supernovae found them fainter than expected, showing the expansion is accelerating. ### ☐ `fact.astronomy.nobel_2020_sgr`: unverified

> **Claim:** Reinhard Genzel and Andrea Ghez shared half of the 2020 Nobel Prize in Physics (Roger Penrose received the other half) for discovering the supermassive object at our galaxy's center.

- **Concept:** The black hole at the center of the Milky Way (`concept.astronomy.sagittarius_a`)
- **Appears in:**
  - L75 “The Black Hole at Our Center” · `card.astronomy.075.c3` (text): A prize and a picture / Watching those stars paid off. Reinhard Genzel and Andrea Ghez shared half of the 2020 Nobel Prize in Physics for discovering the supermassive object at our galaxy’s center. Then, in 2022, the Event Horizon Telescope from Level 74 released an image of Sagittarius A* itself. First we knew it by the company it keeps. Then we got its portrait. / Nobel 2020 · image 2022
  - L75 “The Black Hole at Our Center” · `card.astronomy.075.c2` (text): Following the stars / About 27,000 light-years away, at the center of the Milky Way, stars race around something nobody can see. Picture a racetrack where you can see the cars but not what they’re circling. Astronomers tracked those orbits, and the laws of orbits and gravity from Kepler and Newton turned them into a mass: about 4 million times the Sun’s. One star, S2, laps it about every 16 years. Its name is Sagittarius A*, and it’s a supermassive black hole. / ~4 million Suns
- **Tested by:**
  - L75 `question.astronomy.075.q1`: About how massive is Sagittarius A*, the Milky Way’s central black hole? → **About 4 million times the Sun’s mass**
  - L75 `question.astronomy.075.q2`: How did astronomers first show that a supermassive black hole sits at our galaxy’s center? → **They tracked stars orbiting an invisible massive object**
  - L75 `question.astronomy.075.q3`: From Level 26: which laws let astronomers turn those stars’ orbits into a mass for Sagittarius A*? → **The laws of orbits and gravity from Kepler and Newton**
  - L82 `question.astronomy.082.q3`: From Level 75: what sits at the very center of the Milky Way’s bulge? → **A supermassive black hole, Sagittarius A***

- **Fact-check (2026-09-24):** corrected. NobelPrize.org: the 2020 prize was divided, one half to Roger Penrose and the other half jointly to Genzel and Ghez for the supermassive compact object at the galaxy's centre. The claim implies they shared the whole prize. Sources: https://www.nobelprize.org/prizes/physics/2020/summary/, https://www.nobelprize.org/prizes/physics/2020/press-release/

### ☐ `fact.astronomy.radio_arrays`: unverified

> **Claim:** Radio telescopes can be linked into arrays that work together like one much larger telescope.

- **Concept:** Radio astronomy (`concept.astronomy.radio_astronomy`)
- **Appears in:**
  - L35 “Listening to the Sky: Radio Astronomy” · `card.astronomy.035.c3` (text): Why the dishes are huge / Remember the light bucket from Level 33? Radio waves are very long, so seeing fine detail takes an enormous dish. And when one dish isn't enough, astronomers link many dishes into an array that works together like one much larger telescope. Can't build a bigger bucket? Build a team of buckets. / Longer waves → bigger dishes
  - L35 “Listening to the Sky: Radio Astronomy” · `card.astronomy.035.c4` (fact): The Very Large Array in New Mexico links 27 radio dishes. / Spread out over up to 36 km, they work together like a single dish of that size. Twenty-seven dishes, one enormous eye on the radio sky. The name isn't modest, but it is accurate.
  - L40 “Checkpoint: The Tools of Astronomy” · `card.astronomy.040.c3` (comparison): Match the light to the job: Radio (Passes through dust and clouds; Huge dishes, linked into arrays) vs Infrared (Star birth inside dust; The most distant galaxies) vs X-ray (Extremely hot gas; Only from space)
  - L74 “The Event Horizon” · `card.astronomy.074.c3` (text): An Earth-sized telescope / Level 35 taught two radio tricks: a bigger dish sees finer detail, and dishes linked into an array work like one much larger telescope. The Event Horizon Telescope pushes both to the limit. It links radio telescopes around the world so they work like one Earth-sized telescope. In 2019 it released the first image of a black hole, the one at the center of the galaxy M87. You can’t build a dish the size of a planet, so astronomers borrowed the planet.
- **Tested by:**
  - L35 `question.astronomy.035.q2`: Why are radio telescopes usually so much bigger than optical ones? → **Radio waves are very long, so fine detail needs a huge dish**
  - L35 `question.astronomy.035.q3`: From Level 33: a bigger aperture shows finer detail. How do radio astronomers get an aperture bigger than any single dish? → **By linking many dishes into one array**
  - L74 `question.astronomy.074.q3`: From Level 35: the Event Horizon Telescope is a supersized version of which radio-astronomy technique? → **Linking dishes into an array**
- **Fact-check (2026-09-24):** corroborated. NRAO: separated radio dishes combine signals via interferometry to act as one much larger telescope, as at the VLA. Sources: https://public.nrao.edu/telescopes/radio-telescopes/, https://public.nrao.edu/telescopes/vla/

  - L74 “The Event Horizon” · `card.astronomy.074.c3` (text): An Earth-sized telescope / Level 35 taught two radio tricks: a bigger dish sees finer detail, and dishes linked into an array work like one much larger telescope. The Event Horizon Telescope pushes both to the limit. It links radio telescopes around the world so they work like one Earth-sized telescope. - **Concept:** The search for life (`concept.astronomy.search_for_life`)
- **Appears in:**
  - L79 “The Search for Life” · `card.astronomy.079.c4` (fact): No life has yet been confirmed anywhere beyond Earth. / Not on Mars, not in the ocean moons, not in any alien air.
